Power Towers: Purely Inseparable Galois Theory and Foliations in Positive Characteristic
Przemysław Grabowski

Abstract
. We build a purely inseparable Galois theory using non-derived commutative algebra. Our theory works on fields and on normal varieties. It says that a purely inseparable morphism corresponds to a finite (saturated) subalgebra of differential operators. Our approach unifies most of the literature about purely inseparable morphisms and shows new research directions. Indeed, our theory extends to a theory that covers all (saturated) subalgebras of differential operators. The extended theory gives a correspondence between the subalgebras and a new notion of power towers . A power tower is an object analogous to a foliation from differential geometry. In particular, it admits its own versions of many key results about foliations, such as a “fibrations inject into foliations” and a “Frobenius theorem”. The latter has a nontrivial twist: the local structure is trivial at every point, but it may differ between points! In general, our analogy between power towers and foliations explains why purely inseparable morphisms are foliation-like, because these morphisms are power towers in an explicit way. Finally, we use our theory to produce a formula for pullbacks of canonical divisors for arbitrary purely inseparable morphisms. We use it to conclude some “not-Lüroth theorems”: if the characteristic is high enough, then any variety purely inseparably covered by an n n -dimensional projective space has Iitaka dimension equal to − ∞ -\infty or n n , i.e., it is not zero.



1.
Introduction

Algebraic geometry is an area of mathematics that studies systems of polynomial equations. Frequently, the coefficients of these equations are taken from a fixed field. Any field has a characteristic, which can be equal to zero, e.g., for complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
, or to a prime number
p>0
, e.g., for a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. In the second case, we say that the field is of a positive characteristic. Therefore, we have two types of algebraic geometries over fields: the characteristic zero and the positive characteristic. They have different flavors. The first one is analytical, the second one is arithmetical. Analysis is strongly developed. Consequently, we know plenty about the characteristic zero. On the other hand, classical calculus theorems are not true modulo
p
. Instead, we have the Frobenius morphism:
x\mapsto x^{p}
on any ring satisfying
p=0
. This generates a new class of morphisms: purely inseparable morphisms.
This class is exclusive to the positive characteristic. Most of us know about them from the field theory, where they are called purely inseparable subfields.
Surprisingly, after over 100 years of studying fields abstractly, which was started in
[
Ste10
]
by Ernst Steinitz and his influence is explained in
[
Roq10
]
, we still know little about these morphisms - even when we restrict our attention to fields!
This paper aims to change it for both - for fields and for varieties.

Definition 1.1

(Purely Inseparable Ring Map)
.

Let
B\to A
be an injective morphism between commutative rings. This morphism is
purely inseparable
of exponent at most
r
if for every
a\in A
we have
a^{p^{r}}\in B
. It is of exponent
r
if the integer
r
is the minimal such.

Definition 1.2

(Purely Inseparable Morphism)
.

A morphism
f:X\to Y
between normal varieties is
purely inseparable
of exponent
\leq r
if it is finite, dominant, and affine locally it is purely inseparable of exponent
\leq r
. It is of exponent
r
if the integer
r
is the minimal such.

We say that the morphism
f
is purely inseparable if there is a number
r
such that
f
is purely inseparable of exponent
\leq r
.
1
1

1

For varieties, any purely inseparable morphism is of finite exponent, thus we only define these.

Our main goal in this paper is to establish a Galois-type description for all purely inseparable morphisms - for fields, where we work with subfields, and for varieties, where we work with morphisms. This description refers to a correspondence between these morphisms and other algebraic objects that are easier to handle. In our case, we cannot use groups of automorphisms, because these are blind to
p
-powers; thus, instead, we will use subalgebras of differential operators. We will get a correspondence between purely inseparable morphisms and finite subalgebras of differential operators. However, a natural framework for this theorem uses a new notion:
a power tower
, which is analogous to a foliation (a nice set of vector fields that resembles a fibration). With this notion, we get a correspondence between all subalgebras of differential operators and power towers that restricts to the correspondence for purely inseparable morphisms/subfields.

As a happy consequence of the analogy between power towers and foliations, we establish new, never mentioned anywhere else, properties of purely inseparable morphisms. e.g., any such morphism naturally induces a stratification on its source!

Our secondary goal is to explore explicitly some positive characteristic phenomena. This label refers to all theorems that are unique to the positive characteristic compared to characteristic zero. Usually, they are about “pathologies” related to some techniques or theorems from characteristic zero not being true modulo
p
:
vanishing theorems
[
HP16
]
,
the minimal model program for surfaces

[
Mum69
]
,
etc. In many cases, such as the ones just cited, first we discover that they are no longer verbatim true modulo
p
, then we correct the statements. Often, the new theorems explicitly involve the Frobenius morphism or other purely inseparable morphisms.

In particular, this paper explores the interaction between vector fields and fibrations modulo
p
. The expected result from the characteristic zero fails: vector fields tangent to the fibers do not determine the fibration! The correction is the notion of
power towers
. It emerges as the tangent vector fields plus all that must be added to that to determine the fibration.

Finally, we establish a formula for pullbacks of canonical divisors along arbitrary purely inseparable morphisms. So, we get a tool to track flows of geometric information along these morphisms. We use it to show how some unexpected unirationality of varieties may arise in positive characteristic.

Extra, this paper’s informal short online presentation is available online
[
Gra24
]
.

Question 1.3
.

Do purely inseparable morphisms explain all positive characteristic phenomena?

For example, let’s say we have a theorem from characteristic zero whose statement is not true in positive characteristic. Is there a purely inseparable morphism explicitly involved in this failure?

1.1.
A Brief Literature Overview

The previous main approaches towards purely inseparable Galois theory are:

(1)

In
[
Jac44
]
, Jacobson proved that purely inseparable subfields of exponent one correspond to
p
-Lie algebras.

(2)

In
[
Jac64
]
, a book, we can find Jacobson–Bourbaki correspondence, which says that all subfields of a given field correspond to closed subalgebras of linear endomorphisms. This is based on the works of H. Cartan
[
Car47
]
and Jacobson
[
Jac47
]
. No specialization to all purely inseparable subfields was established, which was well known.

(3)

In
[
Eke87
]
, Ekedahl proved Jacobson correspondence for varieties. It covers exponent one. He also introduced some sketches towards higher exponents, but his ideas were not complete, nor followed by others, till now.

(4)

In
[
Swe68
]
, Sweedler proved that “modular” purely inseparable subfields correspond to sets of higher derivations. These can have arbitrary exponents, but they do not cover all subfields. In his thesis
[
Wec13
]
, Wechter compared the Jacobson–Bourbaki correspondence with Sweedler’s one. It says the higher derivations are a basis of the algebra.

(5)

In
[
BW20
]
, a preprint, Brantner and Waldron used “derived
p
-Lie algebras” called partition Lie algebroids to give a correspondence between them and purely inseparable subfields of any fixed finite exponent.

Our theory of power towers unifies, expands, and completes the first three approaches. The fourth one is a case of our theory when extra structure of a free action of a specific infinitesimal group (
G\cong\alpha_{p^{n_{1}}}\oplus\ldots\oplus\alpha_{p^{n_{k}}}
) is present. That action induces a preferred basis for the algebra.

Indeed, our Galois theory will show that purely inseparable subfields correspond to finite subalgebras of differential operators. This is a specialization of the Jacobson–Bourbaki correspondence. The case of exponent one is obtained by taking operators of order
1
; in this case, they generate the subalgebra. This is Jacobson’s correspondence. We extend these results to varieties via saturation and get Ekedahl’s correspondence. Then, we clarify his sketches using our theory. This is the first follow-up of these ideas in the literature. Finally, if a subfield is modular, then the higher derivations are a special choice of a basis for its subalgebra. But this is an extra choice; we want to work out a natural theory first.

Question 1.4
.

Let
G
be an infinitesimal finite group. Can we obtain “a Galois theory in the form of Sweedler’s one” for the group
G
? I.e., how to describe quotients of free actions of
G
on fields using “higher derivations”-like objects?

The answer is: Yes, we can. However, the question asks “how to do it nicely?” because some choices are involved.

1.2.
What Is a Power Tower? Why Is It Like a Foliation?

A
power tower
is a sequence of approximations up to
p^{n}
-powers.

Definition 1.5

(Up to
p^{n}
-powers.)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
W\subset K
be a subfield. An approximation of
W
up to
p^{n}
-powers is the composite of the subfields
W
and
K^{p^{n}}\coloneqq\{x^{p^{j}}:\ x\in K\}
, i.e.,
W\cdot K^{p^{n}}
. We denote it by
W_{n}
.

Definition 1.6

(Power Tower)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. A power tower on
K
is a sequence of subfields
W_{n}\subset K
for
n=0,1,2,\ldots
such that
W_{j}=W_{i}\cdot K^{p^{j}}
for
j\leq i
.

In differential geometry, the notion of a foliation emerges as a product of gluing local fibrations on a manifold into a global object. This is how Ehresmann introduced it, see e.g.
[
Ehr07
]
. In particular, tautologically, (global)
fibrations inject into foliations
. Immediately, it was established that Lie subalgebras of tangent bundles are equivalent to foliations. Thus, this injection is just the fact that if we take a fibration, then vector fields tangent to its fibers, and then we integrate these vector fields, then in the end, we will get the initial fibration back.

However, modulo
p
, vector fields are weaker.

Derivations kill
p
-powers modulo
p
, e.g.,
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(g^{p})=pg^{p-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(g)=0
.

Thus, a single integration only gives the fibration up to
p
-powers, not the whole fibration. However, we can iterate and collect all the approximations up to
p^{n}
-powers together, and then they recover nice fibrations/morphisms/subfields. We provide an explicit example below, Example
1.9
.

Our Theorem
4.37
covers all subfields that can be recovered from their power towers. In particular, we have:

•

Purely inseparable subfields inject into power towers,
Lemma
4.8
. Consequently, every such subfield admits a canonical decomposition into a sequence of subfields of exponent one - its power tower.

•

Purely transcendental subfields inject into
\infty
-foliations
, Proposition
4.49
, a special class of power towers.

Consequently, under certain natural assumptions, power towers allow us to reduce the study of subfields of a given field into a study of algebraic separable and purely inseparable subfields only, and their finite and infinite compositions.

For varieties, we extend the definition of power towers via normalization, Definition
5.9
, and we obtain a similar result:

•

Separable fibrations inject into
\infty
-foliations,
Theorem
5.65
.

Question 1.7
.

Can we reduce the study of positive characteristic algebraic geometry to finite and infinite interactions between only three classes of operations: étale morphisms, purely inseparable morphisms, and birational modifications?

Local structure of a power tower - a
Frobenius theorem
.

In (complex) differential geometry, foliations are trivial in local analytic coordinates, i.e., at every (regular) point
x
, in some formal coordinates, a foliation
(X/\mathbb{C},\mathcal{F})
is given by

\mathbb{C}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}]]\to\mathbb{C}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]]\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}},

where
m\leq n
. This is a Frobenius theorem for foliations,
[
Voi02
, Theorem 2.20]
.

Power towers satisfy analogous theorem
5.34
- the
Frobenius theorem for power towers
. Locally, in some formal coordinates, at a regular point, it is given by

k[[t_{1}^{p^{a_{1}}},t_{2}^{p^{a_{2}}},t_{3}^{p^{a_{3}}},\ldots,t_{d}^{p^{a_{d}}}]]\to k[[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},\ldots,t_{d}]],

where
0\leq a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq a_{3}\leq\ldots\leq a_{d}\leq\infty
. The convention is
t^{p^{\infty}}=0
. (The classical theorem corresponds to using only
a_{i}=0,\infty
.)

Consequently, we get a function
G
from closed points to tuples of indices

x\mapsto(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})
.
This function does not have to be constant!
Nevertheless, it is constructible, Theorem
5.38
, and thus we get a stratification of our variety, each piece being the locus where the index is constant, e.g., Example
5.53
:

\text{For }f:k[x,y]\supset k[x^{p^{2}},y^{p},y^{p+1}-x^{p}],\text{ we get $G(f):(x,y)\mapsto(a_{1},a_{2})$=}\begin{cases}(0,2),\text{ if }y\neq 0,\\
(1,1),\text{ if }y=0.\end{cases}

Question 1.8
.

What are all possible stratifications induced by purely inseparable morphisms?

Power towers admit the same origin as foliations in differential geometry and have analogous formal local structures. Therefore, they are foliation-like, and anything that can be interpreted as a power tower is foliation-like too. This includes purely inseparable morphisms and nice fibrations.

1.3.
The Basic Example

It is harder to state our Galois theory than to understand it in practice. Therefore, we will give an example and show how the theory applies to it.

Example 1.9
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
f:\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
be the projection corresponding to the ring inclusion
k[x,y]\supset k[y]
— a set of parallel lines on a plane. Here, we will compute its power tower. The following diagram summarizes it.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\ldots}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{1}{p^{2}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{2}}}{\partial x^{p^{2}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{1}{p!}\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial x^{p}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k[x,y]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k[x^{p},y]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k[x^{p^{2}},y]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cdots}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k[y]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{2}}}}

The relative tangent bundle of
f
, vector fields tangent to the fibers, is spanned by
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
. Integrating
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
means to solve
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(g)=0
. By definition, the solution is
k[y]
up to
p
-powers of
k[x,y]
. It equals the ring
k[x^{p},y]
, because derivations kill
p
-powers. The subring
k[x^{p},y]
and the vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
clearly determine each other. This is a case of Jacobson–Ekedahl correspondence.

Vector fields are weaker modulo
p
. We see a manifestation
\{g\in k[x,y]\ :\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(g)=0\}\neq k[y]
. Nevertheless, we can repeat the above computation for
k[x^{p},y]\hookleftarrow k[y]
. We will get
k[y]
up to
p
-powers on
k[x^{p},y]
, which is also
k[y]
up to
p^{2}
-powers on
k[x,y]
. It is the subring
k[x^{p^{2}},y]
that corresponds to the vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}}
.

We repeat over and over. We will get
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{n}}}
and
k[x^{p^{n+1}},y]
that determine each other. Moreover, any of this sequences determine
k[y]
, because
k[y]=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}k[x^{p^{n}},y]
. This is a case of nice fibrations injecting into power towers.

However, out of the
blue
, there is more to this story, because the iterative data we have just obtained can be packed into a subalgebra of differential operators, whose elements, after restricting them to the subrings, will become the vector fields doing approximations up to
p
-powers, e.g.,

\frac{1}{p!}\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial x^{p}}
becomes
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}}
on
k[x,y]
,
\frac{1}{p^{2}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{2}}}{\partial x^{p^{2}}}
becomes
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{2}}}
on
k[x^{p^{2}},y]
, and so on.

1.4.
Galois Theory for Power Towers

Our Galois theory for power towers, Theorems
4.15
and
5.23
, applied to Example
1.9
.

Theorem 1.10

(Galois-Type Correspondence for a Power Tower)
.

Each of the following data from Example
1.9
can be computed from any other:

•

Power Tower
: a sequence of approximations up to
p^{n}
-powers, e.g.,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k[x,y]\supset k[x^{p},y]\supset k[x^{p^{2}},y]\supset\ldots}.

•

Jacobson Sequence:
a particular sequence of sets of vector fields,
p
-Lie algebras, each making an approximation up to
p
-powers, e.g.,

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{2}}},\ldots}.

•

Subalgebra of Differential Operators
, e.g.,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left<1,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{1}{p!}\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial x^{p}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{1}{p^{2}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{2}}}{\partial x^{p^{2}}}},\ldots\right>}\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(k[x,y]).

Explicitly, these computations are done by the following operations:

•

Annihilator:
\operatorname{Ann}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{n}}}})\coloneqq\{g\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k[x^{p^{n}},y]}:\ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{n}}}}(g)=0\}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k[x^{p^{n+1}},y]}
.

•

Tangent Space:
T_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}Y_{n}}/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}Y_{n+1}}}=\{a{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{n}}}}:\ a\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k[x^{p^{n}},y]}\}
.

•

Subalgebra of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left<1,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{1}{p!}\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial x^{p}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{1}{p^{2}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{2}}}{\partial x^{p^{2}}}},\ldots\right>}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Diff}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}Y_{n}}}(X)
.

•

Unpacking:
d(X/{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}Y_{n}})({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{1}{p^{n}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{n}}}{\partial x^{p^{n}}}})={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{n}}}}
.

Above, we used the notation:
X=\operatorname{Spec}(k[x,y]),Y_{n}=\operatorname{Spec}(k[x^{p^{n}},y])
.

Purely inseparable morphisms/subfields inject into power towers. Nice fibrations inject into power towers. Therefore, we can use the above theorem in these cases. In this way, we obtain a purely inseparable Galois theory. And, a new insight that nice fibrations are “infinite compositions” of purely inseparable morphisms, thus they enjoy the same descriptions via taking appropriate limits.

1.5.
Foliations,
1
-Foliations, and
\infty
-Foliations

Power towers are analogous to foliations from the characteristic zero or differential geometry. Here we explain their relationships.

Let
X/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
be a smooth variety over
p
-adic integers. Let
X_{0}/\mathbb{F}_{p}
be its reduction modulo
p
, i.e.,
X_{0}=X\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})}\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
. Let
\mathcal{F}\subset T_{X/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
be a regular foliation on
X
, i.e., a subbundle that is closed under Lie brackets.

Two cases may happen with
\mathcal{F}
modulo
p
:

(1)

It might be closed under
p
-powers. If so, then
\mathcal{F}
modulo
p
is a
1
-foliation on
X_{0}
.

(2)

It might NOT be closed under
p
-powers. Then
\mathcal{F}
modulo
p
is not foliation-like, but there is an obstruction towards being
1
-foliation. It is called a “
p
-curvature”. It is described in
[
Kat70
, Section 5]
. This can be used to study foliations in the characteristic zero, see
[
MP24
]
.

We are interested in the first case, where the information flows freely. However, we lose some information anyway. Indeed, if
X=\mathbb{A}^{2}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x,y])
and
\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}
are the relative tangent bundles for
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[y]\subset\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x,y]
and
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[y+x^{p}]\subset\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x,y]
respectively. They determine these fibrations, but their reductions modulo
p
are equal, i.e., these two fibrations modulo
p
are identical up to
p
-powers. What happened? Here is a helpful diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\to Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Diff}_{Y}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
Fibrations on
X
Foliations’ on
X
Foliations on
X
Fibrations on
X_{0}
\infty
-Foliations on
X_{0}
1
-Foliations on
X_{0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}\to Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Diff}_{Y_{0}}(X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X_{0}/Y_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{mod }p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{mod }p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{mod }p}

The dashed arrows are well defined only on the elements from the first case. The arrows are dashed to
emphasize
it.

The observation is that over
p
-adic integers (or in characteristic zero), derivations inside a subalgebra of differential operators determine
2
2

2

“Determine” is not “generate”. The generation is true in characteristic zero, but not for the
p
-adic integers.
this subalgebra. Above, we denote “foliations given by derivations” by Foliations, and “foliations given by subalgebras of differential operators” by Foliations’. These two notions are equivalent, but this stops being true after reducing them modulo
p
. The subalgebras are richer modulo
p
.

Consequently, the better reduction of
\mathcal{F}
modulo
p
is to take the (saturated) subalgebra of differential operators generated by it, and then to take the reduction modulo
p
of that subalgebra. The result will be an
\infty
-foliation extending
\mathcal{F}
modulo
p
. And,
\infty
-foliations differentiate between fibrations, because nice fibrations inject into
\infty
-foliations, so we no longer lose this information.

This discussion can be reversed into Theorem
6.9
. Here we provide its non-formal version.

Theorem 1.11

(Slogan version of Theorem
6.9
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}
be a
1
-foliation on a variety
X_{0}
. If there is a lifting of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
to a foliation
\mathcal{F}
on
X
, which is a lifting of
X_{0}
, then
\mathcal{F}
induces a unique extension of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
to an
\infty
-foliation on
X_{0}
.

Explicitly,
\mathcal{F}
determines a saturated subalgebra of differential operators on
X
. The reduction modulo
p
of this subalgebra is the extension.

This is a remarkable result, because from one lifting, one vector bundle, we get infinitely many vector bundles, the Jacobson sequence of the
\infty
-foliation.

1.6.
Canonical Divisor Formula

In the positive characteristic, purely inseparable morphisms are a new type of bridge allowing for varieties’ properties to flow through; new if compared to the characteristic zero. One of the basic holders of important geometric information is the canonical divisor, e.g., it is related to rationality questions. The following is our formula that extends Ekedahl’s formula
[
Eke87
]
[Corollary 3.4] from exponent one to all purely inseparable morphisms. And after it, we apply it to unirationality.

Theorem 1.12

(Canonical Divisor Formula: Theorems
7.1
and
7.2
)
.

Let
f:X\to Y\to X^{(n)}
be a purely inseparable morphism of exponent
n
between normal varieties over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
. We assume that the variety
X/k
is geometrically connected.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{1}=T_{X/Y}\coloneqq\Omega_{X/Y}^{*}
. Let
K_{X},K_{Y}
be canonical divisors of
X,Y
respectively.

Then, there exist explicit divisors
D_{2},D_{3},\ldots,D_{n}
on
X
such that

f^{*}K_{Y}-K_{X}=(p^{n}-1)(-c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1}))+\sum_{i=2}^{n}(p^{n+1-i}-1)D_{i},

where
c_{1}
is the first Chern class.
Explicitly, these divisors are given by, for
i\geq 2
,

D_{i}\coloneqq f_{i-1}^{*}c_{1}\left(\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}}\right),

where
Y_{\bullet}
is the power tower of
f
on
X
with
f_{i}:X\to Y_{i}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
is its Jacobson sequence, and
\mathcal{G}_{i}\coloneqq T_{Y_{i}/Y_{i-1}^{(1)}}
.

Moreover, if
f:X\to Y
is a
n
-foliation on
X
, then
D_{i}\geq 0
, for
i\geq 2
.

If
f:X\to Y
is an Ekedahl
n
-foliation on
X
, then
D_{i}=0
, for
i\geq 2
.

Question 1.13
.

What are possible collections of divisors coming from the above formula?

We can use the above formula to prove some “not-Lüroth theorems” for projective spaces.

Theorem 1.14

(Theorem
7.7
)
.

Let
n>1
be an integer.
Let
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k}
be the
n
-dimensional projective space over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
p-1
not divide
n+1
.

If
f:\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k}\to Y
is a purely inseparable morphism between normal varieties over
k
, then the Iitaka dimension of a canonical divisor of
Y
is equal
-\infty
or
n
, i.e., we have

\kappa(Y,K_{Y})=-\infty,\text{or }n.

This is interesting, because for
n=2
, we cannot have
\kappa(Y,K_{Y})=0
, which is puzzling. Indeed, K3 surfaces are such
Y
s, and in the positive characteristic, there are unirational K3 surfaces, see e.g.
[
Lie15
]
,
[
Lie21
]
, and the rational maps
\mathbb{P}^{2}\dashrightarrow Y
are not separable on the generic points. Our result implies that this map cannot be obtained directly from a single purely inseparable morphism, and thus some birational modifications must be present.

Question 1.15
.

I believe that if
p-1
divides
n+1
, then
\kappa(Y,K_{Y})\neq-\infty,\text{and }n
is possible. Of course, it will equal zero then, but I don’t have any example.

Can we uniformly describe all cases when
p-1
divides
n+1
and
\kappa(Y,K_{Y})=0
? Are these morphisms “special”?

Conventions

•

Let
X
be a scheme over a field
k
. The scheme
X
is a
variety
over the field
k
if it is integral, and the morphism
X\to\operatorname{Spec}(k)
is separated and of finite type.

Structure of the paper

The paper consists of
7
main sections:

(1)

Introduction. You have just read it.

(2)

Preliminaries, where we fix notation and recall some basic facts about fields, purely inseparable subfields/morphisms, differential operators, and divided powers. Though some facts might be actually new, we prove them.

(3)

We review previous methods for describing purely inseparable subfields/morphisms that directly precede the developments of this paper.

(4)

We develop a theory of power towers on fields.

(5)

We develop a theory of power towers on normal varieties.

(6)

We discuss a subproblem of a moduli problem for power towers. Namely, a problem of extending power towers into longer power towers.

(7)

We prove the formula for the pullback of canonical divisors with respect to an arbitrary purely inseparable morphism, and we give an application of this formula.

2.
Preliminaries

This section fixes notation and recalls key definitions that are used in this paper, as well as some basic facts about them. Some propositions might actually be new. An expanded version of this preliminary can be found in my thesis
[
Gra25
]
.

2.1.
Definitions and Basic Facts about Fields

Most of the field theory in this paper is standard and easily accessible. For example, here are some sources: Section 09FA in
[
Sta18
]
, Jacobson’s book
[
Jac64
]
, and Bourbaki’s book
[
Bou90
]
.

2.1.1.
Purely Inseparable

Definition 2.1
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.

A field extension
K/W
is
purely inseparable
if for every
x\in K
there is a natural number
n
such that
x^{p^{n}}\in W
.

Definition 2.2

(Subfields of
p
-Powers)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
n\geq 0
be an integer.
A subfield of
p^{n}
-powers of
K
is the subfield
K^{p^{n}}\coloneqq\{x^{p^{n}};x\in K\}
.

Definition 2.3

(Supfields of
p
-Roots)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
n\geq 0
be an integer.
A supfield of
p^{n}
-roots of
K
is the subfield
K^{1/p^{n}}\coloneqq\{x^{1/p^{n}}\in\overline{K};x\in K\}
of an algebraic closure
\overline{K}
of
K
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
The field
K
is
perfect
, if
K=K^{p}
.

The following naming “(co-)perfection” I took from
[
Ked
, Definition 3.4.1]
.

Definition 2.5
.

A
coperfection
, or a
perfect closure
, of a field
K
is a supfield
K^{1/p^{\infty}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{n\geq 0}K^{1/p^{n}}
, where
p
-roots and the union are taken in a single algebraic closure of
K
.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
A
perfection
of a field
K
is the subfield
K^{p^{\infty}}\coloneqq\bigcap_{n\geq 0}K^{p^{n}}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

The perfection of a field is a perfect field. Moreover, it is the largest perfect subfield of this field.

Proof.

Let
K
be a field with
\operatorname{char}(K)=p>0
.
We prove its perfection is perfect. Indeed, we have:

{\left(K^{p^{\infty}}\right)}^{p}=\left(\bigcap_{n\geq 0}K^{p^{n}}\right)^{p}=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}K^{p^{n+1}}=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}K^{p^{n}}=K^{p^{\infty}}.

We prove it is the largest perfect subfield. We notice that if
L
is a perfect subfield, then, for every
n\geq 0
, we have
K^{p^{n}}\supset L^{p^{n}}=L^{p^{n-1}}=\ldots=L
. Therefore, we conclude
\bigcap_{n\geq 0}K^{p^{n}}\supset L
.
∎

Definition 2.8
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
n\geq 0
be an integer, or
\infty
.

A field extension
K/W
is of
exponent
\leq n
if
K\supset W\supset K^{p^{n}}
. And, it is of exponent
n
, if the
n
is minimal such.

Remark 2.9
.

In the literature, at least two names are used to refer to exponents of subfields. For example, the first one is the
exponent
used in Jacobson’s book
[
Jac64
]
. For instance, the second one is a
height
used in Bourbaki’s book
[
Bou90
]
. I prefer to use exponents.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
such that
K/K^{p^{\infty}}
is a finitely generated field extension.

Then every purely inseparable subfield of
K
of exponent
\leq\infty
is of finite exponent.

Proof.

Let
K/W/K^{p^{\infty}}
be a purely inseparable extension of exponent
\leq\infty
. Let
f_{i}
be a finite set of generators of
K
over
K^{p^{\infty}}
. Then, for every
f_{i}
there is an integer
n_{i}>0
such that
f_{i}^{p^{n_{i}}}\in W
. Let
N
be the greatest integer among them. Then,

K^{p^{N}}=K^{p^{\infty}}(f_{i}^{p^{N}})\subset W.

This proves that
K/W
is of exponent
\leq N
.
∎

2.1.2.
p
-basis and separable transcendental basis

Lemma 2.11

(Separable Closure,
[
Sta18
]
[09HC, 030K])
.

Let
L/K
be an extension of fields. There is a maximal subextension
L/K^{s}/K
such that
K^{s}/K
is separable algebraic, where the maximality means that if
L/F/K
is a subextension such that
F/K
is separable algebraic, then
F\subset K^{s}
. The field
K^{s}
is called a
separable closure
of
K
in
L
.

Moreover, if
L/K
is algebraic, then
L/K^{s}
is purely inseparable.

The following two definitions are from
[
Sta18
, 030O (2)]
, or
[
Eis95
, Definition on page 557]
.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
K/W
be a field extension. A subset
S\subset K

is a
separable transcendence basis
of
K/W
if
S
is a transcendence basis of
K/W
, and the extension
K/W(x)_{x\in S}
is separable algebraic, i.e.,
\left(W(x)_{x\in S}\right)^{s}=K
.

If
K/W
admits a separable transcendence basis, then we say that
K
is
separably generated
over
W
.

Definition 2.13
.

A field extension
K/W
is a
separable
extension if for every subextension
K/K^{\prime}/W
with
K^{\prime}
finitely generated as a field over
W
, the extension
K^{\prime}/W
is separably generated.

Corollary 2.14

(MacLane 1939,
[
ML39
]
)
.

Let
K/k
be a field extension with
k
being a perfect field. Then, this extension is separable.

Some basic facts about
p
-bases, I took them from
[
Sta18
, Section 07P0]
.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
p
be a prime number. Let
K/k
be an extension of fields of characteristic
p
. Denote
k\cdot K^{p}
the compositum of
k
and
K^{p}
in
K
.

(1)

A subset
\{x_{i}\}\subset K
is called
p
-independent over
k
if the elements
x^{e}\coloneqq\prod x_{i}^{e_{i}}
where
0\leq e_{i}<p
are linearly independent over
k\cdot K^{p}
.

(2)

A subset
\{x_{i}\}
of
K
is called a
p
-basis of
K
over
k
if the elements
x^{E}
form a basis of
K
over
k\cdot K^{p}
.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
K/k
be a field extension. Assume
k
has characteristic
p>0
. Let
\{x_{i}\}
be a subset of
K
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

the elements
\{x_{i}\}
are
p
-independent over
k
,

(2)

the elements
dx_{i}
are
K
-linearly independent in
\Omega_{K/k}
.

Consequently, any
p
-independent collection can be extended to a
p
-basis of
K
over
k
. In particular, the field
K
admits a
p
-basis over
k
. Moreover, the following are equivalent:

(1)

\{x_{i}\}
is a
p
-basis of
K
over
k
,

(2)

dx_{i}
is a basis of the
K
-vector space
\Omega_{K/k}
.

These two notions, a
p
-basis and a separable transcendence basis, will be equivalent for us. The following is Corollary
2.14
combined with
[
Eis95
, Corollary A1.5]
].

Theorem 2.17
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension.
Then, every
p
-basis of
K
over
k
is a separable transcendence basis of
K/k
, and vice versa.

2.2.
Purely Inseparable Morphisms and Frobenius Morphisms

We already defined purely inseparable morphisms in Definitions
1.1
and
1.2
. These definitions are inspired by private notes on positive characteristic algebraic geometry by Joe Waldron and Zsolt Patakfalvi. I mention it because “purely inseparable morphisms” sometimes are defined as any morphisms that induce purely inseparable subfields on generic points. However, such “purely inseparable morphisms” are too wobbly, too shapeless; thus, we have extra “rigidity” conditions on what we call a purely inseparable morphism. Luckily, this rigidity makes the connection with power towers easy.

Definition 2.18

(Absolute Frobenius Morphism)
.

Let
X
be a scheme over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Then taking
p
-powers in local affine charts defines a morphism
F_{X}:X\to X
. This is the
absolute Frobenius morphism
of
X
.

Definition 2.19

(Relative Frobenius Morphism,
[
Sta18
, Definition 0CC9]
)
.

Let
S
be a scheme over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Let
X
be an
S
-scheme. Then a
relative Frobenius morphism
of
X/S
, denoted by
F_{X/S}
, is defined as the unique dashed arrow from the diagram below.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X/S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{S}}

where
X^{(1)}
is a pullback of
X\to S
and
F_{S}
.

Definition 2.20
.

We define
X^{(2)}
to be
{X^{(1)}}^{(1)}
,
X^{(3)}
to be
{{X^{(2)}}}^{(1)}
, and so on.

The following states that, for a normal variety, purely inseparable morphisms from it and purely inseparable subfields of its generic point are in a natural bijection with each other.

Proposition 2.21
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
, then purely inseparable morphisms from
X
are in a bijection with subfields
W\subset K(X)
of finite exponent, i.e., the ones for which there exists
r
such that
K(X)^{p^{r}}\subset W
.

In particular, purely inseparable factorizations of
F^{r}_{X/k}
given as
X\to Y\to X^{(r)}
with
Y
being a normal variety over
k
, are in bijection with purely inseparable subfields of
K(X)
of exponent
\leq r
.

Proof.

First, we note that we can restrict the reasoning to finite exponents by Lemma
2.10
.

Our proof is an adaptation of the proof of
[
PW22
, Proposition 2.9.]
.
Indeed, we have a sequence of equivalent notions:

•

purely inseparable morphisms
X\to Y\to X^{(r)}
with
Y
being a normal variety over
k
,

•

coherent sheaves of normal rings
\mathcal{O}_{X}\supset\mathcal{A}\supset\mathcal{O}_{X}^{p^{r}}
,

•

intermediate subfields
K(X)\supset W\supset K(X)^{p^{r}}
.

The equivalences are respectively given by:

•

X\to Y\to X^{(r)}\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{X}\supset\mathcal{O}_{Y}\supset\mathcal{O}_{X}^{p^{r}}

•

\mathcal{O}_{X}\supset\mathcal{O}_{Y}\supset\mathcal{O}_{X}^{p^{r}}\mapsto K(X)\supset K(Y)\supset K(X)^{p^{r}}
.

These operations are bijections, because
X,Y
have the same underlying topological space, but different functions on it. To recover
Y
we take the space of
X
with the sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
defined as the normalization of
\mathcal{O}_{X}^{p^{r}}=\mathcal{O}_{X^{(r)}}
in
W
from
K(X)\supset W\supset K(X)^{p^{r}}
.
∎

2.3.
Saturation

In Proposition
2.21
, we used normalization of a ring/sheaf to get a bijection. On the other side of our Galois-type correspondence for power towers, this operation will correspond to taking a saturation.

Definition 2.22
.

Let
X
be a variety over a field
k
. Let
M
be a torsion-free coherent sheaf on
X
. Let
\mathcal{M}=M\otimes K(X)
be the pullback of
M
to the generic point of
X
. Let
\mathcal{N}
be a
K(X)
-vector subspace of
\mathcal{M}
.

We define the
saturation
of
\mathcal{N}
in
M
to be the following subsheaf
N
of
M
:

N(U)\coloneqq\mathcal{N}\cap M(U),

where
U
is an open subset of
X
, and
M(U)\to\mathcal{M}
is the localization.

Finally, we say that a subsheaf
N\subset M
is
saturated
if it is equal to its saturation. (Equivalently, it means that
M/N
is torsion-free.)

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
X
be a variety over a field
k
. Let
M
be a torsion-free coherent sheaf on
X
.
Let
\mathcal{N}
be a subspace of
\mathcal{M}
.

Then, the saturation of
\mathcal{N}
in
M
is a saturated coherent subsheaf of
M
.

Proof.

Trivially, the saturation of a vector subspace is a coherent subsheaf, because it is quasicoherent and inside a coherent one. It is saturated, because if not, then the quotient has a torsion, so there is a local section
x
in
M
, and a local regular function
f
such that
fx
belongs to the saturation, but
x
does not. This is a contradiction with the definition of saturation.
∎

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
X
be a variety over a field
k
. Let
M
be a torsion-free coherent sheaf on
X
. There is a bijection between saturated coherent subsheaves of
M
and vector subspaces of
\mathcal{M}=M\otimes K(X)
. Explicitly, it is given by pullbacking to the generic point, and the inverse is the saturation in
M
.

Proof.

We have to show that these operations are well-defined and inverse to each other.

They are well defined as the first is a pullback, the second one is well defined by Lemma
2.23
.

We show that they are inverse to each other.
Indeed, if
\mathcal{N}
is a subspace, then its saturation
N
in
M
determines every
N(U)
, and these sets determine
\mathcal{N}
. If
N
is a saturated subsheaf, then the saturation of
\mathcal{N}=N\otimes K(X)
is equal to
N
by the definition.
∎

2.4.
Differential Operators

This section is an exposition of an algebraic theory of differential operators based on Grothendieck’s EGA IV
[
Gro67
, Chapter 16]
.

Derivations over a polynomial ring
R=k[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
can be given by formulas of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}
, where
a_{i}\in R
. Suppose
k
is a field of characteristic zero. In that case, any differential operator on
R
is a linear combination of compositions of derivations, i.e., it can be written as
\sum_{I=(c_{i})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}a_{I}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}^{c_{i}}
, where
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
,
a_{I}\in R
. However, in positive characteristic, it is not true, e.g.,
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}^{p}=0
, so we cannot reach the operator
\frac{1}{p!}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}^{p}
via compositions and sums. Yet, this operator must be included, because the natural construction of differential always gives them, or for pragmatic reasons: this operator allows us to get the coefficient next to
x^{p}
. Consequently, the algebraic theory of differential operators modulo
p
initially admits more complexity, but one can quickly realize that this “pathology” is a blessing.

2.4.1.
Inductive Definition

Leibniz’s rule definition of a derivation generalizes to the following one for differential operators.

Definition 2.25
.

Let
R
be a ring and
k
its subring.

For
d\geq-1
we define inductively the module
F_{d}\mathcal{D}_{R/k}
of differential operators of order
\leq d
for
R/k
as follows:

•

F_{-1}\mathcal{D}_{R/k}\coloneqq\{0\}\subset\operatorname{End}_{k}(R)
.

•

For
d\geq 0
,
F_{d}\mathcal{D}_{R/k}\coloneqq\{D\in\operatorname{End}_{k}(R);\ \forall_{f\in R}\ [D,f]\in F_{d-1}\mathcal{D}_{R/k}\}
.

The algebra of differential operators for
R/k
is defined by

\mathcal{D}_{R/k}\coloneqq\bigcup_{d}F_{d}\mathcal{D}_{R/k}\subset\operatorname{End}_{k}(R).

Example 2.26
.

Let
R=k[x]
be a ring of polynomials in one variable over a ring
k
. Let
a\geq 0
be an integer. We can define a
k
-linear function
\frac{1}{a!}\frac{\partial^{a}}{\partial x^{a}}
from
R
to
R
by the formula:

\frac{1}{a!}\frac{\partial^{a}}{\partial x^{a}}(x^{b})\coloneqq{{b}\choose{a}}x^{b-a}.

It is a differential operator of order
a
.
The algebra
\mathcal{D}_{R/k}
is a free
R
-module spanned by all these operators
\frac{1}{a!}\frac{\partial^{a}}{\partial x^{a}}
.

2.4.2.
Universal Construction

The inductive definition is pretty, but it is hard to work with. Here we will describe a definition of differential operators analogous to the one for derivations via the module of Kähler differentials.

Definition 2.27
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings. We define the following objects:

•

A first projection
p_{1}:R\to R\otimes_{k}R
is given by
a\mapsto a\otimes 1
.

•

A second projection
p_{2}:R\to R\otimes_{k}R
is given by
a\mapsto 1\otimes a
.

•

A diagonal map
\Delta_{R/k}:R\otimes_{k}R\to R
is given by
a\otimes b\mapsto ab
.

•

The kernel of
\Delta_{R/k}
is denoted by
J_{R/k}
.

•

A universal “derivation”
d:R\to R\otimes_{k}R
is equal
p_{2}-p_{1}
, i.e., it is given by
a\mapsto 1\otimes a-a\otimes 1
.

Convention 2.28
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings. Unless stated otherwise, the ring
R\otimes_{k}R
is considered an
R
-algebra via
p_{1}
.

Definition 2.29
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings. A
space of principal parts
of
R
over
k
of order at most
n
is
defined by

\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{R/k}\coloneqq R\otimes_{k}R/J^{n+1}_{R/k}.

Remark 2.30
.

For
m\geq n
, there is a natural surjective map
\mathcal{P}^{\leq m}_{R/k}\to\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{R/k}
, i.e., the quotient map.

Definition 2.31
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings.

The set of
differential operators
of order at most
n
on
R
relative to
k
is

\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{k}(R)\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{R/k},R).

The set of
differential operators
on
R
relative to
k
is

\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)\coloneqq\operatorname{colim}_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{k}(R)=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{k}(R),

where the sum is taken with respect to the natural identifications.

The filtration of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
by
\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{k}(R)
is called the (standard) order filtration.

2.4.3.
Comparison of definitions

Lemma 2.32
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings.
The
R
-algebra
R\otimes_{k}R
naturally decomposes as an
R
-module into
R\oplus J_{R/k}
, because
p_{1}
splits
0\to J_{R/k}\to R\otimes_{k}R\to R\to 0
.

Proposition 2.33
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings.
There is a submodule
{\swabfamily E}(R/k)
of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
corresponding to
J_{R/k}
in the decomposition
R\oplus J_{R/k}=R\otimes_{k}R
such that
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)=R\oplus{\swabfamily E}(R/k)
. Namely, it is given by
{\swabfamily E}(R/k)\coloneqq\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(J_{R/k}/J_{R/k}^{n+1},R)
.

Remark 2.34
.

The above notation
{\swabfamily E}(R/k)
is very non-standard. I introduced it in my thesis. A naive notation for this object would be something like

\operatorname{Diff}^{\geq 1}_{k}(R)
, but it is vague and super bad, so I want to avoid it. The name of the font is “textswab”.

Definition 2.35
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings. Let
\Phi
be a differential operator of order at most
n
on
R
relative to
k
, i.e., a morphism of
R
-modules
\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{R/k}\to R
. A
k
-linear operator on
R
corresponding to
\Phi
is given by

D_{\Phi}\coloneqq\Phi\circ p_{2}:R\to R\otimes_{k}R\to\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{R/k}\to R,

where the middle arrow is dividing by the ideal
J_{R/k}^{n+1}
.
The above operation defines a natural morphism of
R
-modules

\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)\to\operatorname{End}_{k}(R):\Phi\mapsto D_{\Phi}.

Finally, the comparison.

Proposition 2.36
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings. The natural map
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)\to\operatorname{End}_{k}(R)
is an injection, and the image of
\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{k}(R)
is
F_{n}\mathcal{D}_{R/k}
.

Moreover, the natural decomposition
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)=R\oplus{\swabfamily E}(R/k)
in terms of endomorphisms is given by

D\mapsto(D(1),D-D(1)).

So, the module
{\swabfamily E}(R/k)
consists of differential operators whose value on
1
is zero.

2.4.4.
Graded Algebras of Differential Operators

Definition 2.37
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings. We define a
graded algebra of differential operators
on
R
over
k
, denoted by
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
, by the following formulas:

\displaystyle\operatorname{gr}^{i}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq i}_{k}(R)/\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq i-1}_{k}(R),

\displaystyle\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\operatorname{gr}^{i}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R),

where
\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq i}_{k}(R)=0
for
i<0
.

Definition 2.38
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings. Let
D\in\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
be a differential operator of order
i
. We define the
leading form
of
D
, denoted by
[D]
, to be the image of
D
with respect to the composition of
\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq i}_{k}(R)\to\operatorname{gr}^{i}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)\to\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
.

Definition 2.39
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings. Let
S
be a subset of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
. We define a graded subset of
S
, or a gradation of
S
, by the formula
\operatorname{gr}(S)\coloneqq\{[D]:D\in S\}
.

Lemma 2.40
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings. We assume
J_{R/k}/J_{R/k}^{2}
is locally free.

Then, we have a natural isomorphism
\operatorname{gr}^{i}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(J_{R/k}^{i}/J_{R/k}^{i+1},R)
.

Proof.

If
J_{R/k}/J_{R/k}^{2}
is locally free, then all
R\otimes_{k}R/J_{R/k}^{i}=R\oplus J_{R/k}/J_{R/k}^{i}
are locally free, so we have
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(R\otimes_{k}R/J_{R/k}^{i},R)=0
.

Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{gr}^{i}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq i}_{k}(R)/\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq i-1}_{k}(R)=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(R\otimes_{k}R/J_{R/k}^{i+1},R)/\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(R\otimes_{k}R/J_{R/k}^{i},R)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(J_{R/k}^{i}/J_{R/k}^{i+1},R).

∎

2.4.5.
Explicit Formulas Using Coordinates

The propositions and definitions below are from a short
[
Gro67
, Section 16.11]
. It allows us to work with differential operators via coordinates. It is useful, because in this way we can check orders of our operators just by writing them down and looking at them.

Definition 2.41
.

Let
f:k\to R
be a smooth morphism of rings. Let
\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
be a
finite
set of elements of
R
. We say that the set
\{x_{i}\}
is a set of coordinates for
k\to K
if it is a basis of
\Omega_{R/k}
.

Thus, we say that
k\to R
admits coordinates, if
k\to R
is smooth, and
\Omega_{R/k}
is free.

Example 2.42
.

Our two cases of admitting coordinates that we use in this paper are:

•

A geometrically regular (i.e., smooth) variety over a perfect field admits coordinates locally. Any local basis of Kähler differentials is a set of coordinates.

•

If
K
is a field of characteristic
p>0
, and
K/K^{p^{\infty}}
is finitely generated, then
K^{p^{\infty}}\to K
admits coordinates. In this case, it is any
p
-basis.

Remark 2.43
.

The above assumption about smoothness is there solely to get the following result that is REALLY what we want from “
admitting coordinates
”, and the only thing we use from that assumption in the whole paper. Thus, in this paper, the next proposition could be taken as a definition of “admitting coordinates”.

Proposition 2.44

(Monomials Generate Spaces of Principal Parts)
.

Let a ring map
k\to R
admit coordinates. Let
\{x_{i}\}
be a set of coordinates of
R
over
k
. Then monomials
d(x)^{e}\coloneqq\prod d(x_{i})^{e_{i}}
, with
e_{i}\geq 0
and
\sum e_{i}\leq n
, form a
R
-basis of
\mathcal{P}_{R/k}^{\leq n}
.

Definition 2.45
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings that admits coordinates. Let
\{x_{i}\}
be a finite set of coordinates of
R
over
k
. We denote the
R
-basis of
\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{k}(R)
that is dual to the
R
-basis
d(x)^{e}
of
\mathcal{P}_{R/k}^{\leq n}
by
D^{e}
, i.e.,
D^{e}(d(x)^{f})=\begin{cases}1,\ \text{if }e=f\\
0,\ \text{if }e\neq f\end{cases}
. We will refer to this basis as the
dual basis
of
\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{k}(R)
corresponding to the set of coordinates
\{x_{i}\}
for
k\to R
.

Remark 2.46
.

The dual basis strongly depends on the choice of the set of coordinates, e.g., both sets
\{x,y\}
and
\{x,x+y\}
are sets of coordinates for
k[x,y]
over
k
. Still, their dual bases are not equal, though it will often happen that they will have operators that are denoted exactly the same way, but are not actually equal. For example, we have:
\{x,y\}
gives rise to
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
, and
\{x,x+y\}
gives rise to
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial x+y}
. However,
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\neq\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
! Therefore, whenever we encounter explicit formulas, it is important to know the whole set of coordinates; otherwise, it is likely that we will make some silly mistakes.

Corollary 2.47
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings that admits coordinates. Let
\{x_{i}\}
be a
finite
set of coordinates of
R
over
k
. The operators
D^{e}
with
\sum e_{i}\geq 0
are a basis of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
.
We call this basis the
dual basis
of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
corresponding to the coordinates
x_{i}
.

The following is a part of
[
Gro67
, Theorem 16.11.2]
.

Proposition 2.48
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings that admits coordinates. Let
\{x_{i}\}
be a finite set of coordinates of
R
over
k
. Let
D^{e}
be the dual
R
-basis of
\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{k}(R)
corresponding to
\{x_{i}\}
. These operators are determined by their values on monomials
x^{f}=\prod_{i}x_{i}^{f_{i}}
, where
f_{i}\geq 0
. These values are:

D^{e}(x^{f})={f\choose e}x^{f-e}=\prod_{i}{f_{i}\choose e_{i}}x_{i}^{f_{i}-e_{i}}.

We mean
D^{e}:R\to R
, not
D^{e}:\mathcal{P}_{R/k}^{\leq n}\to R
for some
n
. (We abuse notation not to write
D_{D^{e}}
.)

Definition 2.49
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings that admits coordinates. Let
\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
be a finite set of coordinates of
R
over
k
.
For every
i=1,2,\ldots,n
and
a\geq 0
we define a differential operator
\frac{1}{a!}\frac{\partial^{a}}{\partial x_{i}^{a}}
on
R
over
k
by the formula
3
3

3

In particular, we do not actually divide by
a!
. It is just a (suggestive) symbol.

\frac{1}{a!}\frac{\partial^{a}}{\partial x_{i}^{a}}(\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{b_{j}})={{b_{i}}\choose{a}}x_{i}^{b_{i}-a}\prod_{j=1,j\neq i}^{n}x_{j}^{b_{j}}.

This operator’s order is
a
.

Corollary 2.50
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings that admits coordinates. Let
\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
be a finite set of coordinates of
R
over
k
. Let
D^{e}
be an operator from the dual basis corresponding to
x_{i}
with
e=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
, then

D^{e}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{e_{i}!}\frac{\partial^{e_{i}}}{\partial x_{i}^{e_{i}}}.

Remark 2.51
.

One can say that the values of the operators
\frac{1}{a!}\frac{\partial^{a}}{\partial x_{i}^{a}}
are
the obvious ones
. The same is true about most relations between them because we can check any relation by comparing values on monomials.

For example, an obvious relation is that all these operators
\frac{1}{a!}\frac{\partial^{a}}{\partial x_{i}^{a}}
commute with each other.

For example, if
p=0
, then a non-obvious relation from a differential geometry point of view is
\left(\frac{1}{a!}\frac{\partial^{a}}{\partial x_{i}^{a}}\right)^{\circ p}=0
for
a\geq 1
. This relation is easy to check on monomials.

2.4.6.
Differential

We discuss the difference between algebras of differential operators. We will define it under mild assumptions following
[
Gro67
, page 23]
.

A motivation for a differential comes from differential geometry. For any smooth map between two manifolds
f:X\to Y
, we get a differential between their tangent spaces
df:TX\to f^{*}TY
. However, it is true that
df
naturally extends to a map
df:\operatorname{Diff}(X)\to f^{*}\operatorname{Diff}(Y)
between their spaces of differential operators. This is what we will define in a general algebraic setting.

In characteristic zero, the first order
TX
determines the algebra
\operatorname{Diff}(X)
, so there is not much gain from considering the extension. However, in general, the algebra
\operatorname{Diff}(X)
is a richer source of information about
X
than
TX
alone, e.g., this exactly happens in positive characteristic.

Definition 2.52
.

Let
k\to A\xrightarrow{f}R
be morphisms of rings.
These maps induce a morphism

A\otimes_{k}A\to R\otimes_{k}R:a\otimes b\mapsto f(a)\otimes f(b)

and this induces morphisms

\Delta^{\leq n}_{R/A}:\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k}=A\otimes_{k}A/J_{A/k}^{n+1}\rightarrow R\otimes_{k}R/J_{R/k}^{n+1}=\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{R/k}.

Definition 2.53
.

Let
k\to A\to R
be morphisms of rings. We have the following natural maps:

(1)

\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{R/k},R)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k}\otimes R,R):\varphi\mapsto\varphi\circ(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k}\otimes R\xrightarrow{\Delta^{\leq n}_{R/A}\otimes 1}\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{R/k})
,

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k},R)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k}\otimes R,R):\varphi\mapsto(a\otimes x\mapsto x\varphi(a))
,

(3)

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k},A)\otimes R\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k},R):\varphi\otimes y\mapsto((A\to R)\circ\varphi)\cdot y
.

Remark 2.54
.

Item 2 from Definition
2.53
is always an isomorphism. It is a universal property of extending scalars from
A
to
R
.

The following definition is non-standard. I introduced it a bit ad hoc.

Definition 2.55
.

Let
k\to A\to R
be morphisms of rings. We say that these morphisms
admit the differential
if the map from item 3 of Definition
2.53
is an isomorphism.

Definition 2.56

(Differential Map for Differential Operators)
.

Let
k\to A\to R
be morphisms of rings that admit the differential. We define
differentials

d^{\leq n}(R/A):\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{k}(R)\to\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{k}(A)\otimes R

by being the composition of the map 1. with the inverses of the maps 2. and 3. from Definition
2.53
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{k}(R)\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k}\otimes R,R)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\text{The item 1.}}\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k}\otimes R,R)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\left(\text{The item 2.}\right)^{-1}}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k},R)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\left(\text{The item 3.}\right)^{-1}}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k},A)\otimes R

\displaystyle\eqqcolon\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{k}(A)\otimes R.

These differentials
\leq n
are compatible with each other and define the
differential

d(R/A):\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)\to\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)\otimes R.

Proposition 2.57
.

Let
k\to A\to R
be morphisms of rings. If
k\to A
is a morphism of rings that admits coordinates, then
k\to A\to R
admits the differential.

Proof.

From
k\to A
admitting finite coordinates, by Proposition
2.44
, we get that
\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k}
is a free
A
-module of finite rank, say
N
. Then, there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k}\simeq\bigoplus^{N}A
. The choice of this isomorphism induces the following canonical isomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k},A)\otimes R

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\bigoplus^{N}A,A)\otimes R\simeq\prod^{N}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(A,A)\otimes R

\displaystyle\rightarrow\prod^{N}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(A,R)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\bigoplus^{N}A,R)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k},R).

The above arrow is an isomorphism, because, in each coordinate, it is explicitly given by the following formula:

(1\mapsto a)\otimes r\mapsto(1\mapsto a\cdot r)

whose explicit inverse is

(1\mapsto r)\mapsto(1\mapsto 1)\otimes r.

∎

Lemma 2.58

(Differential is Restriction)
.

Let
k\to A\to R
be morphisms of rings that admit the differential. Let
D
be a differential operator on
A
relative to
k
, then
d(R/A)(D)=D_{|A}:A\to R
, i.e., the composition
A\to R\xrightarrow{D}R
.

Proof.

It follows from the explicit formulas for the natural maps from Definition
2.53
that appear in the definition of the differential
2.56
.
∎

Example 2.59
.

Let
K
be a field of positive characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be its perfection. We assume that
K/k
is a finitely generated field extension.

Let
x_{1},\ldots x_{n}
form a set of coordinates for
K/k
. (It exists, because it is any
p
-basis, Theorems
2.14
and
2.17
.) Their
p^{m}
-th powers
x_{1}^{p^{m}},\ldots,x_{n}^{p^{m}}
form a set of coordinates for
K^{p^{m}}/k
. Moreover, we have that

d(K/K^{p})\left(\frac{1}{p^{m}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{m}}}{\partial x_{i}^{p^{m}}}\right)=\frac{1}{p^{m-1}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{m-1}}}{\partial\left(x_{i}^{p}\right)^{p^{m-1}}}.

Consequently,

d(K/K^{p^{m}})\left(\frac{1}{p^{m}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{m}}}{\partial x_{i}^{p^{m}}}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(x_{i}^{p^{m}}\right)}.

We can conclude that all these differentials
d(K^{p^{m}}/K^{p^{n}})
, for
m\leq n
, are surjective, which is a special case of my theorem
4.26
. Furthermore, this surjectivity may justify an abuse of notation to use symbols
\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(x_{i}^{p^{m}}\right)}
and
\frac{1}{p^{m}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{m}}}{\partial x_{i}^{p^{m}}}
interchangeably.

2.4.7.
p
-Filtration

From Definition
2.25
, every algebra of differential operators admits a filtration by orders, i.e.,
\bigcup_{n}\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{k}(R)=\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
. It is the “standard filtration”. However, in positive characteristic, algebras of differential operators admit one more filtration that is called a
p
-filtration
in
[
Haa87
, Section 1.2.]
. This filtration is induced by Frobenius morphisms. Moreover, one can easily argue that it is, in some ways, a better filtration than the standard one, because it is a filtration by subalgebras, not just by subsets. Finally, in a positive characteristic, this filtration explains why the algebra of differential operators is usually not finitely generated. The
p
-filtration is not discussed in
[
Gro67
]
.

Definition 2.60

(
p
-Filtration)
.

Let
k\to R
be an injection of rings such that there is a prime number
p
such that
p=0
in
k
.

We define a
p
-filtration
of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
to be the collection of subalgebras
\operatorname{Diff}_{R^{p^{n}}\cdot k}(R)
for
n\geq 0
.

Lemma 2.61
.

The
p
-filtration is a filtration of the algebra of differential operators.

Proof.

We have to show that
\operatorname{Diff}_{R^{p^{n}}}(R)
is a subalgebra of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
, and that
\bigcup\operatorname{Diff}_{R^{p^{n}}}(R)=\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
. Moreover, without loss of generality, we assume that
k\subset R^{p^{n}}
for every
n
, so we do not have to write down the smallest ring containing both
k
and
R^{p^{n}}
, denoted by
R^{p^{n}}\cdot k
, in the below computation.

We show that it is a subalgebra. By Definitions
2.29
and
2.31
we have
\mathcal{P}^{\leq m}_{R/k}=R\otimes_{k}R/J^{m+1}_{R/k}
and
\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq m}_{k}(R)=\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq m}_{R/k},R)
,
\mathcal{P}^{\leq m}_{R/R^{p^{n}}}=R\otimes_{R^{p^{n}}}R/J^{m+1}_{R/R^{p^{n}}}
, and
\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq m}_{R^{p^{n}}}(R)=\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq m}_{R/R^{p^{n}}},R)
.
Therefore, it is enough to compare the spaces
\mathcal{P}^{\leq m}_{R/R^{p^{n}}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\leq m}_{R/k}
with each other. First, we observe that there is an ideal
I^{(p^{n})}
of
R\otimes_{k}R
generated by the set
\{d(x^{p^{n}});\ x\in R\}
satisfying

R\otimes_{k}R/I^{(p^{n})}=R\otimes_{R^{p^{n}}}R.

So, for every
m
, we have a surjection
\mathcal{P}^{\leq m}_{R/k}\to\mathcal{P}^{\leq m}_{R/R^{p^{n}}}
inducing the injection
\operatorname{Diff}_{R^{p^{n}}}(R)\to\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
.

We show that the sum of these subalgebras is the whole algebra. Again, we notice that for on
R\otimes_{k}R
we have an equality of ideals
J^{m+1}_{R/R^{p^{n}}}=J^{m+1}_{R/k}+I^{(p^{n})}
. However, if
m+1=p^{n}
, then we have that
I^{(p^{n})}\subset J^{m+1}_{R/k}
. So, then
J^{m+1}_{R/R^{p^{n}}}=J^{m+1}_{R/k}
, and therefore
\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq p^{n}-1}_{R^{p^{n}}}(R)=\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq p^{n}-1}_{k}(R)
. Consequently, we get the equality
\bigcup\operatorname{Diff}_{R^{p^{n}}}(R)=\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
.
∎

Example 2.62
.

Let
k
be a field of characteristic
p>0
and
R=k[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, then the
p
-filtration satisfies:

•

\operatorname{Diff}_{R^{p}}(R)
is generated by
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}
,

•

\operatorname{Diff}_{R^{p^{2}}}(R)
is generated by
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}},\frac{1}{p!}\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial x_{i}^{p}}
,

•

\operatorname{Diff}_{R^{p^{3}}}(R)
is generated by
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}},\frac{1}{p!}\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial x_{i}^{p}},\frac{1}{p^{2}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{2}}}{\partial x_{i}^{p^{2}}}
,

•

In general,
\operatorname{Diff}_{R^{p^{n}}}(R)
is generated by
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}},\frac{1}{p!}\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial x_{i}^{p}},\ldots,\frac{1}{p^{n-1}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{n-1}}}{\partial x_{i}^{p^{n-1}}}
.

Furthermore, each of the subalgebras above has a basis consisting of monomials in generators enlisted next to it with powers of the generators being smaller than
p
, e.g., for
n=3
,
\operatorname{Diff}_{R^{p^{3}}}(R)
has a
R
-basis made of
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}^{a_{1}}\circ\frac{1}{p!}\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial x_{j}^{p}}^{a_{2}}\circ\frac{1}{p^{2}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{2}}}{\partial x_{k}^{p^{2}}}^{a_{3}}
, where
0\leq a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}<p
, and
i,j,k\in\{1,2,3\}
.

We can observe that all the inclusions
\operatorname{Diff}_{R^{p^{n}}}(R)\to\operatorname{Diff}_{R^{p^{n+1}}}(R)
are proper inclusions, thus
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)
is not finitely generated algebra over
R
.

2.4.8.
Relative Differential Operators

We define differential operators over
k
between
R
-modules
D:M\to N
for given
k\to R
. The previous definitions and work were about the case
M=N=R
. We will use this notion only once in Theorem
5.38
(The stratification exists.), where we will have
M=R,N=R/q
, where
q
is an ideal in
R
.

Definition 2.63

(
[
Gro67
, Proposition (16.8.4)]
)
.

Let
k\to R
be a morphism of rings. Let
M,N
be
R
-modules, then

\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(R)^{\leq n}(M,N)\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{R/k}\otimes M,N).

Now, we could repeat enlisting all extra structures: the inductive definition, the standard filtration, the graded algebra, the
p
-filtration, etc. However, I believe in the reader, so I skip it.

2.5.
Divided Power Rings

A divided power ring intuitively is a ring
R
with a structure consisting of countably many functions
f_{n}:R\to R
, which should be thought of as the operations
x\mapsto\frac{x^{n}}{n!}
. However, these operations cannot be canonically defined for every ring; therefore, if we want to work with them, we have to choose a set of such functions. For us, the need for introducing them comes from differential operators, because graded algebras of differential operators (under mild assumptions) admit a
canonical
divided power structure (and even if not, then they always have it at least partially!). This is useful because it makes a lot of explicit computation involving leading forms of differential operators possible and relatively easy.

Remark 2.64
.

Abstract divided powers were introduced to algebraic geometry to allow Taylor expansions in positive characteristic. Indeed, if we have a polynomial
f\in k[x]
, then

f(x+h)=f(x)+f^{(1)}(x)h+\frac{f^{(2)}(x)h^{2}}{2!}+\frac{f^{(3)}(x)h^{2}}{3!}+\ldots+\frac{f^{(n)}(x)h^{n}}{n!}+\ldots,

But
\frac{1}{n!}
could make no sense, so we must do something about it. One can add
\frac{h^{n}}{n!}
abstractly. This leads to crystalline cohomology. I want to thank Jan Stienstra for sharing this origin story with me. However, we will use divided powers differently. We will show that
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
admits a canonical such structure, Proposition
2.75
, so it will be more about
[\frac{\partial^{n}}{n!\partial x^{n}}]
.

2.5.1.
Divided Powers

Definition 2.65

(
[
Sta18
, Definition 07GL]
)
.

A
divided power ring
is a triple
(A,I,\gamma)
, where
A
is a commutative ring,
I
is an ideal of
A
, and
\gamma
is a collection of functions
\gamma_{i}:I\to A
for
i\geq 0
such that

(1)

For all
x\in I
, we have
\gamma_{0}(x)=1
,
\gamma_{1}(x)=x
, and
\gamma_{i}(x)\in I
for
i>1
.

(2)

For all
x,y\in I
, we have
\gamma_{k}(x+y)=\sum_{i+j=k}\gamma_{i}(x)\gamma_{j}(y)
.

(3)

For all
\lambda\in A
,
x\in I
, we have
\gamma_{k}(\lambda x)=\lambda^{k}\gamma_{k}(x)
.

(4)

For all
x\in I
, we have
\gamma_{i}(x)\gamma_{j}(x)={{i+j}\choose{i}}\gamma_{i+j}(x)
.

(5)

For all
x\in I
, we have
\gamma_{i}(\gamma_{j}(x))=\frac{(ij)!}{i!j!^{i}}\gamma_{ij}(x)
.

Definition 2.66
.

[
Sta18
, Section 07H4]

Let
A
be a ring. A
divided power polynomial algebra
over
A
in
t\geq 1
variables is the following
A
-module:

A\left<x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}\right>\coloneqq\bigoplus_{n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{t}\geq 0}Ax_{1}^{[n_{1}]}\cdot x_{2}^{[n_{2}]}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{t}^{[n_{t}]}

with the (commutative) multiplication determined by

x_{i}^{[n]}\cdot x_{i}^{[m]}\coloneqq{{n+m}\choose n}x_{i}^{[n+m]}.

We also set
x_{1}=x_{i}^{[1]}
. Note that
1=x_{1}^{[0]}\cdot x_{2}^{[0]}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{t}^{[0]}
. There is a canonical
A
-algebra map
A\left<x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}\right>\to A
sending
x_{i}^{[n]}
to zero for
n>0
. The kernel of this map is denoted
A\left<x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}\right>_{+}
.

Lemma 2.67

(
[
Sta18
, Lemma 07H5]
for
I=0
)
.

Let
A
be a ring. Let
t\geq 1
. There exists a natural divided power structure
\delta
such that

(A\left<x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}\right>,A\left<x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}\right>_{+},\delta)
is a divided power ring. This structure is determined by

\delta_{n}(x_{i})=x_{i}^{[n]},

where
i=1,\ldots,t
and
n\geq 0
.

Here is a valuable observation that directly follows from the definition.

Lemma 2.68
.

Let
A
be a ring. Let
R=A\left<x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}\right>
be a divided power polynomial ring. It is a graded ring, where the order is given by the sum
n_{1}+\ldots+n_{t}
, the numbers are from the “exponents”
[n_{i}]
, i.e.,
R_{d}=\bigoplus_{n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{t}\geq 0;\sum n_{i}=d}Ax_{1}^{[n_{1}]}\cdot x_{2}^{[n_{2}]}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{t}^{[n_{t}]}
.

Moreover, if the ring
A
is an integral domain of characteristic
p=0
, then
R
admits a
p
-filtration
F_{p^{\bullet}}
by “partial divided power polynomial rings” that are the subrings

F_{p^{n}}(R)=\bigoplus_{p^{n}>n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{t}\geq 0}Ax_{1}^{[n_{1}]}\cdot x_{2}^{[n_{2}]}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{t}^{[n_{t}]}.

In particular, the order
p^{n}
of the divided power polynomial ring naturally decomposes

R_{p^{n}}\cong\left(F_{p^{n}}(R)\cap R_{p^{n}}\right)\oplus\left(\gamma_{p^{n}}\left(R\right)\otimes_{\gamma_{p^{n}}(A)}A\right).

Proof.

The only thing that must be checked is that
\left(x_{i}^{[{n}]}\right)^{p}=0
for all
i=1,2,\ldots,t
, and
n\geq 0
, if
A
is of characteristic
p>0
. This follows from the identity
[
Sta18
, Lemma 07GM (1)]

p!\gamma_{p}(x)=x^{p}

that holds for any divided power ring
(A,I,\gamma)
if
x\in I
. The rest follows.
∎

2.5.2.
Graded Dual Algebras of Symmetric Algebras

We recall what graded dual algebras are in the case of symmetric algebras. Furthermore, we prove that these subalgebras naturally are divided power rings. Later, we will prove that many graded algebras of differential operators are graded dual algebras of symmetric algebras, Proposition
2.71
, so this will prove that each of these graded algebras of differential operators admits the canonical divided power structure.

Definition 2.69

(
[
Bou98
, page 587]
)
.

Let
A
be a ring. Let
M
be a finite
A
-module. The
graded dual of the symmetric algebra

S(M)
is

S(M)^{*gr}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(S^{n}(M),A),

where
S^{n}(M)
is the
m
-th symmetric power of the module
M
.
Moreover, we introduce the following notations:

\displaystyle\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{n}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(S^{n}(M),A),

\displaystyle\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{>0}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(S^{n}(M),A).

Proposition 2.70
.

Let
A
be a ring. Let
M
be a finite
A
-module. Then
the symmetric algebra
S(M)
admits a natural coalgebra structure given by

c:S(M)\xrightarrow{S(\Delta)}S(M\times M)\xrightarrow{h}S(M)\otimes_{A}S(M),

where
\Delta:M\to M\times M
is given by
x\mapsto(x,x)
, and
h
is the ring homomorphism determined by
M\times M\ni(x,y)\mapsto x\otimes 1+1\otimes y\in S(M)\otimes_{A}S(M)
.

Consequently, the graded dual algebra
S(M)^{*gr}
is naturally a commutative ring with respect to the coalgebra structure
c
.

Proof.

The natural coalgebra structure
c
on
S(M)
is defined in
[
Bou98
, page 575, Example (6)]
. It is shown that this structure
c
is cocommutative in
[
Bou98
, page 582, Example (5)]
, hence the ring
S(M)^{*gr}
is commutative.
∎

The following result checks that the two rings defined in two contexts are isomorphic. I do not know how well known is this particular fact.

Proposition 2.71
.

Let
A
be a ring. Let
M
be a finite
A
-module. Then the graded dual algebra
S(M)^{*gr}
admits a natural extension to a divided power (commutative) ring

(S(M)^{*gr},\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{>0},\gamma)
.
This structure is uniquely determined by the functions

\gamma_{n}:\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{1}\to\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{n}

given by

\gamma_{n}(\varphi)(a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\ldots\otimes a_{n})=\varphi(a_{1})\cdot\varphi(a_{2})\cdot\ldots\cdot\varphi(a_{i}),

where
\varphi:M=S^{1}(M)\to A
, and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in M
.

Moreover, if
M
is free, and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{t}
is a
A
-basis of
\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{1}
, then the natural map of divided power rings

(A\left<x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}\right>,A\left<x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}\right>_{+},\delta)\to(S(M)^{*gr},\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{>0},\gamma)

that is given by
x_{i}\mapsto y_{i}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We prove the theorem for the free modules first, and then we conclude the general case from the free one.

Let
M
be a finite free module. Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{t}
be a basis of
M
. Then monomials
e^{a}\coloneqq e_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot e_{n}^{a_{t}}
are a basis of
S^{m}(M)
, where
a_{i}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{t}a_{i}=m
. Let
u_{a}
be the dual basis of
\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{m}
with respect to
e^{a}
. By the explicit calculation in
[
Bou98
, page 593, (33)]
,
we have:

u_{a}\cdot u_{b}=\prod_{i=1}^{t}{{a_{i}+b_{i}}\choose{a_{i}}}u_{a+b},

where the multiplication is the one of
S(M)^{*gr}
. This means that the
A
-homomorphism

A\left<x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}\right>\to S(M)^{*gr}

determined by

x_{1}^{[n_{1}]}\cdot x_{2}^{[n_{2}]}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{t}^{[n_{t}]}\mapsto u_{(n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{t})}

is a ring isomorphism. Also, the ideals
A\left<x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}\right>_{+}
and
\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{>0}
are mapped to each other by this isomorphism.
Finally, the functions

\gamma_{n}:\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{1}\to\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{n}
coincide with restrictions of the functions
\delta_{n}
(from Lemma
2.67
) to
\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{1}
. Consequently,
\delta
extends the functions
\gamma_{n}
to a divided power structure.
And, the ideal
\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{>0}
is generated by
\gamma_{n}(D)
for
D\in\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{1}
, it follows from applying axioms from Definition
2.66
. So, there exists at most one such extension of the functions
\gamma_{n}
to a divided power structure on
(S(M)^{*gr},\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{>0})
. Since we have already found one extension, i.e.,
\delta
, this extension exists and it is unique. Also, the definition of the functions
\gamma_{n}
is natural. Therefore, the structure
\delta
is natural.

Now, let
M
be a finite
A
-module. Let
N
be a finite free
A
-module with a surjection
f:N\to M
. Then we have a natural map

S(f)^{*gr}:S(M)^{*gr}\to S(N)^{*gr}

that is an injection of rings. We observe that the functions
\gamma_{n}
for
S(M)^{*gr}
are the restrictions of the functions
\gamma_{n}
for
S(N)^{*gr}
. Let
\delta
be the unique extension of
\gamma_{n}
to a divided power structure on
S(N)^{*gr}
.
By using the axioms from Definition
2.66
, we see that for every
D\in\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{>0})
we have
\delta_{n}(D)\in\left(S(M)^{*gr}\right)^{>0})
.
Hence, the restrictions of the functions
\delta_{n}
are a divided power structure on
S(M)^{*gr}
. Finally, this structure does not depend on
N
, because if we have two surjections
f_{1}:N_{1}\to M
and
f_{2}:N_{2}\to M
, where
N_{1},N_{2}
are finite free modules, then there exists a finite free module
N_{3}
with surjections
g_{1}:N_{3}\to N_{1}
and
g_{2}:N_{3}\to N_{1}
such that the unique divided power structures on
S(N_{1})^{*gr}
and
S(N_{2})^{*gr}
are the restrictions of the unique divided power structure on
S(N_{3})^{*gr}
. Therefore, the divided power structure we obtained on
S(M)^{*gr}
does not depend on
N
, so it is natural. This finishes the proof.
∎

2.6.
Tool: Diff=End Lemma

This subsection proves the following key lemma that will allow us to specialize the Jacobson–Bourbaki correspondence to purely inseparable subfields/morphisms. It might be well known to some experts, but I could not find any good source.

Proposition 2.72

(Diff=End Lemma)
.

Let
r
be a natural number. Let

A\supset B\supset A^{p^{r}}

be inclusions of rings of characteristic
p>0
. Let
A
be a finite
B
-module.
Then the natural map

\operatorname{Diff}_{B}(A)\to\operatorname{End}_{B}(A)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

By Definition
2.31
, we have that

\operatorname{Diff}_{B}(A)=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{B}(A)
and
\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{B}(A)=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(A\otimes_{B}A/J_{A/B}^{n+1},A)
,

where
A\otimes_{B}A
is a
A
-module with respect to
p_{1}
.

First, we prove that the ideal
J_{A/B}^{n+1}
is zero for
n
great enough. We recall an elementary fact that the ideal
J_{A/B}
is generated by elements
\tilde{d}(a)\coloneqq 1\otimes a-a\otimes 1
, where
a\in A
. Moreover, if
b\in B
, then
\tilde{d}(ba)=b\tilde{d}(a)
by a simple calculation

\tilde{d}(ba)=1\otimes ba-ba\otimes 1=b\otimes a-ba\otimes 1=(b\otimes 1)\tilde{d}(a)=b\tilde{d}(a).

In particular,
\tilde{d}(b)=0
, because
\tilde{d}(1)=0
.

Now, by
A/B
being finite, there exists an integer
m
and elements
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\in A
such that
A=\sum_{i=1}^{m}B\cdot x_{i}
. Therefore, for every
a\in A
, there exist
b_{i}\in B
such that
a=\sum_{i=1}^{m}b_{i}x_{i}
. From this, we conclude that
J_{A/B}
is generated by
\tilde{d}(x_{1}),\tilde{d}(x_{2}),\ldots,\tilde{d}(x_{m})
. Consequently, the ideal
J_{A/B}^{n+1}
is spanned by monomials
\tilde{d}(x_{1})^{e_{1}}\tilde{d}(x_{2})^{e_{2}}\ldots\tilde{d}(x_{m})^{e_{m}}
, where
\sum_{i=1}^{m}e_{i}\geq n+1
. Finally, if
n+1>mp^{r}-m
, then for at least one
i
we have
e_{i}\geq p^{r}
, say
i=1
, hence

\tilde{d}(x_{1})^{e_{1}}=\tilde{d}(x_{1})^{p^{r}}\tilde{d}(x_{1})^{e_{1}-p^{r}}=\tilde{d}(x_{1}^{p^{r}})\tilde{d}(x_{1})^{e_{1}-p^{r}}=0,

because
x_{1}^{p^{r}}\in A^{p^{r}}\subset B
. Therefore, all these monomials are zero, and hence the ideal
J_{A/B}^{n+1}
is zero for
n+1>mp^{r}-m
.

We already know that the ideal
J_{A/B}^{n+1}
is zero for
n
great enough, say
n>N
, where
N
is an integer. From this, we get

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}_{B}(A)=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{B}(A)=\bigcup_{n\geq N}\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq n}_{B}(A)=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{n\geq N}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(A\otimes_{B}A,A)=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(A\otimes_{B}A,A).

Now, the natural map
\operatorname{Diff}_{B}(A)\to\operatorname{End}_{B}(A)
is obtained by mapping
a function
A\otimes_{B}A\xrightarrow{f}A
to the composition
A\xrightarrow{p_{2}}A\otimes_{B}A\xrightarrow{f}A
.
This is the same map from the natural isomorphism
\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(A\otimes_{B}A,A)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(A,A)=\operatorname{End}_{B}(A)
. This proves the lemma.
∎

2.7.
Tool: Enveloping Algebras from Divided Powers

Here we will prove an interesting observation. Let
A/k
be a ring extension that admits a separable transcendental basis. Then the graded algebra
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
naturally is isomorphic to a divided power polynomial ring. Moreover, if we have a subalgebra of differential operators
\mathcal{D}\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
of the form
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{Diff}_{C}(A)
, where
k\to C\to A
, then the inclusion of their graded algebras factorizes through a divided power polynomial ring that depends only on the first order of
\mathcal{D}
, i.e., on the
p
-Lie algebra
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{D}\cap T_{A/k}=\operatorname{Der}_{C}(A)
and

\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}\subset{S}(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{*gr}\subset\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K).

Finally, these results hold for modules of relative differential operators, i.e., if we have
k\subset C\subset A\to B
. These results hold for the modules
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A,B)
and
\operatorname{Diff}_{C}(A,B)\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A,B)
, though the modules are not algebras - their graded modules are algebras! Even commutative ones.

These properties are key ingredients in some technical parts of the next chapters. In particular, it is used to prove that the stratification induced by regular power towers is constructible. Some experts might know these facts, but they are not known to everyone.

The precise statements follow now.

Lemma 2.73
.

Let
k\to A
be a morphism of rings that admits coordinates.

Then, the natural map

S(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2})\to\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}I_{A/k}^{i}/I_{A/k}^{i+1}

is an isomorphism of graded rings, where
S(V)
is the symmetric algebra for a
K
-vector space
V
, and
I_{K/k}
is the ideal of the diagonal for
A/k
.

Proof.

The natural map
S(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2})\to\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}I_{A/k}^{i}/I_{A/k}^{i+1}

exists by the universal property of the symmetric algebra
S(V)
for
V=I_{K/k}/I_{K/k}^{2}
(i.e.,
S(V)
is a polynomial ring.) applied to the inclusion
