I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2}\to\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}I_{A/k}^{i}/I_{A/k}^{i+1}.

It is an isomorphism by dimension counting in each degree. More specifically, if
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
form coordinates for
A/k
, then
dx_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdot dx_{2}^{a_{2}}\cdot\ldots\cdot dx_{n}^{a_{n}}
, where
a_{i}\geq 0
and
\sum a_{i}=m
, is a basis for
S^{m}(I_{K/k}/I_{K/k}^{2})
These elements are mapped to
\tilde{d}x_{1}^{a_{1}}\tilde{d}x_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots\tilde{d}x_{n}^{a_{n}}
mod
I_{A/k}^{m+1}
that are a basis of
I_{K/k}^{m}/I_{K/k}^{m+1}
, see Proposition
2.44
.. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.74
.

I do not like my definition of
“admitting coordinates”
. I have my two main examples, and they satisfy that definition, good!

However, all that I want from the notion of “admitting coordinates” is precisely the content of the above lemma: the map
S(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2})\to\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}I_{A/k}^{i}/I_{A/k}^{i+1}
is an isomorphism of
A
-modules and
I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2}
is free of finite rank. It is the same remark as before, but slightly restated.

Thus, my personal taste would be to redefine “admitting coordinates” to mean precisely that. I believe that this notion has an “official” name in the literature.

Proposition 2.75
.

Let
k\to A
be a morphism of rings that admits coordinates. Let
p
be a prime number. Let
p=0
in
A
.

Then, the graded algebra of differential operators

\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
extends naturally to a divided power ring

(\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A),[{\swabfamily E}(A/k)],\gamma)
, where
{\swabfamily E}(A/k)
is the subset of all operators
D
such that
D(1)=0
.

Moreover, if
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
form coordinates for
A/k
, then the divided power structure
\gamma
is determined by the values

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}\left(\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right]\right)=\left[\frac{1}{i!}\frac{\partial^{i}}{\partial x_{j}^{i}}\right]

for
j=1,2,\ldots,n
, and
i\geq 1
. In particular, we have an isomorphism of divided power rings

A\left<y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n}\right>\to\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)

given by
y_{i}\mapsto\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\right]
for
i=1,2,\ldots,n
, where
A\left<y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n}\right>
is a divided power polynomial algebra.

Proof.

First, we prove that there is a natural isomorphism of rings preserving grading

\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)\simeq S(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2})^{*gr},

where
I_{A/k}
is the diagonal ideal for
K/k
, and
S(M)^{*gr}
is a graded dual algebra of a symmetric algebra for an
A
-module
M
, Definition
2.69
.
Let
d\geq 0
be an integer. This isomorphism in degree
d
is given by the composition of morphisms of
A
-modules:

\displaystyle\operatorname{gr}^{d}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq d}_{k}(A)/\operatorname{Diff}^{\leq d-1}_{k}(A)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(I_{K/A}^{d}/I_{A/k}^{d+1},A)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(S^{d}(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2}),A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(S(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2})^{*gr}\right)^{d},

where the first isomorphism is from Lemma
2.40
and the second one from Lemma
2.73
.

We show that the above isomorphisms for all
d\geq 0
together define a ring map. We recall, Proposition
2.70
, that the natural ring structure on any
S(M)^{*gr}
is given by the map

c:S(M)\to S(M)\otimes_{A}S(M)

that is determined by
x\mapsto x\otimes 1+1\otimes x
for
x\in M=S^{1}(M)
. And, though in this paper we recalled the definition of the composition for differential operators to be the composition as operators, the composition
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
may be abstractly defined by the following maps

\delta:\mathcal{P}^{\leq a+b}_{A/k}\to\mathcal{P}^{\leq a}_{A/k}\otimes_{K}\mathcal{P}^{\leq b}_{A/k},

where
\mathcal{P}^{\leq a}_{A/k}
are defined in Definition
2.29
.
These maps
\delta
are from
[
Gro67
, Lemme 16.8.9.1]
, and, according to
[
Gro67
, page 45]
, they are determined by

A\otimes_{k}A\to\mathcal{P}^{\leq a}_{A/k}\otimes_{A}\mathcal{P}^{\leq b}_{A/k}:\ x\otimes y\mapsto\left(x\otimes 1\text{ mod }I_{A/k}^{a+1}\right)\otimes\left(1\otimes y\text{ mod }I_{A/k}^{b+1}\right).

Finally, by dividing by right powers of the ideal
I_{K/k}
, we obtain that the ring structure on
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
is given by the following comultiplication.

\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}I_{A/k}^{i}/I_{A/k}^{i+1}\to\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}I_{A/k}^{i}/I_{A/k}^{i+1}\otimes_{A}\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}I_{A/k}^{i}/I_{A/k}^{i+1}

This map is determined by
x\mapsto x\otimes 1+1\otimes x
for
x\in I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2}
. Hence, this is the above map
c
up to the isomorphism from Lemma
2.73
. Therefore, the natural isomorphism
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)\simeq S(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2})^{*gr}
of
A
-modules is an isomorphism of
A
-algebras.

Next, the isomorphism of graded rings
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)\simeq S(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2})^{*gr}
allows to enrich the algebra
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
into a divided power ring. Indeed, by Proposition
2.71
, the algebra
S(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2})^{*gr}
is a divided power ring in a natural way. It is isomorphic to the divided power polynomial algebra
A\left<y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n}\right>
with respect to the prescribed map from the theorem we are proving.

We finish the proof by showing that the divided power structure
\gamma
on
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
coming from
S(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2})^{*gr}
is the one determined by the requirement that
\gamma_{i}\left(\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right]\right)=\left[\frac{1}{i!}\frac{\partial^{i}}{\partial x_{j}^{i}}\right]
.

We recall that the space for principal parts
\mathcal{P}^{\leq m}_{A/k}
for
A/k
has a basis

\prod_{k=1}^{n}\tilde{d}x_{k}^{a_{k}}

for
0\leq a_{k}\leq m
and
\sum a_{k}\leq m
. Thus, the classes of

\prod_{k=1}^{n}\tilde{d}x_{k}^{a_{k}}
modulo
\mathcal{P}^{\leq m-1}_{A/k}

for
0\leq a_{k}\leq m
and
\sum a_{k}=m
form a basis of
S^{m}(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2})
, we denote them by
\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\tilde{d}x_{k}^{a_{k}}\right]
.

Now, we prove the operators
\gamma_{i}\left(\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right]\right)
and
\left[\frac{1}{i!}\frac{\partial^{i}}{\partial x_{j}^{i}}\right]
have the same values on
S^{i}(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2})
as linear operators.
In our computation, we will freely refer to Definition
2.59
of the operators
\frac{1}{i!}\frac{\partial^{i}}{\partial x_{j}^{i}}
, and to the definition of
\gamma_{i}
from Proposition
2.71
.

Let
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
,
i\geq 1
, and
m=i
. Then, we compute

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}\left(\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right]\right)\left(\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\tilde{d}x_{k}^{a_{k}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}\left(\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right]\right)\left(\bigotimes_{k=1}^{n}\left[\tilde{d}x_{k}\right]^{\otimes a_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\prod_{k=1}^{n}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right]\left(\left[\tilde{d}x_{k}\right]\right)^{a_{k}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\left(dx_{k}\right)^{a_{k}}=\begin{cases}1,\text{ if $a_{j}=i$,}\\
0,\text{ if $a_{j}\neq i$.}\end{cases}

And, we have

\displaystyle\left[\frac{1}{i!}\frac{\partial^{i}}{\partial x_{j}^{i}}\right]\left(\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\tilde{d}x_{k}^{a_{k}}\right]\right)=\frac{1}{i!}\frac{\partial^{i}}{\partial x_{j}^{i}}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{n}\tilde{d}x_{k}^{a_{k}}\right)=\begin{cases}1,\text{ if $a_{j}=i$,}\\
0,\text{ if $a_{j}\neq i$.}\end{cases}

These operators have equal values on the monomials from the basis, so they are equal to each other by Corollary
2.48
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.76
.

I do not know any direct reference for the fact that the graded algebra of differential operators for
k\to A
admits a canonical divided power structure. The only paper I know that explicitly mentions this fact is
[
Eke87
]
. In that paper, Ekedahl claims this is true, but he does not prove it or provide any reference.

Proposition 2.77
.

Let
k\to A
be a morphism of rings that admits coordinates. Let
k\to C\to A
be ring maps.

Let
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{Diff}_{C}(A)\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
be a subalgebra.
Let
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{D}\cap T_{A/k}=\operatorname{Der}_{C}(A)
.

Then, the natural inclusion

\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}\subset\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
of graded algebras
factorizes

\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}\subset{S}(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{*gr}\subset\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)

through the graded dual of the symmetric algebra of
S(\mathcal{F}^{*})
. The inclusions are ring morphisms.

Moreover, the ring
{S}(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{*gr}
is a divided power subring of
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
. It is isomorphic to a divided power polynomial algebra if
\mathcal{F}
is a free
A
-module. Consequently, it is generated as a subring of
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
by all elements
\gamma_{i}(D)
, where
D\in\mathcal{F},i\geq 0
.

Proof.

The inclusion
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}\subset\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
is induced by the surjection of rings

\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}I_{A/k}^{i}/I_{A/k}^{i+1}\to\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}I_{A/C}^{i}/I_{A/C}^{i+1}

via taking graded duals.

By Lemma
2.73
, we have
S(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2})\simeq\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}I_{A/k}^{i}/I_{A/k}^{i+1}
. Therefore, the above map is determined by the first degree
I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2}\to I_{A/C}/I_{A/C}^{2}
. Consequently, it factorizes through
S(I_{A/C}/I_{A/C}^{2})
:

S(I_{A/k}/I_{A/k}^{2})\simeq\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}I_{A/k}^{i}/I_{A/k}^{i+1}\to S(I_{A/C}/I_{A/C}^{2})\to\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}I_{A/C}^{i}/I_{A/C}^{i+1},

and both maps are surjective here.
Now, by taking graded duals of the above factorization, we obtain the inclusions

\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}\subset{S}(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{*gr}\subset\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K).

Finally, the algebra
{S}(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{*gr}
is a divided power polynomial ring, and it is generated by all
\gamma_{i}(D)
for
D\in\mathcal{F}
by Proposition
2.71
.
∎

Remark 2.78
.

The role of the algebra
{S}(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{*gr}
for a
p
-Lie algebra
\mathcal{F}
is analogous to the universal property of the universal enveloping algebra of a Lie algebra. So, one could call it a
universal divided power enveloping algebra
of the
p
-Lie algebra
\mathcal{F}
.
Actually, it is possible that there is a paper calling it precisely that way, but I do not have such a reference.

Moreover, we can observe that we did not use the characteristic of
A
at all. So, accidentally, we proved the existence of “universal enveloping algebras of Lie algebras” of some sort.

Corollary 2.79
.

Let
k\to A
be a morphism of rings that admits coordinates. Let
k\to C\to A\to B
be ring maps. We assume that
A\to B
is surjective.

Let
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{Diff}_{C}(A,B)\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A,B)
be a submodule.
Let
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{D}\cap\operatorname{Der}_{k}(A,B)=\operatorname{Der}_{C}(A,B)
. We assume
\mathcal{F}
is a free
A
-module.

Then, the natural inclusion

\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}\subset\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
of graded modules
factorizes

\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}\subset{S}(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{*gr}\subset\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A,B)

through the graded dual of the symmetric algebra of
S(\mathcal{F}^{*})
.

Moreover, the graded
B
-modules
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}
,
{S}(\mathcal{F}^{*})^{*gr},\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A,B)
are
B
-algebras.

Proof.

It is analogous to the previous theorem.

We recall Definition
2.63
. It follows that

\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A,B)\coloneqq\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)^{\leq n}(A,B)\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k}\otimes A,B)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k}\otimes A,A)\otimes_{A}B=\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)\otimes B,

where we have the isomorphism, because
\mathcal{P}^{\leq n}_{A/k}
is a free
A
-module.

Consequently, the graded module of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)\otimes B
is the graded dual of the ring

\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\left(I_{A/k}^{i}/I_{A/k}^{i+1}\otimes_{A}B\right)

as a
B
-module.

Similarly, the graded module of
\operatorname{Diff}_{C}(A,B)=\operatorname{Diff}_{C}(A)\otimes B
is the graded dual of the ring

\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\left(I_{A/C}^{i}/I_{A/C}^{i+1}\otimes_{A}B\right)

as a
B
-module.

Finally, we have a factorization of
B
-algebras:

\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\left(I_{A/C}^{i}/I_{A/C}^{i+1}\otimes_{A}B\right)\simeq S(I_{A/k}^{1}/I_{A/k}^{2})\otimes_{A}B\to S(I_{A/C}^{1}/I_{A/C}^{2}\otimes_{A}B)\to\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\left(I_{A/C}^{i}/I_{A/C}^{i+1}\otimes_{A}B\right)

Each map is a surjection; thus, the maps between their graded duals, as
B
-algebras, are injections. Moreover, the graded modules are graded dual algebras; thus, they are algebras.
∎

3.
Review of Previous Galois Theories

This section quickly reviews the Galois-type theories that we generalize in this paper. A more detailed presentation is available in my thesis
[
Gra25
]
, where I also discuss Sweedler’s theory following Wechter.

3.1.
Jacobson–Bourbaki Correspondence

In short, this correspondence says that a subfield
K\subset L
and an algebra
\operatorname{End}_{K}(L)
of linear endomorphisms of
L
determine each other naturally. These theorems are taken from
[
Jac80
, Section 8.2]
. I added the extra adjectives “topological” and “discrete”.

Definition 3.1

(Finite Topology/the Krull Topology)
.

Let
X,Y
be sets. The
finite topology
on functions from
X
to
Y
, this space is denoted by
Y^{X}
, is the topology with a base given by subsets of the form

U(f,S)\coloneqq\{g\in Y^{X}:\forall_{x\in S}g(x)=f(x)\}_{f,S},

where
f
is a function from
X
to
Y
, and
S
is a finite subset of
X
.

Theorem 3.2

(Topological Jacobson–Bourbaki Correspondence)
.

Let
L
be a field. There is a natural order reversing correspondence between subfields
K
of
L
, and closed in the finite topology
L
-subalgebras of
\operatorname{End}_{+}(L)
.

Explicitly,
this correspondence is given by the following formulas.

From subfields to closed subalgebras:

L\supset K\mapsto\mathcal{JB}(K)\coloneqq\operatorname{End}_{K}(L)\subset\operatorname{End}_{+}(L).

From closed subalgebras to subfields:

\mathcal{JB}=\overline{\mathcal{JB}}\subset\operatorname{End}_{+}(L)\mapsto\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{JB})\coloneqq\{x\in L;\forall_{D\in\mathcal{JB}}\ [x,D]=0\}\subset L.

Finite subalgebras are closed in the finite topology. This gives the following result.

Corollary 3.3

(Discrete Jacobson–Bourbaki Correspondence)
.

Let
L
be a field. There is a natural order reversing correspondence between subfields
K
of
L
with
[L:K]<\infty
, and finite dimensional
L
-subalgebras of
\operatorname{End}_{+}(L)
.

Explicitly,
this correspondence is given by the following formulas.

From subfields of finite coorder to finite-dimensional subalgebras:

L\supset K\mapsto\mathcal{JB}(K)\coloneqq\operatorname{End}_{K}(L)\subset\operatorname{End}_{+}(L).

From finite-dimensional subalgebras to subfields of finite coorder:

\mathcal{JB}\subset\operatorname{End}_{+}(L)\mapsto\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{JB})\coloneqq\{x\in L;\forall_{D\in\mathcal{JB}}\ [x,D]=0\}.

Moreover, we have that
\operatorname{dim}_{K}(L)=\operatorname{dim}_{L}(\mathcal{JB}(K))
.

Remark 3.4
.

For a Galois extension
L/K
with a Galois group
G
. The subalgebra
\mathcal{JB}=\operatorname{End}_{K}(L)
is the group ring
L[G]
, because it is inside and the dimensions agree. So,
G
is a nice choice of generators for
\mathcal{JB}
.

3.2.
Jacobson Correspondence

In short, this correspondence says that a subfield of exponent one
K^{p}\subset W\subset K
and a module of relative derivations
T_{K/W}\coloneqq\operatorname{Der}_{K^{p}}(K,W)
determine each other, if we have
[K:K^{p}]<\infty
. Originally, this theorem was proved in
[
Jac44
]
, but it can be obtained as a corollary of the Jacobson–Bourbaki correspondence, as it is done in the book
[
Jac80
]
. A part of deducing it in that way includes a lemma that gives an equivalent definition of a
p
-Lie algebra. We assume
\operatorname{char}(K)=p>0
.

Remark 3.5
.

Derivations kill
p
-powers, thus if we have
L\subset K^{p}\subset W
, then
\operatorname{Der}_{L}(K,W)=\operatorname{Der}_{K^{p}}(K,W)
, i.e.,
K^{p}
is a natural base to study derivations on
K
modulo
p
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
T_{K/K^{p}}
be the tangent space of
K
. We define two operations:

•

Lie bracket:
T_{K/K^{p}}\oplus T_{K/K^{p}}\to T_{K/K^{p}}:(D,E)\mapsto[D,E]\coloneqq D\circ E-E\circ D
,

•

p
-power:
T_{K/K^{p}}\to T_{K/K^{p}}:D\mapsto D^{\circ p}
.

Since
p=0
, we see that
v^{p}(fg)=\sum_{i=0}^{p}{{p}\choose{i}}v^{i}(f)v^{p-i}(g)=fv^{p}(g)+v^{p}(f)g
, so the above operations are well defined.

Definition 3.7
.

A
p
-Lie algebra on a field
K
is a
K
-vector subspace of
T_{K/K^{p}}
that is closed under Lie bracket and
p
-power operations.

We can provide an equivalent definition that does not use any fancy operations.

Lemma 3.8
.

We assume
[K:K^{p}]<\infty
.
Let
\mathcal{F}\subset T_{K/K^{p}}
be a
K
-vector subspace. Let
\left<\mathcal{F}\right>
be the smallest subalgebra of
\operatorname{End}_{k}(K)
containing
\mathcal{F}
, where
k=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}K^{p^{n}}
. Then
\mathcal{F}
is a
p
-Lie algebra on
K
if and only if the equality
\left<\mathcal{F}\right>\cap T_{K/K^{p}}=\mathcal{F}
is satisfied.

Theorem 3.9

(Jacobson Correspondence aka Jacobson’s Purely Inseparable Galois Theory for Exponent
1
)
.

Let
K
be a field of positive characteristic
p>0
. Let
k
be the perfection of
K
, i.e.,
k=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}K^{p^{n}}
. Assume that
K/k
is a finitely generated field extension.

Then, there is a natural inclusion reversing correspondence between
p
-Lie algebras on
K
and subfields
K\supset W\supset K^{p}
.

Explicitly, it is given by the following formulas.

From subfields of exponent
\leq 1
to
p
-Lie subalgebras:

K\supset W\supset K^{p}\mapsto T_{K/W}\subset T_{K/k}.

From
p
-Lie subalgebras to subfields of exponent
\leq 1
:

\mathcal{F}\subset T_{K/k}\mapsto K\supset\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F})\supset K^{p},

where
\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F})\coloneqq\{x\in K|\forall_{D\in\mathcal{F}}D(x)=0\}
.
Moreover,
p^{\operatorname{dim}_{K}(\mathcal{F})}=\operatorname{dim}_{W}(K)
.

Remark 3.10
.

The theorem still holds if we assume only that
[K:K^{p}]<\infty
. This is implied by our condition that
K\supset k
is finitely generated as a field extension. In other words, our statement is for generic points of varieties, because we only work in this case.

3.3.
Ekedahl Correspondence

Ekedahl’s correspondence is the Jacobson correspondence for normal varieties. Originally, it was proved by Ekedahl in
[
Eke87
, Proposition 2.2.]
. A more recent treatment is given in
[
PW22
]
.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
.
A
p
-Lie algebra
, or a
1
-foliation
, on
X
is a quasicoherent subsheaf
\mathcal{F}
of the tangent sheaf
T_{X/k}\coloneqq\Omega_{X/k}^{*}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\Omega_{X/k},\mathcal{O}_{X})
such that

•

\mathcal{F}
is saturated, i.e., the quotient
T_{X/k}/\mathcal{F}
is torsion-free,

•

\mathcal{F}
is closed under Lie bracket, i.e., for every open subset
U\subset X
and sections
v,w\in\mathcal{F}(U)
we have
[v,w]\in\mathcal{F}(U)
,

•

\mathcal{F}
is closed under
p
-power, i.e., for every open subset
U\subset X
and a section
v\in\mathcal{F}(U)
we have
v^{\circ p}\in\mathcal{F}(U)
.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
p
-Lie algebra on
X
.

Then, the sheaf of annihilated elements by
\mathcal{F}
is defined by

\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F})(U)=\{x\in\mathcal{O}_{X/k}(U):\ \forall_{D\in\mathcal{F}(U)}D(x)=0\},

where
U\subset X
is an open subset.

Remark 3.13
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
p
-Lie algebra on
X
. Then,
\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F})
is a quasicoherent sheaf of algebras on
X^{(1)}
, because vector fields kill
p
-powers.

Theorem 3.14

(Ekedahl Correspondence)
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
. Then there is a bijection between purely inseparable morphisms
X\to Y\to X^{(1)}
with
Y
a normal variety over
k
and
1
-foliations on
X
.

Explicitly, it is given by the following formulas.

From purely inseparable morphisms of exponent
\leq 1
to
1
-foliations:

X\to Y\to X^{(1)}\mapsto T_{X/Y}\subset T_{X/k}.

From
1
-foliations to purely inseparable morphisms of exponent
\leq 1
:

\mathcal{F}\subset T_{X/k}\mapsto X\to\operatorname{Spec}_{X^{(1)}}(\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}))\to X^{(1)}.

4.
Power Towers on Fields

We develop a theory of power towers on fields, primarily for fields that are generic points of varieties. We define power towers. We show their basic properties. We prove the Galois-type theorem for power towers, which includes that power towers are in an explicit bijection with subalgebras of differential operators. We show how these subalgebras can be visualized. There is a way to think about them generally that leads to the right ideas for proving things about them. We prove interactions between these algebras using differentials between them - we obtain a short exact sequence. We use that development to figure out exactly which subfields are power towers. Finally, we study a specific class of power towers:
n
-foliations, where we give examples of power towers that do not correspond to any subfields.

4.1.
Basic Theory

We repeat Definition
1.6
of a power tower from the introduction and introduce a compact notation.

Definition 4.1

(Power Tower)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
A
power tower
on
K
is a sequence of subfields
W_{n}\subset K
for
n=0,1,2,\ldots
such that
W_{j}=W_{i}\cdot K^{p^{j}}
for
j\leq i
.

We denote the power tower
(W_{0},W_{1},W_{2},\ldots)
on
K
by
W_{\bullet}
.

Observation 4.2
.

If
W_{\bullet}
is a power tower on a field
K
, then
K\supset W_{n}\supset W_{n}\cdot K^{p^{n}}\supset K^{p^{n}}
, i.e., the subfield
W_{n}
is of exponent
\leq n
.

It is convenient to visualize a power tower in the form of a diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K=W_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{p^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{p^{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots.}

We have already noticed two simple examples of power towers.

Example 4.3
.

A constant tower
W_{n}\coloneqq K
is a power tower on
K
.

Example 4.4
.

A tower of
p
-powers of
K
,
W_{n}\coloneqq K^{p^{n}}
, is a power tower on
K
.

We can strengthen Observation
4.2
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
W_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
K
, then each extension
W_{i}/W_{i+1}
is of exponent at most
1
, i.e.,
W_{i+1}\supset W_{i}^{p}
, where
i\geq 0
.

Proof.

We have to prove that
W_{i+1}\supset W_{i}^{p}
. This follows from the following calculation

W_{i}^{p}={\left(W_{i+1}\cdot K^{p^{i}}\right)}^{p}=W_{i+1}^{p}\cdot K^{p^{i+1}}\subset W_{i+1}\cdot K^{p^{i+1}}=W_{i+1},

where, in the second equality, we used that taking
p
-powers commutes with taking composites.
∎

More examples come from power towers that are eventually constant.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
W_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
K
. It is of
length at most
n
if
W_{N}=W_{n}
for
N\geq n
. It is of length
n
, if the integer
n
is minimal such.

Furthermore, we say that a power tower is of
finite length
if there is an integer
n
such that the tower is of length at most
n
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
W_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
K
.

Then, for
i\geq 0
, all the inclusions
W_{i}\subset W_{i+1}
are proper inclusions, or the power tower
W_{\bullet}
is eventually constant.

If the power tower
W_{\bullet}
is eventually constant of length
n
, then the inclusions
K=W_{0}\supset W_{1}\supset W_{2}\supset\ldots\supset W_{n}
are proper, and all the inclusions
W_{n}\supset W_{n+1}\supset W_{n+2}\supset\ldots
are equalities.

Proof.

Let
0\leq i
be such that
W_{i}=W_{i+1}
. We claim that this implies that for
i<k
we have
W_{k}=W_{i}
, i.e., the power tower is of length
\leq i
.
Indeed, we can prove it by induction.

It is true for
k=i+1
, because
W_{i}=W_{i+1}
. Now, we prove that if
W_{k}=W_{k+1}
for
k\geq i
, then
W_{k+2}=W_{k+1}=W_{k}
. Indeed, we have
W_{k+1}=W_{k}\supset K^{p^{k}}
. Thus, by Lemma
4.5
, we have
W_{k+2}\supset W_{k+1}^{p}\supset W_{k}^{p}\supset\left(K^{p^{k}}\right)^{p}=K^{p^{k+1}}
. Consequently,
W_{k+1}=W_{k+2}\cdot K^{p^{k+1}}=W_{k+2}
. This proves that for
i<k
we have
W_{k}=W_{i}
.

Consequently, if
n\geq 0
is the minimal such that
W_{n}=W_{n+1}
, then
W_{\bullet}
is of length
n
, because if it was of length
<n
, then
n
would not be minimal.
∎

Lemma 4.8

(Purely Inseparable Subfields of Finite Exponents are Power Towers of Finite Length)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
n
be a positive integer. Then, there is an explicit bijection between power towers on
K
of length
n
, and subfields of
K
of exponent
n
.

Explicitly, this correspondence is given by the operation
W_{\bullet}\mapsto W_{n}
.

Proof.

We prove that the map
W_{\bullet}\mapsto W_{n}
is injective by proving that any power tower
W_{\bullet}
of length
n
is determined by
W_{n}
, which is of exponent
n
. Indeed, if
i<n
, then
W_{i}=W_{n}\cdot K^{p^{i}}
by Definition
4.1
, and if
i>n
, then
W_{i}=W_{n}
by Definition
4.6
. And, the subfield
W_{n}
is of exponent
n
, because
W_{n}\supset K^{p^{n}}
by Observation
4.2
, and, by Lemma
4.7
, we have
W_{n-1}\neq W_{n}
, so
W_{n}\not\supset K^{p^{n-1}}
, i.e., it is not of exponent
\leq n-1
.

The map is surjective, because the map
W\mapsto W_{\bullet}\coloneqq(W_{i}\coloneqq W\cdot K^{p^{i}})_{i\geq 0}
from subfields of exponent
n
is its left inverse.

This proves that the map
W_{\bullet}\mapsto W_{n}
is a bijection.
∎

By Lemma
4.7
, the inclusions of subfields in a power tower are proper till they are equalities. We can compute the dimensions between these subfields - under mild assumptions on
K
.

Proposition 4.9

(Nonincreasing Degrees)
.

Let
K
be a field of positive characteristic
p>0
. Let
\operatorname{dim}_{K^{p}}(K)
be finite.
Let
W_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
K
.

Then, the power tower
W_{\bullet}
satisfies the following sequence of inequalities:

\operatorname{dim}_{W_{1}}(K)\geq\operatorname{dim}_{W_{2}}(W_{1})\geq\ldots\geq\operatorname{dim}_{W_{n}}(W_{n-1})\geq\ldots\geq 0.

Proof.

First, we note that all these dimensions are finite, because all dimensions
\operatorname{dim}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\operatorname{dim}_{K^{p^{i+1}}}(K^{i})=\operatorname{dim}_{K^{p}}(K)^{n}
are finite. Therefore, the dimension
\operatorname{dim}_{W_{n}}(W_{n-1})
is finite since we have
K\supset W_{n-1}\supset W_{n}\supset K^{p^{n}}
.

Now, let
n\geq 0
. We are going to show that
\operatorname{dim}_{W_{n+1}}(W_{n})\geq\operatorname{dim}_{W_{n+2}}(W_{n+1})
by writing some generators of these extensions explicitly.

Let
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}\subset K^{p^{n+1}}
be a minimal subset such that
W_{n+1}=W_{n+2}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
. We have that
x_{i}^{p}\in W_{n+2}
for
i=1,\ldots,r
, because, by Lemma
4.5
, the extension
W_{n+1}/W_{n+2}
is of exponent at most
1
. Therefore, this set is a
p
-basis for
W_{n+1}/W_{n+2}
, Definition
2.15
.
Now, we claim that

\operatorname{dim}_{W_{n+2}(x_{i})}(W_{n+2}(x_{i}^{1/p}))=\operatorname{dim}_{W_{n+2}}(W_{n+1}).

Indeed, if not, then there is a nontrivial
(W_{n+2}(x_{i}))
-linear relation
F
between monomials
\prod_{i=1}^{r}{\left(x_{i}^{1/p}\right)}^{a_{i}}
, where
0\leq a_{i}\leq p-1
, but then
F^{p}
is a
W_{n+2}
-linear relation for monomials
\prod_{i=1}^{r}{\left(x_{i}\right)}^{a_{i}}
, because
W_{n+2}(x_{i})^{p}\subset W_{n+2}
. This means that the set
\{x_{i}\}
is not a
p
-basis. This is a contradiction. Thus, the equality of dimensions holds.

Next, we have a series of extensions

W_{n+2}\subset W_{n+1}=W_{n+2}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\subset W_{n+1}(x_{1}^{1/p},\ldots,x_{r}^{1/p})\subset W_{n}

that gives us

\displaystyle\operatorname{dim}_{W_{n+1}}(W_{n})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{dim}_{W_{n+1}(x_{i}^{1/p})}(W_{n})\cdot\operatorname{dim}_{W_{n+1}}(W_{n+1}(x_{i}^{1/p}))

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{dim}_{W_{n+1}}(W_{n+1}(x_{i}^{1/p}))=\operatorname{dim}_{W_{n+2}}(W_{n+1}).

This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

Proposition
4.9
can be applied to generic points of varieties over perfect fields. Namely: Let
K
be a field of positive characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be its perfection. If
K/k
is a finitely generated field extension, then
\operatorname{dim}_{K^{p}}(K)
is finite.

We finish this section with some equivalent conditions for being a power tower.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
K=W_{0}\supset W_{1}\supset W_{2}\supset\ldots
be a sequence of subfields. The following statements are equivalent.

(1)

(W_{0},W_{1},W_{2},\ldots)
is a power tower on
K
.

(2)

For every
n\geq 1
, we have
W_{n}=W_{n+1}\cdot K^{p^{n}}
.

(3)

For every
n\geq 1
, we have
W_{n}=W_{n+1}\cdot W_{n-1}^{p}
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by proving four implications.

(1.\Longrightarrow 2.)
The condition
W_{n}=W_{n+1}\cdot K^{p^{n}}
is a part of the definition of a power tower, Definition
4.1
.

(2.\Longrightarrow 1.)
Let
i<j
, then

W_{i}=W_{i+1}\cdot K^{p^{i}}=W_{i+2}\cdot K^{p^{i+1}}\cdot K^{p^{i}}=W_{i+2}\cdot K^{p^{i}}=\ldots=W_{j}\cdot K^{p^{i}}.

For
i=j
, we have
W_{i}=W_{i+1}\cdot K^{p^{i}}\supset K^{p^{i}}
, so
W_{i}=W_{i}\cdot K^{p^{i}}
.

(2.\Longrightarrow 3.)
We have

W_{n}\supset W_{n+1}\cdot W_{n-1}^{p}\supset W_{n+1}\cdot K^{p^{n}}=W_{n},

where we used
W_{n-1}=W_{n}\cdot K^{p^{n-1}}\supset K^{p^{n-1}}
. So,
W_{n}=W_{n+1}\cdot W_{n-1}^{p}
.

(3.\Longrightarrow 2.)
For
n=1
, we have
W_{1}=W_{2}\cdot W_{0}^{p}=W_{2}\cdot K^{p}
. Now, let assume that
n\geq 2
and that we already know
W_{n-1}=W_{n}\cdot K^{p^{n-1}}
. This gives

W_{n}=W_{n+1}\cdot W_{n-1}^{p}=W_{n+1}\cdot\left(W_{n}\cdot K^{p^{n-1}}\right)^{p}=W_{n+1}\cdot W_{n}^{p}\cdot K^{p^{n}}.

But
W_{n+1}=W_{n+2}\cdot W_{n}^{p}\supset W_{n}^{p}
, so
W_{n+1}\cdot W_{n}^{p}\cdot K^{p^{n}}=W_{n+1}\cdot K^{p^{n}}
. Therefore
W_{n}=W_{n+1}\cdot K^{p^{n}}
.
∎

4.2.
Galois-Type Correspondence for Power Towers

The fundamental theorem of Galois theory says that any algebraic separable subfield, a field theoretic datum, is equivalent to a subgroup of a Galois group, a group theoretic datum. By a Galois-type correspondence, I mean a bijection that takes field data and outputs something else: rings, groups, algebras, etc. Something that is usually more computable than abstract fields.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Power towers on
K
are a field-theoretic datum.
Here, we prove that two more notions are equivalent to it. Both of them are of an infinitesimal nature. The first is a subalgebra of differential operators, Section
2.4
.
The second is a sequence of
p
-Lie algebras satisfying a particular compatibility condition. And, a
p
-Lie algebra is a set of vector fields/derivations, Definition
3.7
.

First, we write down all required definitions.

Definition 4.12

(Jacobson sequence)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be its perfection. We assume that
K/k
is a finitely generated field extension.

A
Jacobson sequence
on
K
is a sequence of
p
-Lie algebras
(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2},\ldots)
such that

•

\mathcal{F}_{1}
is a
p
-Lie algebra on
K
,

•

for
i\geq 1
,
\mathcal{F}_{i+1}
is a
p
-Lie algebra on
\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{i})
,

•

for
i\geq 1
, these
p
-Lie algebras satisfy

\mathcal{F}_{i+1}\cap T_{\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{i})/\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{i-1})^{p}}=0,

where we put
\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\coloneqq K
.

We denote the Jacobson sequence
(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2},\ldots)
by
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
.

Moreover, we say that a Jacobson sequence
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
is of length at most
n
if
\mathcal{F}_{N}=0
for
N>n
. And, we say it is of length
n
if this integer
n
is minimal.

Remark 4.13
.

I named the above notion “
Jacobson sequence
” after Nathan Jacobson to celebrate his contributions to the theory of
p
-Lie algebras. This notion arises from iteratively applying his correspondence to a power tower.

Alternatively, we could call it a “
sequence of floors
”. Then we would call the
p
-Lie algebra
\mathcal{F}_{1}
the “first floor”,
\mathcal{F}_{2}
the “second floor”, and so on. The letter
\mathcal{F}
could represent a
floor
. This would be compatible with the name power
tower
: a tower and its floors.

Definition 4.14
.

(
p
-Filtration of a Subalgebra)
Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be its perfection.
Let
K\subset\mathcal{D}\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
be a
K
-subalgebra of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
.

The
p
-filtration
of
\mathcal{D}
is the family of subalgebras
\mathcal{D}_{n}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}\cap\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)
for
n\geq 0
.

Moreover, a subalgebra
\mathcal{D}
is of
height at most
n
if
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{n}
. And, of height
n
, if the number
n
is minimal.

Here is the theorem.

Theorem 4.15

(Galois-Type Correspondence for Power Towers)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k\coloneqq\bigcap_{n\geq 0}K^{p^{n}}
be the perfection of
K
. Let the extension
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension.

Then, there are natural bijections between the following data on
K
:

(1)

power towers on
K
,

(2)

Jacobson sequences on
K
,

(3)

K
-subalgebras of differential operators on
K
over
k
.

Explicitly,
some of these bijections are given by the following operations.

(1)

From power towers to Jacobson sequences.
An iterated tangent space
:

(W_{0},W_{1},W_{2},\ldots)\mapsto(T_{W_{0}/W_{1}},T_{W_{1}/W_{2}},T_{W_{2}/W_{3}},\ldots),

(2)

From Jacobson sequences to power towers.
An iterated annihilator
:

(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathcal{F}_{3},\ldots)\mapsto(K,\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{1}),\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{2}),\ldots),

(3)

From power towers to subalgebras.
An algebra of differential operators relative to a power tower
:

W_{\bullet}=(W_{0},W_{1},W_{2},\ldots)\mapsto\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{\bullet}}(K)\coloneqq\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{i}}(K),

(4)

From subalgebras to power towers.
Fields of constants of the
p
-filtration
:

\mathcal{D}\mapsto(K=\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{D}_{0}),\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{D}_{1}),\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{D}_{2}),\ldots).

Proof.

This proof carefully shows that all the operations mentioned in the theorem are well defined and bijective. There are no fireworks.

(1.
\Longleftrightarrow
2.)
For convenience, we recall Jacobson Correspondence
3.9
. It says that a subfield
K\supset W\supset K^{p}
corresponds to a
p
-Lie algebra
T_{K/W}=\operatorname{Der}_{W}(K)
and
\operatorname{Ann}(T_{K/W})=W
.

First, we prove that the operations 1. and 2. are well defined. Then, they are inverse to each other.

We prove that taking an iterated tangent space, the operation 1., is well defined. Let
W_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
K
. Then we can compute a sequence of
p
-Lie algebras

(T_{W_{0}/W_{1}},T_{W_{1}/W_{2}},T_{W_{2}/W_{3}},\ldots)
.
By Jacobson Correspondence,
T_{W_{0}/W_{1}}
is a
p
-Lie algebra on
W_{0}=K
, and

T_{W_{n}/W_{n+1}}
is a
p
-Lie algebra on
W_{n}=\operatorname{Ann}(T_{W_{n-1}/W_{n}})
for
n\geq 1
.
Therefore, in order to show that this sequence is a Jacobson sequence, it is sufficient to show that for
n\geq 1
we have

T_{W_{n}/W_{n+1}}\cap T_{W_{n}/W_{n-1}^{p}}=0.

We prove the above here. Any intersection of
p
-Lie subalgebras of a
p
-Lie algebra is a
p
-Lie subalgebra of that
p
-Lie algebra, so

T_{W_{n}/W_{n+1}}\cap T_{W_{n}/W_{n-1}^{p}}\subset T_{W_{n}/W_{n}^{p}}
is a
p
-Lie algebra on
W_{n}
. By Jacobson Correspondence, it equals
T_{W_{n}/H}
, where
W_{n}\supset H\supset W_{n}^{p}
is a subfield. By the construction, this subfield contains both
W_{n+1}
and
W_{n-1}^{p}
, so it contains their composite
W_{n+1}\cdot W_{n-1}^{p}
that is equal to
W_{n}
by Lemma
4.11
. Therefore,
H=W_{n}
, so

T_{W_{n}/W_{n+1}}\cap T_{W_{n}/W_{n-1}^{p}}=T_{W_{n}/H}=T_{W_{n}/W_{n}}=0.

This proves that taking an iterated relative tangent space is a well-defined operation.

We prove that taking an iterated annihilator, the operation 2., is well defined. Let
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
be a Jacobson sequence on
K
. Then we can compute a sequence of subfields of
K

K\supset\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{1})\supset\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{2})\supset\ldots.

To show that this sequence is a power tower on
K
, by Lemma
4.11
, it is enough to show that for every
n\geq 1
we have

\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n+1})\cdot\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n-1})^{p}.

This follows immediately from the definition of a Jacobson sequence, Definition
4.12
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle 0=\mathcal{F}_{n+1}\cap T_{\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n})/\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n-1})^{p}}

\displaystyle=T_{\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n})/\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n+1})}\cap T_{\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n})/\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n-1})^{p}}

\displaystyle=T_{\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n})/\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n+1})\cdot\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n-1})^{p}}.

Now, since the subfield
\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n+1})\cdot\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n-1})^{p}\subset\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n})
is of exponent at most
1
, we conclude the equality
\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n+1})\cdot\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n-1})^{p}=\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{n})
from Jacobson Correspondence. This proves that taking an iterated annihilator is a well-defined operation.

Finally, we know these operations 1 and 2 are well defined. Now, we prove they are inverse to each other. Indeed, by iteratively applying Jacobson Correspondence, we get that
W_{1}
corresponds to
\mathcal{F}_{1}
,
W_{2}
corresponds to
\mathcal{F}_{2}
, and so on. Therefore, these operations are bijective.

(1.
\Longleftrightarrow
3.) For convenience, we recall the discrete Jacobson–Bourbaki Correspondence
3.3
. It says that a subfield
K\supset L
with a finite degree
[K:L]
corresponds to a finite algebra of linear endomorphisms
\operatorname{End}_{L}(K)
and
\operatorname{const}(\operatorname{End}_{L}(K))=L
. Moreover, if
L
is a purely inseparable subfield of
K
, then it is of finite exponent by Lemma
2.10
. In particular, it is a finite extension. Therefore, we have
\operatorname{Diff}_{L}(K)=\operatorname{End}_{L}(K)
, by Lemma(Diff=End)
2.72
. Hence, there is a correspondence between purely inseparable subfields and finite subalgebras of differential operators given by the Jacobson–Bourbaki correspondence operations.

First, we prove that operations 3 and 4 are well-defined and inverse to each other.

We prove that taking an algebra of differential operators relative to a power tower, the operation 3., is well defined.
Let
W_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
K
. We can compute the subalgebras
\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{i}}(K)\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
of differential operators commuting with
W_{i}
. Since we have inclusions
W_{i}\supset W_{i+1}
, therefore we have inclusions
\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{i}}(K)\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{i+1}}(K)
. This means that the sum
\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{i}}(K)
is an ascending sum of inclusions of subalgebras of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
, and hence it is a subalgebra of differential operators.

We prove that taking fields of constants of the
p
-filtration, the operation 4., is well defined. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a
K
-subalgebra (with
1
) of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
. By Definition
4.14
, the
p
-filtration of
\mathcal{D}
is the sequences of subalgebras
\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathcal{D}\cap\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{i}}}(K)
. From it, we can compute a sequence of subfields
W_{i}=\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{D}_{i})
. And, from the inclusions
\mathcal{D}_{i}\subset\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
, we get
W_{i}\supset W_{i+1}
. Now, we show that this sequence
(W_{0},W_{1},W_{2},\ldots)
is a power tower on
K
.
The algebra
\mathcal{D}_{i}
is a finitely dimensional subalgebra of linear endomorphisms, so, by Jacobson–Bourbaki Correspondence, it is equal to
\operatorname{End}_{W_{i}}(K)
. And, by the definition, for
j\leq i
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{i}\cap\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{j}}}(K)=\mathcal{D}_{j}
, i.e.

\operatorname{End}_{W_{i}}(K)\cap\operatorname{End}_{K^{p^{j}}}(K)=\operatorname{End}_{W_{j}}(K).

The left hand side equals
\operatorname{End}_{W_{i}\cdot K^{p^{j}}}(K)
, therefore
W_{j}=W_{i}\cdot K^{p^{j}}
by Jacobson–Bourbaki Correspondence. So, the sequence is a power tower on
K
.

Finally, by Jacobson–Bourbaki Correspondence, the operations 3. and 4. are inverse to each other, because
W_{1}
corresponds to
\mathcal{D}_{1}
,

W_{2}
corresponds to
\mathcal{D}_{2}
, and so on. Therefore, these operations are bijective.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

The assumption “Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k\coloneqq\bigcap_{n\geq 0}K^{p^{n}}
be the perfection of
K
. Let the extension
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension.” could be replaced with “Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
such that
[K:K^{p}]<\infty
.” No changes are required to the above proof. We only need to be able to access Jacobson–Bourbaki Correspondence and End=Diff Lemma, because Jacobson Correspondence follows from Jacobson–Bourbaki Correspondence.

We frequently use the former assumption, here and there, because we will apply the theorem only to that case.

One of the remaining bijections is the one from subalgebras to Jacobson sequences.
We call this operation “unpacking”: for a power tower, given in the form of a subalgebra, we get a sequence of floors, its Jacobson sequence. Later, we will discuss Remark
4.25
’s non-explicit “inverse” to unpacking that we call “packing”.

Theorem 4.17

(Unpacking)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k\coloneqq\bigcap_{n\geq 0}K^{p^{n}}
be the perfection of
K
. Let the extension
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension.
Let
\mathcal{D}
be a subalgebra of differential operators on
K
over
k
corresponding to a power tower
W_{\bullet}
, and to a Jacobson sequence
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
.

Then, we have the following equalities that we call
an
unpacking
of the subalgebra
\mathcal{D}
into the Jacobson sequence
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\cap T_{K/K^{p}}

\displaystyle=T_{K/W_{1}}=\mathcal{F}_{1},

\displaystyle d(K/W_{1})(\mathcal{D})\cap T_{W_{1}/W_{1}^{p}}

\displaystyle=T_{W_{1}/W_{2}}=\mathcal{F}_{2},

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle=\ldots,

\displaystyle d(K/W_{n})(\mathcal{D})\cap T_{W_{n}/W_{n}^{p}}

\displaystyle=T_{W_{n}/W_{n+1}}=\mathcal{F}_{n+1},

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle=\ldots,

where
d(K/W_{i}):\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\to\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W_{i})\otimes K
are differentials between algebras of differential operators, Definition
2.56
.

Proof.

First, we prove the first equality from the unpacking of
\mathcal{D}
into a Jacobson sequence:
\mathcal{D}\cap T_{K/K^{p}}=T_{K/W_{1}}
. It is proved by the following calculation.

\mathcal{D}\cap T_{K/K^{p}}=\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{i}}(K)\cap T_{K/K^{p}}=\bigcup_{i\geq 0}T_{K/K^{p}\cdot W_{i}}=\bigcup_{i\geq 1}T_{K/W_{1}}=T_{K/W_{1}}.

Now, we prove the equalities
d(K/W_{n})(\mathcal{D})\cap T_{W_{n}/W_{n}^{p}}=T_{W_{n}/W_{n+1}}
for
n\geq 1
.

First, we explain that the left-hand side makes sense. By Corollary
2.57
, for every field extension
K/W/k
, we have a differential between their algebras of differential operators

d(K/W):\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\to\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)\otimes K
, Definition
2.56
, which is a restriction of a map
K\to K
to a map
W\to K
, Lemma
2.58
. The algebra
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)
contains
T_{W/W^{p}}
in its first degree, and the map
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)\to\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)\otimes K
is an inclusion. Consequently, we can intersect the image of
\mathcal{D}
with the
p
-Lie algebra
T_{W/W^{p}}
.

We proceed to the proof. Let
i>n
. We have

d(K/W_{n})(\mathcal{D}_{i})=d(K/W_{n})(\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{i}}(K))=d(K/W_{n})(\operatorname{End}_{W_{i}}(K))=\operatorname{Hom}_{W_{i}}(W_{n},K),

where we used Lemma(Diff=End)
2.72
.
At the same time, we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{W_{i}}(W_{n},K)=\operatorname{Hom}_{W_{i}}(W_{n},W_{n})\otimes K=\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{i}}(W_{n})\otimes K.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{i}}(W_{n})\otimes K\cap T_{W_{n}/W_{n}^{p}}=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{i}}(W_{n})\cap T_{W_{n}/W_{n}^{p}}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{W_{n}/W_{i}}=T_{W_{n}/W_{i}\cdot W_{n}^{p}}=T_{W_{n}/W_{n+1}}.

This result does not depend on
i
and therefore it holds for
\mathcal{D}
, and not just for
\mathcal{D}_{i}
, because we have
\mathcal{D}=\bigcup_{i>n}\mathcal{D}_{i}
. This finishes the proof of the unpacking.
∎

4.3.
Distinguished Generators of Subalgebras

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension, where
k
is the perfection of
K
. Then,
K/k
admits coordinates, Definition
2.41
. Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
be a set of coordinates for
K/k
. Then any differential operator on
K
relative to
k
can be described in terms of the differential operators
\frac{1}{n!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial x_{i}^{n}}
for
n\geq 0
,
1\leq i\leq m
, Corollary
2.50
. It is not a canonical description; it depends on the coordinates. In this section, we will show that any subalgebra of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
admits analogous generators to the symbols
\frac{1}{n!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial x_{i}^{n}}
.

Definition 4.18
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension, where
k
is the perfection of
K
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a subalgebra of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
. Let
W_{\bullet}
be a power tower that corresponds to
\mathcal{D}
. Let
p^{r_{i}}=[W_{i}:W_{i-1}]
for
i>0
.

We define a set of
distinguished generators
of
\mathcal{D}
to be a subset

G=\{G^{m}_{1},\ldots,G^{m}_{r_{m}}\}_{m=1,2,\ldots}\subset\mathcal{D}

such that

(1)

Every operator
G^{m}_{i}
for
m>0
,
1\leq i\leq r_{m}
is of order
p^{m-1}
.

(2)

The leading form
[G^{m}_{i}]
for
m>1,1\leq i\leq r_{m}
is a
K
-linear sum of leading forms
\gamma_{p^{m-1}}([G^{1}_{j}])
for
1\leq j\leq r_{1}
.

(3)

The graded subset
[G]=\{[D]:D\in G\}\subset\operatorname{gr}(\mathcal{D})
is
K
-linearly independent.

Moreover, we say that the set of distinguished generators
G
is
well-chosen
if
d(K/W_{m-1})(G^{m}_{i})
belongs to
\mathcal{F}_{m}
for every
i
, where
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
is the Jacobson sequence of
W_{\bullet}
.

Remark 4.19
.

The second condition in Definition
4.18
may be changed to an equivalent, though a priori stronger, condition:

“2’. The leading form
[G^{m}_{i}]
for
m>1,1\leq i\leq r_{m}
is

a
K
-linear sum of leading forms
\gamma_{p}([G^{m-1}_{j}])
for
1\leq j\leq r_{m-1}
.”.

This follows from the methods from the proof of Theorem
5.33
, more specifically, the diagram there proves that.

Definition 4.20
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension, where
k
is the perfection of
K
. Let
p^{n}=[K:K^{p}]
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a subalgebra of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
. Let
G=\{G^{m}_{1},\ldots,G^{m}_{r_{m}}\}_{m=1,2,\ldots}
be a set of distinguished generators of
\mathcal{D}
.

We define a
completion
of
G
to be a subset

E=\{E^{m}_{1},\ldots,E^{m}_{n-r_{m}}\}_{m=1,2,\ldots}\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)

such that

•

Every operator
E^{m}_{i}
for
m>0
,
1\leq i\leq n-r_{m}
is of order
p^{m-1}
.

•

The set
\{[G^{m}_{1}],\ldots,[G^{m}_{r_{m}}],[E^{m}_{1}],\ldots,[E^{m}_{n-r_{m}}]\}
for
m>0
is a
K
-basis for the
K
-vector space spanned by
\gamma_{p^{m-1}}([T_{K/K^{p}}])
inside
\operatorname{gr}(\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K))
.

Example 4.21
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension, where
k
is the perfection of
K
. Let
p^{n}=[K:K^{p}]
.

Let
K\supset W\supset k
be a subfield such that
K/W
is a separable extension. Then, by Theorem
2.14
, all extensions
K/W
,
K/k
,
W/k
are separable. Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
be a separable transcendental basis for
W/k
. Let
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-r}
be a separable transcendental basis for
K/W
. Then the set
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-r}
is a separable transcendental basis for
K/k
. With respect to this basis, the set
G
:

\left\{\frac{1}{p^{m}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{m}}}{\partial x_{1}^{p^{m}}},\ldots,\frac{1}{p^{m}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{m}}}{\partial x_{r}^{p^{m}}}\right\}_{m=1,2,\ldots}

is a set of (well-chosen) distinguished generators for
\operatorname{Diff}_{W}(K)
, and the set
E
:

\left\{\frac{1}{p^{m}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{m}}}{\partial y_{1}^{p^{m}}},\ldots,\frac{1}{p^{m}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{m}}}{\partial y_{n-r}^{p^{m}}}\right\}_{m=1,2,\ldots}

is a completion of this distinguished basis. Moreover, what is not part of the definition, and it is a special property of this example, is that all these operators commute with each other.

It is important to emphasize that the sets
G
and
E
are not canonical and they heavily depend on the choice of the basis
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-r}
.

Lemma 4.22

(Graded Differential and Graded Kernels of Differentials)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension, where
k
is the perfection of
K
.
Let
\mathcal{D}
be a subalgebra of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
. Let
W_{\bullet}
be its power tower. Let
m\geq 1
.

Then the following are true:

(1)

The differential
d(K/K^{p^{m}})
induces a
graded differential

\operatorname{gr}d(K/K^{p^{m}})
:

\operatorname{gr}d(K/K^{p^{m}}):\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\to\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K^{p^{m}})\otimes K.

Explicitly, if
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are coordinates for
K/k
, then the graded differential is determined by, see Example
2.59
:

\operatorname{gr}d(K/K^{p^{m}}):\left[\frac{1}{p^{M}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{M}}}{\partial x_{i}^{p^{M}}}\right]\mapsto\left[\frac{1}{p^{M-m}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{M-m}}}{\partial x_{i}^{p^{M-m}}}\right]

for
i=1,2,\ldots,n
,
M\geq 0
. If
M-m<0
, then we put the value zero.

In terms of Proposition
2.75
, it is simply dividing by the ideal
I=(\gamma_{j}(y_{i}))_{0<j<p^{m},i=1,\ldots,n}
of
K\left<y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\right>
, so, up to that isomorphism, we have:

\operatorname{gr}d(K/K^{p^{m}}):K\left<y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\right>\to K\left<y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\right>/I.

(2)

We have
\operatorname{ker}d(K/K^{p^{m}})\cap\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{ker}d(K/W_{m})\cap\mathcal{D}
.

(3)

We have
\operatorname{gr}\left(\operatorname{ker}d(K/W_{m})\cap\mathcal{D}\right)=\operatorname{ker}\left(\operatorname{gr}d(K/K^{p^{m}})\right)\cap\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}
.

Proof.

The first item is so detailed that it proves itself.

The second item. We will show it for
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{m+i}
, where
i\geq 1
. it is enough, because
\mathcal{D}
is a sum of its
p
-filtration,
\mathcal{D}=\bigcup_{N>m}\mathcal{D}_{m}
.

We observe that
d(W_{m}/K^{p^{m}})
is injective on
\bigcup_{N>m}\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{N}}(W_{m})
, because every differential is a restriction, Lemma
2.58
, and we do not add any more commutativity. It is also true after tensoring with
K
. Since
d(K/K^{p^{m}})=d(W_{m}/K^{p^{m}})\otimes K\circ d(K/W_{m})
, we conclude that the kernels of
d(K/K^{p^{m}})
and
d(K/W_{m})
intersected with
\mathcal{D}_{m+i}
are equal, i.e.,

\operatorname{ker}d(K/K^{p^{m-1}})\cap\mathcal{D}_{m+i}=\operatorname{ker}d(K/W_{m-1})\cap\mathcal{D}_{m+i}.

This proves the second item by taking a union over
i\geq 1
.

The third item. Again, we work with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{m+i}
,
i\geq 1
. We have a sequence; the first three arrows are exact:

0\to\mathcal{D}_{m+i}\cap\operatorname{ker}(d(K/K^{p^{m}}))\to\mathcal{D}_{m+i}\to d(K/K^{p^{m}})(\mathcal{D}_{m+i})\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K^{p^{m}})\otimes K.

From the first item, we have that
\operatorname{gr}d(K/K^{p^{m-1}})
is well defined, and it fits the following short exact sequence:

0\to T\to\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}_{m}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{gr}d(K/K^{p^{m-1}})}\operatorname{gr}(d(K/K^{p^{m-1}})(\mathcal{D}_{m}))\to 0,

where
T=\operatorname{ker}(\operatorname{gr}d(K/K^{p^{m-1}}))\cap\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}_{m}
.

An elementary fact is that if
V
is a vector subspace of a graded vector space
W
, then the dimension of
\operatorname{gr}(V)
equals the dimension of
V
.
Thus, we have that the dimension of
\operatorname{gr}(d(K/K^{p^{m}})(\mathcal{D}_{m+i}))
is the dimension of
d(K/K^{p^{m}})(\mathcal{D}_{m+i})
, and therefore the dimension of
T
is the dimension of
\mathcal{D}_{m+i}\cap\operatorname{ker}(d(K/K^{p^{m}}))
.
Finally, we have
\operatorname{gr}(\mathcal{D}_{m}\cap\operatorname{ker}(d(K/K^{p^{m-1}})))\subset T
, so they are equal. This proves the third item.
∎

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension, where
k
is the perfection of
K
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a subalgebra of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
.

Then, a set of well-chosen distinguished generators for
\mathcal{D}
exists, and every such set admits a completion.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.15
,
\mathcal{D}
corresponds to a power tower
W_{\bullet}
and to a Jacobson sequence
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
.
The main goal of the proof is to show that for every
i\geq 1
we can take a basis of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
and “pack it back” to the subalgebra
\mathcal{D}
, see Theorem
4.17
. Initially, we take any preimages with respect to the unpacking, and then we correct them to satisfy our distinguished conditions.

Let
p^{r_{i}}=[W_{i}:W_{i-1}]
for
i>0
.

We begin with
m=1
. Let
G^{1}_{1},\ldots,G^{1}_{r_{1}}
be any
K
-basis of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
.

Let
m>1
. Let
G^{\prime m}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime m}_{r_{m}}
be a
W_{m-1}
-basis of
\mathcal{F}_{m}
. According to the unpacking, Theorem
4.17
, we have that

d(K/W_{m-1})(\mathcal{D})\cap T_{W_{m-1}/W_{m-1}^{p}}=d(K/W_{m-1})(\mathcal{D}_{m})\cap T_{W_{m-1}/W_{m-1}^{p}}=\mathcal{F}_{m}.

(We recall
\mathcal{D}_{m}=\mathcal{D}\cap\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{m}}}(K)
.)
Therefore, there are differential operators
G^{\prime\prime m}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime\prime m}_{r_{m}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{m}
that restrict to
G^{\prime m}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime m}_{r_{m}}
respectively, Lemma
2.58
. However, not every choice gives distinguished generators, so we must choose them carefully. We will use Lemma
4.22
.

We now work with
\mathcal{D}_{m}
, instead of the whole
\mathcal{D}
, to simplify some reasoning.

We have
G^{\prime\prime m}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime\prime m}_{r_{m}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{m}

that are operators restricting to
G^{\prime m}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime m}_{r_{m}}
.

If operators
G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{r_{m}}\in\mathcal{D}_{m}
are another such choice, then we have, for
0\leq i\leq r_{m}
,

G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{i}=G^{\prime\prime m}_{i}+B_{i},

where
B_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{m}\cap\operatorname{ker}(d(K/W_{m-1}))=\mathcal{D}_{m}\cap\operatorname{ker}(d(K/K^{p^{m-1}}))
, by Lemma
4.22
. We will finish the proof by modifying operators
B_{i}
.

If the order of
G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{i}
is higher than
p^{m-1}
, then
\operatorname{gr}d(K/K^{p^{m-1}})([G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{i}])=0
, so, by Lemma
4.22
, there is an operator
C_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
that is also from the kernel of
d(K/K^{p^{m-1}})
such that
[C_{i}]=[G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{i}]
.

We substitute
G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{i}
with
G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{i}-C_{i}
. The new
G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{i}
still satisfies the conditions, but it is of lower order. We continue these substitutions till the order of
G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{i}
becomes
p^{m-1}
. It cannot go lower, because
d(K/K^{m-1})(G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{i})\neq 0

Now, by Proposition
2.75
and Lemma
2.68
,
we have

[G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{i}]=D_{i}+E_{i}

where the leading form
D_{i}
is a linear combination of
\gamma_{p^{m-1}}([G^{1}_{j}])
, and the leading form
E_{i}
is a linear combination of compositions of
\gamma_{k}([G^{1}_{j}])
for
k<p^{m-1}
. So,
E_{i}
is killed by
d(K/K^{p^{m-1}})
. This means that there is an operator
E^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{m}\cap d(K/K^{p^{m-1}})=\mathcal{D}_{m}\cap\operatorname{ker}(d(K/W_{m-1}))
such that
[E^{\prime}_{i}]=E_{i}
, Lemma
4.22
. The final substitution for
G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{i}
is
G^{\prime\prime\prime m}_{i}-E^{\prime}_{i}
. We name the result:
G^{m}_{i}
.

Finally, the leading forms
[G^{m}_{i}]
for all admissible
m,i
are linearly independent, because for different
m
they are in various degrees. For the same order
p^{m-1}
, they stay independent after applying the linear map
d(K/W_{m-1})
, so they are independent.
This finishes the construction of well-chosen distinguished generators for
\mathcal{D}
.

Now, we prove that every set of distinguished generators admits a completion. It is trivial: just complete independent vectors
[G^{m}_{i}]
by some vector
E^{\prime m}_{j}
to a basis of the vector space spanned by
\gamma_{p^{m-1}}([T_{K/K^{p}}])
inside
\operatorname{gr}(\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K))
and take operators
E^{m}_{j}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
such that
[E^{m}_{j}]=E^{\prime m}_{j}
. The union of all these operators
E^{m}_{j}
is a completion of
\{G^{m}_{i}\}
.
∎

Here is the main point why these distinguished generators are useful.

Proposition 4.24
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension, where
k
is the perfection of
K
. Let
p^{n}=[K:K^{p}]
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a subalgebra of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
. Let
G=\{G^{m}_{1},\ldots,G^{m}_{r_{m}}\}_{m=1,2,\ldots}
be a set of distinguished generators of
\mathcal{D}
. Let
E=\{E^{m}_{1},\ldots,E^{m}_{n-r_{m}}\}_{m=1,2,\ldots}
be a completion of
G
.

Then, the set of differential operators

\left\{\left(\prod_{m=1}^{k}\prod^{r_{m}}_{i=1}\left(G^{m}_{i}\right)^{b^{m}_{i}}\right):k\geq 0,0\leq b^{m}_{i}\leq p-1\right\}

is a
K
-basis of
\mathcal{D}
.
And, the set of differential operators

\left\{\left(\prod_{m=1}^{k}\prod^{r-r_{m}}_{j=1}\left(E^{m}_{j}\right)^{a^{m}_{j}}\right)\cdot\left(\prod_{m=1}^{k}\prod^{r_{m}}_{i=1}\left(G^{m}_{i}\right)^{b^{m}_{i}}\right):k\geq 0,0\leq a^{m}_{i}\leq p-1,0\leq b^{m}_{i}\leq p-1\right\},

is a
K
-basis of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.79
, we know that
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}
is a subset of a divided power polynomial algebra. Inside this algebra, it is clear that leading forms

\left[\left(\prod_{m=1}^{k}\prod^{r_{m}}_{i=1}\left(G^{m}_{i}\right)^{b^{m}_{i}}\right)\right]

are
K
-independent, and therefore the operators

\prod_{m=1}^{k}\prod^{r_{m}}_{i=1}\left(G^{m}_{i}\right)^{b^{m}_{i}}
are
K
-independent.
Moreover, for every
n\geq 0
, we have that
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is of the dimension that is equal to the number of distinct operators
\prod_{m=1}^{k}\prod^{r_{m}}_{i=1}\left(G^{m}_{i}\right)^{b^{m}_{i}}
inside it. Consequently, those operators form a basis. This proves the first claim.

The second claim follows from the first one, because the set
G\cup E
is a set of distinguished generators for
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
and the order of compositions does not matter for the leading forms argument above, because
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
is commutative.

∎

4.3.1.
Visualization of Subalgebras

This section will provide a neat graphical way to think about any subalgebra of differential operators.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension, where
k
is the perfection of
K
. Let
p^{n}=[K:K^{p}]
.

The algebra
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
is
big and noncommutative
, but it admits many regularities, and these regularities can be turned into a semiformal way of thinking about it and its subalgebras. The main pieces are unpacking
4.17
and divided powers
2.66
. Actually, during my PhD, I had first discovered these visual representations. I had believed in them, and these pictures guided me to develop the formal theory of power towers, my PhD thesis, and now this paper. I think it is helpful to share it, though it may be initially confusing to read.

The algebra
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
has its first degree
T_{K/k}
. This first degree does not generate it, but it may determine
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
with a push from divided powers. The graded algebra is a divided power polynomial ring, and thus it is generated by all

T_{K/k}\cup\gamma_{p}\left(T_{K/k}\right)\cup\gamma_{p^{2}}\left(T_{K/k}\right)\cup\ldots.

For every
n\geq 0
, the set
\gamma_{p^{n}}\left(T_{K/k}\right)
is a set of forms of order
p^{n}
. So, we can draw
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
as an infinite rectangular, a
tower
, and we can draw
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
similarly. Still, if we do it in a logarithmic scale, in a way that
+1
higher is
p
-times higher, then we can put
\gamma_{p^{n}}\left(T_{K/k}\right)
to be arithmetically growing
floors
of that tower.

Now, let
\mathcal{D}
be a subalgebra of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
. We can observe that
this algebra looks like
stairs
inside the tower of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
. Indeed, if
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
is its Jacobson sequence, Definition
4.12
, and
W_{\bullet}
its power tower, then their dimensions are nonincreasing
4.9
. Thus, how much space
\mathcal{D}
takes inside
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
looks like
stairs
. Finite, or with the last step being infinite. This makes more sense inside
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}
, because we can “pack back”
shadows
of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
there. Indeed, let

\overline{\mathcal{F}_{i}}\coloneqq\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}\cap\gamma_{p^{i-1}}\left(T_{K/k}\right).

This is well defined and the rank of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
(over
W_{i-1}
) is the rank of
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
(over
K^{p^{i-1}}
). Indeed, let
G=\{G^{m}_{1},\ldots,G^{m}_{r_{m}}\}_{m=1,2,\ldots}
be a set of well-chosen distinguished generators, Definition
4.18
. Then
\operatorname{gr}G\cap\gamma_{p^{i-1}}\left(T_{K/k}\right)
is a basis for
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
and
d(K/W_{i-1})(G)\cap T_{W_{i-1}/W_{i-1}^{p}}
is a basis of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
. So, we do not have
\mathcal{F}_{i}
,
i\geq 2
, inside
\mathcal{D}
, but we have their
shadows
inside
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}
.

The figure below, which was created using
https://www.mathcha.io
, is a visual manifestation of the above discussion.

gr
continues
\text{Diff}_{k}(K)
gr
continues
\text{gr Diff}_{k}(K)
D
\text{gr}\ D
\gamma_{p}(T_{K/K^{p}})
\ \ \ \ \ T_{K/K^{p}}
\gamma_{p^{2}}(T_{K/K^{p}})
\gamma_{p^{3}}(T_{K/K^{p}})
\ \ \ \ \ T_{K/K^{p}}
continues
continues
F_{1}
\overline{F_{2}}
\overline{F_{3}}
\overline{F_{4}}
F_{1}

Figure 1
.
A visualization of a subalgebra
\mathcal{D}\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
for orders satisfying
n>r_{1}>r_{2}=r_{3}>r_{4}=r_{5}=\ldots>0
, where
p^{r_{i}}=[W_{i}:W_{i-1}]
for
i>0
.

Remark 4.25
.

Above, we have
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{i}}\coloneqq\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}\cap\gamma_{p^{i-1}}\left(T_{K/k}\right)
, i.e. it is canonically defined.
Moreover, we have an isomorphism that I call “packing”.

\mathcal{F}_{m}\to\overline{\mathcal{F}_{m}}\otimes W_{m-1}:\sum_{i=1}^{r_{m}}a_{i}d(K/W_{m-1})(G^{m}_{i})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{r_{m}}a_{i}[G^{m}_{i}],

where
a_{i}\in W_{m-1}
. It does not depend on the chosen distinguished generators’ choice
G
. This can be checked using Lemma
4.22
.

The vector spaces
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{m}}
look great, but they know less than
\mathcal{F}_{m}
. This is because different subalgebras can have the same graded subalgebras. In particular, we will see later that all
n
-foliations with the same
W_{1}
have the same graded subalgebras, Proposition
4.44
.

However, we cannot put
\mathcal{F}_{m}
directly into
\mathcal{D}
. The best thing is to choose
G
and to take vector spaces generated by its subsets with fixed top index, but this is not canonical. Therefore, I claim no “canonical formula for computing
\mathcal{D}
from
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
” exists. This means I cannot really compute any good generators of
\mathcal{D}
from all
\mathcal{F}_{m}
. At least I do not know how to do it, but if there were such a way, then there would be a formula for how to get a set of distinguished generators
G
from bases of all
\mathcal{F}_{m}
, but the differentials
d(K/W_{m-1})
are generally not injective… So, why one choice over another? Sometimes, like in Sweedler’s modular theory, a group makes the choice, but generally, there is nothing.

4.4.
Short Exact Sequence for Differential between Algebras of Differential Operators

We use the differential between algebras of differential operators to unpack
4.17
any subalgebra into a Jacobson sequence. Here is more information about that function - we show it is always surjective and we describe the kernel. This should be compared with a differential for derivations, which is false. Indeed, the differential
d(K/K^{p}):T_{K/k}\to T_{K^{p}/k}\otimes K
is zero, because derivations kill
p
-powers. Consequently, the surjectivity follows from interactions between different orders of differential operators.

Theorem 4.26

(SES for Diff)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be the perfection of
K
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension.
Let
K\supset W\supset k
be a subfield.

Then,
we have a short exact sequence of
K
-vector spaces:

0\to\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\circ{\swabfamily E}(K/W)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\xrightarrow{d(K/W)}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)\otimes K\to 0,

where
{\swabfamily E}(K/W)\coloneqq\{D\in\operatorname{Diff}_{W}(K)\ :\ D(1)=0\}
, and
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\circ{\swabfamily E}(K/W)
is a left-ideal of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
that is spanned as a
K
-vector space by
D_{1}\circ D_{2}
for
D_{1}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
and
D_{2}\in{\swabfamily E}(K/W)
.

Proof.

This proof consists of two parts. In the first part, we prove the surjectivity of
d(K/W)
. In the second part, we provide a description of the kernel.

Part 1: Surjectivity
The first part of the proof consists of three steps. First, we prove the surjectivity for separable extensions
K/W
. Second, we prove surjectivity for purely inseparable extensions
K/W
. Third, we combine the previous two steps to prove it in full generality.

Part 1, Step 1: Surjectivity For Separable Extensions

We prove that if the field extension
K/W
is separable, then the differential
d(K/W)
is surjective.

By the assumption, the extension
K/W
is separable, i.e., it admits a separable transcendence basis
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
. And, the extension
W/k
admits a separable transcendence basis
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}
by Theorem
2.17
. Therefore, we can conclude that
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
is a separable transcendence basis for the extension
K/k
.

We use the bases
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}
to compute
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
and
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)
respectively, Proposition
2.48
. In particular, we know that every differential operator on
W
over
k
is uniquely determined by its values on monomials in
y_{i}
’s, and every differential operator on
K
over
k
is determined by its values on monomials in
y_{i}
’s and
x_{j}
’s. Moreover, every choice of these values defines a unique differential operator.

We prove the surjectivity. First, we observe that it is enough to show that for every
\varphi\in\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)
, there is a
\psi\in\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
such that
d(K/W)(\psi)=\varphi
, because
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)\otimes K
is spanned by
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)
. Now, let
\varphi\in\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)
. We define
\psi\in\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
by having the same values as
\varphi
on monomials in
y_{i}
’s and by being zero on monomials in
x_{i}
’s and
y_{i}
’s, including some
x_{i}
’s. Consequently, we have
d(K/W)(\psi)=\varphi
.

This finishes the proof of the surjectivity for separable extensions.

Part 1, Step 2: Surjectivity For Purely Inseparable Extensions

We prove that if the field extension
K/W
is purely inseparable, then
d(K/W)
is surjective.

The extension
K/W
is of finite exponent by Lemma
2.10
, i.e., there is an integer
n
such that
W\supset K^{p^{n}}
. This implies that the
p
-filtration of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
partially descends to
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n+1}}}(K)\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n+2}}}(K)\subset\ldots\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(W)\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n+1}}}(W)\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n+2}}}(W)\subset\ldots\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W).

Moreover, we can show that
d(K/W)
is compatible with these filtrations. Indeed, being compatible means that the subalgebra
\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n+i}}}(K)
is mapped to
\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n+i}}}(W)\otimes K
. This holds, because the differential
d(K/W)
is a restriction from
K
to
W
, Lemma
2.58
, so it maps
K^{p^{n+i}}
-linear maps to
K^{p^{n+i}}
-linear maps.

Next, we prove that every map
\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n+i}}}(K)\to\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n+i}}}(W)\otimes K
is surjective for
i\geq 0
.
First, we observe that this map is the natural map

\operatorname{Hom}_{K^{p^{n+i}}}(K,K)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{K^{p^{n+i}}}(W,K)
,
by Proposition(Diff=End)
2.72
. Now, let
\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{K^{p^{n+i}}}(W,K)
. We have that
W\subset K
is a
K^{p^{n+i}}
-linear subspace, so there exists a complementary vector space
V
such that
W\oplus V=K
. Let
\psi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{K^{p^{n+i}}}(K,K)
be a linear operator that is equal
\varphi
on
W
and
0
on
V
, then the restriction of
\psi
to
W
is equal
\varphi
. Therefore, this map is surjective.

Finally, the map
d(K/W)
is surjective, because we have an equality

\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n+i}}}(W)\otimes K=\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)\otimes K.

This finishes the proof of the surjectivity for purely inseparable extensions.

Part 1, Step 3: Surjectivity In General

Let
K/W/k
be an arbitrary field extension. We prove that
d(K/W)
is surjective.

We can decompose the extension
K/W
into two extensions
K/W^{\prime}/W
, where the extension
K/W^{\prime}
is purely inseparable and
W^{\prime}/W
is separable. Indeed, let
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
be a transcendental basis for
K/W
. (This basis is finite, because
K/W
is finitely generated.) This means that the extension

K/W(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is finite and therefore algebraic.
Let
W^{\prime}
be the separable algebraic closure of
W(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
in
K
. Then, the extension
K/W^{\prime}
is purely inseparable, and the extension
W^{\prime}/W
is separable. (Caveat: this decomposition is not canonical if
n\neq 0
.)

Finally, we can conclude that the differential
d(K/W)
is a composition of surjections
d(K/W^{\prime})
and
d(W^{\prime}/W)\otimes K
:

\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\to\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W^{\prime})\otimes K\to\left(\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)\otimes W^{\prime}\right)\otimes K=\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)\otimes K.

Therefore, it is surjective.

This finishes the proof of part 1.

Part 2: Kernel
Let
\mathbb{K}
be the kernel of
d(K/W)
. We prove that it is equal to
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W)
in two steps. First, we will show that the space
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W)
is inside the kernel. Second, we will show that it is the whole kernel by a dimension counting argument.

Part 2, Step 1:
We prove
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W)\subset\mathbb{K}
.

We recall that the differential
d(K/W)
is a restriction from
K
to
W
, Lemma
2.58
. This means that
\mathbb{K}
is the subspace of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
consisting of all operators that are equal to zero on
W
.

First, we prove that the space
{\swabfamily E}(K/W)
is in
\mathbb{K}
. Indeed, let
D\in{\swabfamily E}(K/W)
and
w\in W
, then
D(w)=wD(1)=0
.

Next, we prove that the kernel
\mathbb{K}
is a left ideal. Indeed, let
D\in\mathbb{K}
, i.e.,
D(W)=0
, then for every
D^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
we have
D^{\prime}\circ D(W)=D^{\prime}(D(W))=D^{\prime}(0)=0
.

Finally, we can conclude that the left ideal generated by
{\swabfamily E}(K/W)
, i.e., the space
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W)
, is in the kernel
\mathbb{K}
.

This proves the inclusion.

Part 2, Step 2:
We prove that the inclusion
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W)\subset\mathbb{K}
is an equality.
The proof goes by comparing
p
-filtrations of these spaces, and using distinguished generators to count dimensions.

The
p
-filtration of the kernel
\mathbb{K}
are the subspaces

\mathbb{K}_{n}\coloneqq\mathbb{K}\cap\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K).

And, the
p
-filtration of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W)
are the subspaces

\left(\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W)\right)\cap\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K).

However, we will focus on its subfiltration

\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{n})\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W)\cap\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K).

where
W_{n}=W\cdot K^{p^{n}}
is
W
up to
p^{n}
-powers. It is a filtration:

\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{n})=\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W).

We observe that

\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{n})\subset\mathbb{K}_{n}=\operatorname{ker}(d(K/W_{n}))\cap\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)

because
\mathbb{K}
is the set of operators that are zero on
W
, so
\mathbb{K}_{n}
is the set of operators that are zero on
W
and
K^{p^{n}}
, i.e., on
W_{n}
.

For convenience, we include a diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{D:D(W)=0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{End}_{k}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(W,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ker}(d(K/W))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)\otimes K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(W_{n})\otimes K.}

We prove that, for every
n\geq 0
, we have

\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{n})=\mathbb{K}_{n}
.

First, we make some preparations. Let
[K:K^{p}]=p^{r}
,
[K:W_{n}]=p^{N}
, and
[W_{n}:K^{p^{n}}]=p^{M}
. Then, by Proposition(Diff=End)
2.72
and elementary calculations, we have the following equalities

\displaystyle\operatorname{dim}_{K}(\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K))=p^{N+M}=p^{rn},

\displaystyle\operatorname{dim}_{K}(\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(W_{n})\otimes K)=p^{M},

\displaystyle\operatorname{dim}_{K}(\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{n}}(K))=p^{N},

\displaystyle\operatorname{dim}_{K}{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{n})=p^{N}-1.

Now, we compute the dimension of
\mathbb{K}_{n}
. It is

\operatorname{dim}_{K}(\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K))-\operatorname{dim}_{K}(\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(W_{n})\otimes K)=p^{N+M}-p^{M}.

Next, we compute the dimension of
\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{n})
.

Let
G=\{G^{m}_{1},\ldots G^{m}_{r_{m}},\}_{m=1,2,\ldots,n}
be a set of distinguished generators for
\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{n}}(K)
and let
\{E^{m}_{1},\ldots,E^{m}_{r-r_{m}}\}_{m=1,2,\ldots}
be a completion of
G
, Definitions
4.18
and
4.20
. Now, by Proposition
4.24
, we can identify elements of
\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{n})
as the ones that are a
K
-linear sum of operators

\left(\prod_{m=1}^{n}\prod^{r-r_{m}}_{j=1}\left(E^{m}_{j}\right)^{a^{m}_{j}}\right)\left(\prod_{m=1}^{n}\prod^{r_{m}}_{i=1}\left(G^{m}_{i}\right)^{b^{m}_{i}}\right),

where
0\leq a^{m}_{i}<p-1
and
0\leq b^{m}_{i}<p-1
, but
\sum_{i,m}b^{m}_{i}>0
. Moreover, these operators are linearly independent, forming a basis of this subspace.

We count them. First, we fix the part
\prod_{m=1}^{n}\prod^{r_{m}}_{i=1}\left(G^{m}_{i}\right)^{b^{m}_{i}}
. There are exactly
p^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}r-r_{m}}=p^{rn-\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{m}}=p^{(N+M)-N}=p^{M}
monomials with this part fixed.

Now, since the monomials
\prod_{m=1}^{n}\prod^{r_{m}}_{i=1}\left(G^{m}_{i}\right)^{b^{m}_{i}}
are a basis of
{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{n})
, there are
p^{N}-1
of them. Therefore, the dimension of
\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{n})
is equal
p^{M}(p^{N}-1)
.

The numbers are equal:
p^{N+M}-p^{M}=p^{M}(p^{N}-1)
. Consequently, the inclusion

\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{n})\subset\mathbb{K}_{n}
is an equality.

Finally, we have

\mathbb{K}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathbb{K}_{n}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{n})=\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W).

This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.27

(Left Ideals,
D
-modules)
.

We can observe that in Theorem
4.26
the
K
-vector space
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W)
is only a left ideal of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
, but not a two-sided ideal. In particular, the space
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W)\otimes K
is not an algebra. In fact, we could easily prove that the algebra
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
is simple, i.e., it does not admit any non-trivial two-sided ideals. Indeed, the essence of a proof is that for every nonzero differential operator
D
of order at least
1
there exists a sequence of elements
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in K
such that
0\neq[[\ldots[D,x_{1}],x_{2}],\ldots],x_{n}]\in K
. This simple lemma could be proved by writing
D
in terms of some coordinates and then choosing elements
x_{i}
from the coordinates based on the leading form of
[D]
. From this lemma, we can conclude that every non-zero two-sided ideal of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
contains
1
, so it is the whole algebra.

Moreover, the algebra
\operatorname{Diff}_{W}(K)
is equivalent to
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W)
. Consequently, power towers are equivalent to some left
D
-modules, where we put
D=\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
. Any left
D
-module can be considered a differential equation, thus power towers can be considered solutions to some differential equations.

4.5.
Subfields and Power Towers

We determine which subfields inject into power towers.

Definition 4.28
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
W
be a subfield of
K
.
We define
the power tower of
W
on
K
to be the power tower
W_{\bullet}
defined by
W_{i}\coloneqq W\cdot K^{p^{i}}
for
i\geq 0
.

Moreover, we say that
W_{n}
is
W

up to
p^{n}
-powers
of
K
.

Lemma 4.29
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
W
be a subfield of
K
. Then the power tower of
W
on
K
is a power tower on
K
.

Proof.

We have to show that
(W\cdot K^{p^{n}})\cdot K^{p^{m}}=W\cdot K^{p^{m}}
for
0\leq m\leq n
.
This follows from the fact that taking the composite of subfields is associative. Indeed, we have

(W\cdot K^{p^{n}})\cdot K^{p^{m}}=W\cdot(K^{p^{n}}\cdot K^{p^{m}})=W\cdot K^{p^{m}}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Here are some examples.

Example 4.30
.

The constant tower on
K
, Example
4.3
, is the power tower of
K
on
K
. It corresponds to the subalgebra
K\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
, and it corresponds to the Jacobson sequence
(0,0,\ldots)
.

Example 4.31
.

The tower of
p
-powers of
K
, Example
4.4
, is the power tower of
k
on
K
.
It corresponds to the subalgebra
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
, and it corresponds to the Jacobson sequence
