(T_{K/K^{p}},T_{K^{p}/K^{p^{2}}},T_{K^{p^{2}}/K^{p^{3}}},\ldots)
.

Example 4.32
.

Finite length power towers are the power towers of purely inseparable subfields on
K
, Lemma
4.8
.

In particular, the extension
K/K^{p^{n}}
corresponds to the subalgebra
\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
, and to the Jacobson sequence
(T_{K/K^{p}},T_{K^{p}/K^{p^{2}}},\ldots,T_{K^{p^{n-1}}/K^{p^{n}}},0,0,\ldots)
.

Example 4.33
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
K=k(x,y)
, and
W=k(x)
. Then the subfield
W_{n}=k(x,y^{p^{n}})
is
W
up to
p^{n}
-powers of
K
.

The power tower of
k(x)
on
k(x,y)
corresponds to the subalgebra
K
-spanned by
\frac{1}{a!}\frac{\partial^{a}}{\partial y^{a}}
for
a\geq 0
.
And, it corresponds to the Jacobson sequence

(k(x,y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y},k(x,y^{p})\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{p}},k(x,y^{p^{2}})\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{p^{2}}},\ldots).

The next definition introduces a name for power towers that are power towers of subfields: algebraically integrable ones. This name is inspired by the theory of foliations, where foliations corresponding to rational fibrations are called algebraically integrable. A foliation corresponds to a rational fibration if its generic leaf is an algebraic variety.

Definition 4.34
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
V_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
K
.
We say that the tower
V_{\bullet}
is
algebraically integrable
if there is a subfield
W
of
K
such that
V_{\bullet}=W_{\bullet}
, where
W_{\bullet}
is the power tower of
W
on
K
.

In the theory of foliations, if we have an algebraically integrable foliation, then we can recover the rational fibration corresponding to it by computing its first integrals. This inspires the name in the following definition.

Definition 4.35
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
W_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
K
. We define a field

W_{\infty}\coloneqq\bigcap_{n\geq 0}W_{n}.

We call it the field of
first integrals
of the power tower
W_{\bullet}
.

The following is the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 4.36
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be the perfection of
K
. We assume that
K/k
is a finitely generated field extension.
Let
W
be a subfield of
K
.

Then, the field of first integrals of the power tower of
W
on
K
is the separable closure of
k\cdot W
in
K
, i.e.,
W_{\infty}=\left(k\cdot W\right)^{s}
.

Moreover, the power tower of
W
on
K
and the power tower of
W_{\infty}
on
K
are equal.

Proof.

First, we observe that
W_{\bullet}=\left(W\cdot k\right)_{\bullet}
. Therefore, without loss of generality, we can assume that
k\subset W
.

Now, we prove that
W\subset W^{s}\subset W_{\infty}
, and their power towers on
K
are equal. Indeed, for every
n\geq 0
, we have

K\supset\left(W^{s}\right)_{n}\supset W_{n}\supset K^{p^{n}}.

Thus
\left(W^{s}\right)_{n}\supset W_{n}
is purely inseparable, but it is also separable. Therefore, we have
\left(W^{s}\right)_{n}=W_{n}
. Hence
W^{s}\subset W_{\infty}
, because
W^{s}\subset W_{n}
for every
n\geq 0
.

Finally, power towers of
W^{s}
and
W_{\infty}
on
K
are equal, because

W^{s}\subset W_{\infty}\subset W_{n}

implies

W_{n}=W^{s}\cdot K^{p^{n}}\subset W_{\infty}\cdot K^{p^{n}}\subset W_{n}.

This finishes the proof of the power towers being equal.

Moreover, by the same argument as above, we have that
W_{\infty}^{s}=W_{\infty}
.

Next, we prove that if
W^{s}\neq W_{\infty}
, then
T_{W_{\infty}/W^{s}}\neq 0
.
Indeed, we know that we have an inclusion
W^{s}\subset W_{\infty}
that is a finitely generated field extension, and the fields
W^{s},W_{\infty}
are separably closed in
K
. Now, if
W^{s}\neq W_{\infty}
, then we can find a finite sequence of elements
x_{i}\in W_{\infty}
, where
i=1,\ldots,r
, such that

L_{0}\coloneqq W\subset L_{1}\coloneqq W(x_{1})^{s}\subset L_{2}\coloneqq L_{1}(x_{2})^{s}\subset...\subset L_{r}\coloneqq L_{r-1}(x_{r})^{s}=W_{\infty}

is a sequence of proper extensions, and
x_{i}
is purely inseparable, or purely transcendental over
L_{i-1}
. A simple calculation shows that
\Omega_{L_{r}/L_{r-1}}
has dimension one, so it is non-zero. Furthermore,
\Omega_{L_{r}/L_{0}}
surjects onto it, so it is non-zero too. Therefore, we have

T_{W_{\infty}/W^{s}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{W_{\infty}}(\Omega_{L_{r}/L_{0}},W_{\infty})\neq 0.

Finally, we can prove that the assumption
W^{s}\neq W_{\infty}
leads to a contradiction. Indeed, we have the following short exact sequences, Theorem
4.26
:

\displaystyle 0\to\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W^{s})\hookrightarrow

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\xrightarrow{d(K/W^{s})}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W^{s})\otimes K\to 0,

\displaystyle 0\to\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{\infty})\hookrightarrow

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\xrightarrow{d(K/W_{\infty})}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(W_{\infty})\otimes K\to 0.

However, every differential is a restriction of linear operators, Lemma
2.58
, so
W^{s}
-linear operators are mapped to
W^{s}
-linear operators; therefore, we get the following short exact sequences:

\displaystyle 0\to\left(\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W^{s})\right)_{W^{s}}\hookrightarrow

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}_{W^{s}}(K)\xrightarrow{d(K/W^{s})}\operatorname{Diff}_{W^{s}}(W^{s})\otimes K\to 0,

\displaystyle 0\to\left(\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{\infty})\right)_{W^{s}}\hookrightarrow

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}_{W^{s}}(K)\xrightarrow{d(K/W_{\infty})}\operatorname{Diff}_{W^{s}}(W_{\infty})\otimes K\to 0,

where
\left(\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W^{s})\right)_{W^{s}}\coloneqq\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W^{s})\cap\operatorname{Diff}_{W^{s}}(K)
and also
\left(\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{\infty})\right)_{W^{s}}\coloneqq\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{\infty})\cap\operatorname{Diff}_{W^{s}}(K)
.

Now, we know that power towers of
W^{s}
and
W_{\infty}
on
K
are equal, therefore
\operatorname{Diff}_{W^{s}}(K)=\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{\infty}}(K)
and
{\swabfamily E}(K/W^{s})={\swabfamily E}(K/W_{\infty})
. Consequently, we have an equality of left ideals:

\left(\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W_{\infty})\right)_{W^{s}}=\left(\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)\cdot{\swabfamily E}(K/W^{s})\right)_{W^{s}}.

This implies that

\operatorname{Diff}_{W^{s}}(W_{\infty})\otimes K\xrightarrow{d(W_{\infty}/W^{s})\otimes K}\operatorname{Diff}_{W^{s}}(W^{s})\otimes K

is an isomorphism. And this leads to a contradiction:

0\neq T_{W_{\infty}/W^{s}}\otimes K\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{W^{s}}(W_{\infty})\otimes K\simeq\operatorname{Diff}_{W^{s}}(W^{s})\otimes K=W^{s}\otimes_{W^{s}}K=K,

i.e., first-order operators are in the zeroth order operators. This cannot be true.

This finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

The next result determines all subfields determined by their approximations up to
p^{n}
-powers.

Corollary 4.37

(Subfields Into Power Towers)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be the perfection of
K
. We assume that
K/k
is a finitely generated field extension.

Then, there is a bijection between subfields
W\subset K
satisfying the condition
k\subset W=W^{s}\subset K
and algebraically integrable power towers on
K
.

Explicitly, this bijection is given by taking the power tower of a subfield
W
, i.e.,
W\mapsto W_{\bullet}
. And, the inverse of this map is taking the field of first integrals of an algebraically integrable power tower, i.e,.
W_{\bullet}\mapsto W_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Let
W
be a subfield such that
k\subset W=W^{s}\subset K
. Let
W_{\bullet}
be the power tower of
W
on
K
. Then, by Theorem
4.36
, we have
W_{\infty}=\left(W\cdot k\right)^{s}=W^{s}=W
.

Let
W_{\bullet}
be an algebraically integrable power tower on
K
. Let
V\subset K
be a subfield such that
V_{\bullet}=W_{\bullet}
. Then,
W_{\infty}=\left(V\cdot k\right)^{s}
by Theorem
4.36
. Therefore, we have
k\subset W_{\infty}=W_{\infty}^{s}\subset K
and
\left(W_{\infty}\right)_{\bullet}=W_{\bullet}
.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Non-algebraically integrable power towers exist, and it is generally complicated to know whether or not a given power tower is algebraically integrable. This resembles a situation from characteristic zero, where whether a given foliation on a variety is algebraically integrable is a nontrivial question. Furthermore, there are “more” non-algebraically integrable power towers than algebraically integrable ones.

Example 4.38

(Not Algebraically Integrable Power Tower)
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
K=k(x,y)
. We define a power tower on
K
by

W_{n}\coloneqq k(x+y^{p}+\ldots+y^{p^{n-1}},y^{p^{n}}).

The power tower
W_{\bullet}
is not algebraically integrable.

Indeed, if it were a power tower of a subfield
W\subset K
, then we would have
W\subset W\cdot k\subset\bigcap_{n\geq 0}W_{n}=k
, and, consequently,
W\cdot k=k
. But then, we would have that
W_{n}=W\cdot K^{p^{n}}=W\cdot k\cdot K^{p^{n}}=k\cdot K^{p^{n}}=K^{p^{n}}
. This is a contradiction.

We finish this section with two remarks connecting algebraically integrable power towers with the topological Jacobson–Bourbaki correspondence
3.2
.

Remark 4.39
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be the perfection of
K
. We assume that
K/k
is a finitely generated field extension.

Let
\mathcal{D}\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
be a
K
-subalgebra. Let
W_{\infty}
be the first integrals of the power tower
W_{\bullet}
corresponding to
\mathcal{D}
.

We can consider
\mathcal{D}
as a subalgebra of
\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{Z}}(K)
. Let
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
be the closure of
\mathcal{D}
in
\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{Z}}(K)
in the finite topology, Definition
3.1
. This closure is of the form
\operatorname{End}_{W}(K)
, where
W
is a subfield of
K
, by Topological Jacobson–Bourbaki Correspondence
3.2
.
We can prove that
W=W_{\infty}
. Indeed,
\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{D})=W_{\infty}
, so
\operatorname{End}_{W_{\infty}}(K)\supset\mathcal{D}
. Hence,
\operatorname{End}_{W}(K)\subset\operatorname{End}_{W_{\infty}}(K)
, so
W_{\infty}\subset W
. But,
\operatorname{End}_{W}(K)\supset\mathcal{D}
, so
\operatorname{const}(\operatorname{End}_{W}(K))=W\subset\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{D})=W_{\infty}
.

However,
\mathcal{D}
is determined by
W_{\infty}
if and only if
W_{\bullet}
is algebraically integrable.

Remark 4.40
.

One could say that there are more not algebraically integrable power towers than algebraically integrable ones. Indeed, let
k
be a countable field and let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension with
k
being the perfection of
K
, then if
K/k
has transcendental degree at least two, there are only countably many subfields between
k
and
K
. Still, there are uncountable many power towers on
K
. Therefore, in most cases, for such
k
, a closure of a subalgebra of differential operators (in the finite topology) says little about this subalgebra because the corresponding power tower is most likely not algebraically integrable.

4.6.
n
-Foliations,
n=1,2,\ldots,\infty

We develop a big class of examples of power towers.

4.6.1.
Basic Theory

The following definition shall be compared with Proposition
4.9
.

Definition 4.41
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be the perfection of
K
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension.

Let
0\leq n\leq\infty
.
Let
W_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
K
.

The power tower
W_{\bullet}
is a
n
-foliation
if it is of length
n
, and it satisfies

\operatorname{dim}_{W_{i+1}}(W_{i})=\operatorname{dim}_{W_{j+1}}(W_{j})

for
0\leq i,j<n
.

The rank of a
n
-foliation
W_{\bullet}
is
\operatorname{log}_{p}(\operatorname{dim}_{W_{1}}(K))
.

In other words, a power tower
W_{\bullet}
is an
n
-foliation if and only if it is a power tower such that

K=W_{0}\supset W_{1}\supset W_{2}\supset\ldots\supset W_{n}=W_{n+1}=\ldots,

\operatorname{[W_{0}:W_{1}]=[W_{1}:W_{2}]=\ldots=[W_{n-1}:W_{n}]},

where
[K:W]=\operatorname{dim}_{W}(K)
.

Remark 4.42
.

The name “
n
-foliation” is an extension of the name “
1
-foliation” that in the literature refers to saturated
p
-Lie algebras on varieties,
[
Lan15
]
. I believe that the origin of this name is just a short way to refer to “foliations of height
n
” for
n=1
, which were introduced by Ekedahl in
[
Eke87
]
. His definition applied to the space
\operatorname{Spec}(K)
is equivalent to the definition of power towers on
K
we just gave. However, the original definition is formulated for varieties, so we will discuss it later, Proposition
5.52
.

The following might be considered the motivational example for the notion of an
n
-foliation.

Example 4.43
.

Let
k
be a perfect field.
Let
K\supset W\supset k
be both purely transcendental extensions.
Then, the power tower of
W
on
K
is an
\infty
-foliation. Indeed, we can write
W=k(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
and
K=W(y_{1},\ldots y_{m})
, where
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
are transcendental bases for
W/k
and
K/W
respectively.
Then

W_{n}=W(y_{1}^{p^{n}},\ldots y_{m}^{p^{n}})=k(x_{1},\ldots x_{r},y_{1}^{p^{n}},\ldots y_{m}^{p^{n}}).

Therefore,
\operatorname{dim}_{W_{n}}(W_{n-1})=p^{m}
is constant. Consequently,
W_{\bullet}
is a
\infty
-foliation on
K
of rank
m
, and the power tower
(W_{n})_{\bullet}
is a
n
-foliation of rank
m
.

The visualization of an
n
-foliation begins with the following result.

Proposition 4.44
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be the perfection of
K
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension.

Let
0\leq n\leq\infty
. Let
W_{\bullet}
be a
n
-foliation on
K
of rank
r
. Let
\mathcal{D}\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
be a subalgebra corresponding to
W_{\bullet}
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\mathcal{D}\cap T_{K/K^{p}}
.

Then, there exists a set of well-chosen distinguished generators for
\mathcal{D}
:

G=\{G^{m}_{1},\ldots,G^{m}_{r}\}_{m=1,2,\ldots,n}

such that

[G^{m}_{i}]=\gamma_{p^{m-1}}([G^{1}_{i}])

for
i=1,2,\ldots,r
and
1\leq m<n+1
. Consequently, the graded subalgebra satisfies

\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}={S}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{*})^{*gr}\cap\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K),

where
{S}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{*})^{*gr}
is the divided power polynomial algebra in variables
\mathcal{F}_{1}
.

Proof.

There is almost nothing to be proved, but there is still something. Proposition
4.23
gives us a set of well-chosen distinguished operators

G=\{G^{m}_{1},\ldots,G^{m}_{r_{m}}\}_{m=1,2,\ldots}

for
\mathcal{D}
. Their leading forms
[G^{m}_{i}]
are linear combinations of
\gamma_{p^{m}}(G_{j}^{1})
, now we want equalities, not combinations. We will do some corrections.

We recall and add. The numbers
r_{m}
are defined to satisfy the relation
p^{r_{m}}=[W_{m+1}:W_{m}]
, so they are equal
r
for
m<n
and
0
for
m\geq n
. This means that the effective range of the index
m
is
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.

By Definition
4.18
, the leading forms
[G^{m}_{i}]
for
i=1,2,\ldots,r
are linearly independent inside
\gamma_{p^{m-1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})
. This space has dimension
r
, therefore the set
[G^{m}_{i}]
for
i=1,2,\ldots,r
is a basis. Consequently, we can find a
K
-linear combination of these leading forms that satisfies

\sum_{j=1}^{r}a_{j}[G^{m}_{j}]=\gamma_{p^{m-1}}([G^{1}_{i}]),

where
a_{j}\in K
. So, we can substitute
G^{m}_{i}
with
\sum_{j=1}^{r}a_{j}G^{m}_{j}
(a linear base change). However, it is not obvious that these new operators are well-chosen. This is true, but it requires an argument. Indeed, by simple linear algebra, we see that the derivation
d(K/W_{m-1})(\sum_{j=1}^{r}a_{j}G^{m}_{j})
is the unique one in
\mathcal{F}_{m-1}\otimes K
to be mapped to
\operatorname{gr}d(K/K^{m-1})[\gamma_{p^{m-1}}([G^{1}_{i}])]
, see Lemma
4.22
, but that derivation already belongs to
\mathcal{F}_{m-1}
implying that
a_{j}\in W_{m-1}
for
j=1,2,\ldots,r
, thus the operators
\sum_{j=1}^{r}a_{j}G^{m}_{j}
are well-chosen.

Finally, by Proposition
2.79
, we have a factorization

\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}\to{S}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{*})^{*gr}\to\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K),

and
{S}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{*})^{*gr}
is a divided power polynomial algebra. So, the leading forms of elements from
G
generate exactly
{S}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{*})^{*gr}\cap\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)
, thus the graded algebra
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}
is equal to this intersection. This finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 4.45
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be the perfection of
K
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension.

The graded subalgebra of an
n
-foliation on
K
depends only on
W_{1}
and
n
.

Proof.

It directly follows from the last part of Proposition
4.44
. Indeed, let
W_{\bullet}
be a
n
-foliation, then

\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{\bullet}}(K)={S}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{*})^{*gr}\cap\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)
, and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
depends only on
W_{1}
, and it is of height
n
, so the
n
is minimal such in that formula.
∎

Remark 4.46
.

Let
W_{\bullet}
be an
n
-foliation corresponding to a subalgebra
\mathcal{D}
.
Let
G
be a set of distinguished generators for
\mathcal{D}
from Proposition
4.44
.

From the elementary fact that
\gamma_{p}^{\circ m}
is equal to
\gamma_{p^{m}}
up to an integer that is invertible modulo
p
, we see that
\gamma_{p}([G^{m}_{j}])
for
j=1,2,\ldots,r
is a basis for
\gamma_{p^{m+1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})
for
m<n
.

Remark 4.47
.

The Ekedahl’s definition of a “foliation of height
n
” applied to a field
K
is
[
Eke87
, Definition 3.1]
. We repeat it:

A subalgebra
\mathcal{D}\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K)
is a foliation of height
n

if it satisfies
the equality
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}={S}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{*})^{*gr}\cap\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{K^{p^{n}}}(K)
.

From this equality for the graded algebra, we can soundly read out that the power tower corresponding to
\mathcal{D}
is of length
n
and is of constant degree till its length, i.e., it is an
n
-foliation. And, the converse is true by Proposition
4.44
. Therefore, our definition and Ekedahl’s are equivalent to each other on fields.

Remark 4.48
.

We can visualize Proposition
4.44
for an
\infty
-foliation in an analogous way to Figure
1
in the figure below. (The figure was created using
https://www.mathcha.io
.) The visualization of a
n
-foliation is the truncation of the one for
\infty
-foliation at
\gamma_{p^{n-1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})
.

gr
continues
D
\text{gr}\ D
\gamma_{p}(F_{1})
F_{1}
\gamma_{p^{2}}(F_{1})
\gamma_{p^{3}}(F_{1})
\ \ \ \ \ F_{1}
continues

Figure 2
.
A visualisation of a subalgebra corresponding to a
\infty
-foliation.

4.6.2.
Separable Field Extensions and
\infty
-Foliations

A definition of a separable field extension can be found in Definition
2.13
. Essentially, it says that such an extension admits a separable transcendental basis. It is algebraic if and only if the basis is empty.

Proposition 4.49

(Separable Subfields Into
\infty
-Foliations)
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be the perfection of
K
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension.

Let
K/W/k
be a subfield such that
K/W
is a separable extension.

Then, the power tower of
W
on
K
is a
\infty
-foliation on
K
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
W=W^{s}
inside
K
, because the power towers of
W
and
W^{s}
on
K
are equal, Theorem
4.36
.

Let
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}
be a
p
-basis of
W
over
k
, see Definition
2.15
. Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
be a separating transcendence basis of
K
over
W
. Then
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m},x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
form a
p
-basis of
K
over
k
.
In particular, we have

\displaystyle k(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})^{s}

\displaystyle=W,

\displaystyle W(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})^{s}

\displaystyle=K,

\displaystyle k(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m},x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})^{s}

\displaystyle=K,

where the upper index
s
means separable closure in
K
.

(To shorten the notation in the rest of the proof, we will write
(x_{i})
for
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
,
(y_{j})
for
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})
, etc.)

Example
4.43
says that the power tower of
k(x_{i})
on
k(x_{i},y_{j})
is a
\infty
-foliation on
k(x_{i},y_{j})
. The rest of the proof pulls back this information to the power tower of
W
on
K
.

Let
n\geq 0
be an integer.
We have that
k(x_{i},y_{j}^{p^{n}})^{s}=W_{n}
, where the separable closure is taken in
K
.
Moreover, we claim that

k(x_{i},y_{j}^{p^{n}})\otimes_{k(x_{i},y_{j}^{p^{n+1}})}W_{n+1}\simeq W_{n+1}(y_{j}^{p^{n}})=W_{n}

via natural maps to
K
.
Of course, we have that
W_{n+1}(y_{j}^{p^{n}})\subset W_{n}
.
We also have
W_{n+1}(y_{j}^{p^{n}})=W_{n+1}(y_{j}^{p^{n}})^{s}\supset k(x_{i},y_{j}^{p^{n}})^{s}=W_{n}
. Therefore
W_{n+1}(y_{j}^{p^{n}})=W_{n}
.

Finally, we can conclude that the dimension of
k(x_{i},y_{j}^{p^{n}})
over
k(x_{i},y_{j}^{p^{n+1}})
is the same as the dimension of
W_{n}
over
W_{n+1}
, because the later comes from the former by extending coefficients to
W_{n+1}
and this operation is flat. This finishes the proof.
∎

4.6.3.
Examples of
\infty
-Foliations

We compute more examples of
\infty
-foliations, their Jacobson sequences, and their subalgebras.

Definition 4.50
.

Let
K=k(x,y)
be the field of rational functions in two variables over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
.

Let
A_{i}\in k
for
i\geq 1
. We define a power tower
W_{\bullet}
of a “formal subfield”

\overline{W_{\infty}}=k(x+A_{1}y^{p}+A_{2}y^{p^{2}}+A_{3}y^{p^{3}}+\ldots)

by the following formulas,
n\geq 1
,

W_{n}\coloneqq k(x+A_{1}y^{p}+A_{2}y^{p^{2}}+\ldots+A_{n-1}y^{p^{n-1}},y^{p^{n}}).

Question 4.51

(Algebraic Solutions to
D
-Modules from Power Towers)
.

We do not define any “formal subfields” in the above definition. From the above definition, we treat the symbol
\overline{W_{\infty}}
as a suggestive label for the power tower. It is not an actual notion, nor is it an actual object. It is just a name, a label.

Nevertheless, I believe that if a good functor of solutions for algebraic
D
-modules over arbitrary bases exists, then these “formal subfields” could be something like “formal first integrals on the generic point” from that functor. It is pure speculation.

Does it make any sense? Are there actual formal subfields?

It is clear that the above power towers of “formal subfields” are power towers on
K
. Below, we present calculations of their Jacobson sequences and their subalgebras of differential operators.

Example 4.52
.

Let
K=k(x,y)
be rational functions in two variables over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
.

Let
W_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
K
of a “formal subfield”, Definition
4.50
,

\overline{W_{\infty}}=k(x+A_{1}y^{p}+A_{2}y^{p^{2}}+A_{3}y^{p^{3}}+\ldots).

We compute Jacobson sequences and subalgebras of differential operators corresponding to
W_{\bullet}
, Theorem
4.15
(Galois-type Correspondence for Power Towers). However, we will use symbols
\frac{1}{p^{m}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{m}}}{\partial y^{p^{m}}}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(y^{p^{m}}\right)}
, and
\frac{1}{p^{m}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{m}}}{\partial x^{p^{m}}}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(x^{p^{m}}\right)}
interchangeably on all subfields of
K
. (We can do this without losing anything, because they are mapped to each other by differentials, Proposition
2.59
.)

We begin with the Jacobson sequence.

The first field extension
K/W_{1}
is
k(x,y)\supset k(x,y^{p})
. This corresponds to a
p
-Lie algebra
\mathcal{F}_{1}
on
K
spanned by the vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
.

The second extension
W_{1}/W_{2}
is
k(x,y^{p})\supset k(x+A_{1}y^{p},y^{p^{2}})
. The relative tangent space
T_{W_{1}/W_{1}^{p}}
can be computed using
x,y
. Indeed, it is spanned by

\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{p}}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
.

Knowing this, we clearly see that the extension
W_{1}/W_{2}
corresponds to a
p
-Lie algebra
\mathcal{F}_{2}
on
W_{1}
spanned by a vector field

\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{p}}-A_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}.

This is a
p
-Lie algebra, because the
p
-power of
\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{p}}-A_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
is zero, because
A_{1}\in k
(!).

The third extension
W_{2}/W_{3}
is

k(x+A_{1}y^{p},y^{p^{2}})\supset k(x+A_{1}y^{p}+A_{2}y^{p^{2}},y^{p^{3}}).

From here, the computation of a relative tangent space
T_{W_{2}/W_{2}^{p}}
in terms of
x,y
gets harder.
Indeed, we can use the fact that that the differential
d(K/W_{2})
is surjective, Theorem
4.26
,
to find differential operators on
K
that are mapped onto a basis of
T_{W_{2}/W_{2}^{p}}
.
It is easy to check that the operators

\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{p^{2}}}-A_{1}^{p}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}

are mapped to
T_{W_{2}/W_{2}^{p}}
and span it. (My method: I have guessed them.)
In this basis, the extension
W_{2}/W_{3}
corresponds to a
p
-Lie algebra
\mathcal{F}_{3}
on
W_{2}
spanned by a vector field

\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{p^{2}}}-A_{1}^{p}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}}-A_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}.

We continue similarly for extensions
W_{n-1}/W_{n}
.
Let
n\geq 3
. We have that
T_{W_{n-1}/W_{n-1}^{p}}
is spanned by the operators

\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{p^{n-1}}}-A_{1}^{p^{n-2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{n-2}}}-A_{2}^{p^{n-3}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{n-3}}}-\ldots-A_{n-2}^{p}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
.

And, the extension
W_{n-1}/W_{n}
corresponds to a
p
-Lie algebra
\mathcal{F}_{n}
on
W_{n-1}
spanned by the vector field

\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{p^{n-1}}}-A_{1}^{p^{n-2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{n-2}}}-A_{2}^{p^{n-3}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{n-3}}}-\ldots-A_{n-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}.

Finally, by Theorem
4.17
(Unpacking), we can conclude that
\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{\bullet}}(K)
is generated by the operators

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial y},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{p}}-A_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x},

\displaystyle\ldots,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{p^{n-1}}}-A_{1}^{p^{n-2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{n-2}}}-A_{2}^{p^{n-3}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{n-3}}}-\ldots-A_{n-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x},

\displaystyle\ldots

that form a set of well-chosen distinguished generators for the subalgebra
\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{\bullet}}(K)
, Definition
4.18
. One of its completions, Definition
4.20
, is the set
\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p^{2}}},\ldots
.

This finishes the computation of these examples.

5.
Power Towers on Varieties

We extend the theory of power towers from fields to normal varieties. We discuss why we assume “geometrically connected” everywhere. This is related to discussing a “natural perfect field of definition” for a variety. After explaining this, we set up definitions to prove the Galois-type correspondence for power towers on varieties. It follows from the case for fields. In particular, there is a bijection between the power tower on a variety and the power towers on its generic point. The next sections are about the properties of power towers, which actually depend on the variety. We study the regularity of power towers. We show that these power towers are “trivial” at any closed point in some formal coordinates, a Frobenius-type theorem. However, immediately after that, we show how “trivial” it is at one point may differ from how “trivial” it is at another point. This leads to a stratification of the variety. Then, we discuss a class of power towers inspired by Ekedahl’s paper
[
Eke87
]
. We discuss when a nice morphism, a fibration, is a power tower.

5.1.
Natural Perfect Base

Our definition of a variety is
[
Sta18
, Definition 020D]
. We repeat:

Definition 5.1

(Variety)
.

Let
X
be a scheme over a field
k
. The scheme
X
is a
variety
over the field
k
if it is integral, and the morphism
X\to\operatorname{Spec}(k)
is separated and of finite type.

Remark 5.2
.

If
X
is an integral scheme, then we denote its
field of rational functions
by
K(X)
. This
K
is a notation from
[
Har77
]
, not the base field of
X
. In particular, one can use this notation for schemes that are not over any field, such as
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
, i.e.,
K(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}))=\mathbb{Q}
. This notation is compatible with the notation used in the previous chapter about power towers on fields, where we often have a big field
K
and its perfection
k
. This chapter often has
K=K(X)
and the base field
k
.

In this chapter, we will encounter many “geometric
something
” notions, e.g.,
something
may equal connected, reduced, normal, regular. However, instead of enlisting all lemmas about them here, we will organically cite them inside proofs as we go. Nevertheless, the notion of geometrically connected requires its own paragraph, as it will be almost universally present.

To develop the power tower theory for varieties, we will assume our varieties to be geometrically connected.
We will assume this hypothesis for freely moving between a variety
X/k
and its generic point
K(X)/k
without doing any twists/pullback/changes, ensuring that
k
is the perfection of
K=K(X)
. We do not lose any generality by doing so, because any variety
X/k
over a perfect field
k
admits a factorization through its perfection, the below Lemma
5.3
:

X\to\operatorname{Spec}(K(X)^{p^{\infty}})\to\operatorname{Spec}(k).

So, at any moment we can replace
k
with
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
and then the variety
X/K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
is geometrically connected by the below Proposition
5.6
. This discussion belongs to a classical question: “What is a good field of definition for a given variety?”.

Lemma 5.3

(Natural Perfect Base Field)
.

Let
X/k
be a variety over a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
. Then, we have a factorization

X\to\operatorname{Spec}(K(X)^{p^{\infty}})\to\operatorname{Spec}(k).

In particular, the field extension
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}/k
is finite.
Moreover, if
U=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
is an affine open subset of
X
, then
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}\subset A
is the greatest subfield inside
A
, and it equals
A^{p^{\infty}}=\cap_{n\geq 0}A^{p^{n}}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
X
is affine and it is given by a ring
A
, then by the Noether normalization theorem, we have

k[t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}]\to A,

where the arrow is a finite morphism, and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}
are free variables. This morphism factorizes

k[t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}]\to k^{\prime}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}]\to A,

where
k^{\prime}
is the greatest subfield inside
A
4
4

4

In general, the variables
t_{i}
won’t be free in
k^{\prime}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}]
. They will be the only generators. We just abuse the notation.
(so, the first arrow adds algebraic functions that do not involve any
t_{i}
, this happens finitely many times, and the second arrow adds algebraic functions involving some
t_{i}
nontrivially), and
k^{\prime}=A^{p^{\infty}}
(all functions involving the variables
t_{i}
disappear by taking the perfection), because
k^{\prime}
is perfect by being a finite extension of a perfect field. (The extension
k^{\prime}/k
is finite, because otherwise
A/k
is not of finite type.) Finally, we can conclude that

A^{p^{\infty}}=k^{\prime}=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}.

This proves the lemma.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

In Lemma
5.3
, the assumption that
k
is perfect is essential. Indeed, here is a counterexample.

Let
k=l(x)
, where
l
is a perfect field. Let
A=k[t]
. Then
K(X)=l(x,t)
, so
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=l
. Since it would have a nontrivial kernel, there cannot be any ring map
f:k\to l
.

Definition 5.5

(
[
Sta18
, Definition 0362]
)
.

A scheme
X
over a field
k
is
geometrically connected
if for every field extension
k^{\prime}/k
the base change

X_{k^{\prime}}\coloneqq X\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(k)}\operatorname{Spec}(k^{\prime})

is connected.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
X
be a variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
. Then,
X
is geometrically connected if and only if
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
.

Proof.

(
\Leftarrow
) If the equality
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
is true, then we can apply
[
Sta18
, Lemma 04KV]
to conclude that
X
is geometrically connected. Indeed,
this lemma says that: If
X
is a scheme over a field
k
and it has a point
x\in X
such that
k
is algebraically closed in
\kappa(x)
, then
X
is geometrically connected.

We apply this lemma to the generic point of our
X
to learn that if
k
is algebraically closed in
K(X)
, then
X
is geometrically connected. Now, we assume that
k
is the perfection of
K(X)
, so it is perfect, and it is separably closed in
K(X)
by Theorem
4.37
(Subfields into Power Towers). Therefore,
k
is algebraically closed in
K(X)
. This means that
X
is geometrically connected.

(
\Rightarrow
) We have the inclusion of perfect fields
k\subset K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
, because
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
is the largest perfect subfield of
K(X)
, Lemma
2.7
.

By Lemma
5.3
, we have a factorization:

X\to\operatorname{Spec}(K(X)^{p^{\infty}})\to\operatorname{Spec}(k).

And, we have that
\operatorname{Spec}(K(X)^{p^{\infty}})\to\operatorname{Spec}(k)
corresponds to a finite field extension. So, it is algebraic separable, because
k
is perfect.

Finally, if we have
k\neq K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
, then
\operatorname{Spec}(K(X)^{p^{\infty}})\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(k)}\operatorname{Spec}(K(X)^{p^{\infty}})
is disconnected. Therefore,
X_{K(X)^{p^{\infty}}}
is disconnected. This is a contradiction with
X
being geometrically connected. Therefore, we proved that
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.
∎

Remark 5.7

(Why is “
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
” everywhere?)
.

Lemma
5.3
says that for every variety
X
over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
there exists the factorization

X\to\operatorname{Spec}(K(X)^{p^{\infty}})\to\operatorname{Spec}(k).

Therefore, every variety
X
in positive characteristic is naturally a variety over
\operatorname{Spec}(K(X)^{p^{\infty}})
that is a perfect field. It means that every variety in positive characteristic admits a
natural perfect base field
over which it is defined.

Consequently, in the following sections, the assumption that
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
is not super essential and in most cases could probably be dropped, because we can pretty much always switch
k
to be the natural perfect base
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
. Still, it would be a drag to repeat it in every proof. Moreover, I enjoy being able to recover the base field from the tower of
p
-powers whenever I want, because this is recovering the trivial fibration
X\to\operatorname{Spec}(k)
. Therefore, I assume it everywhere. This helps my sanity because, for me, the tower of
p
-powers is the “foliation” corresponding to the variety itself, and I do not wish to have any twists around that.

5.2.
Basic Theory Including Galois-type Correspondence

We define power towers on varieties and show that they are in an explicit bijection with power towers on the generic point. We use it to conclude a Galois-type correspondence for power towers on varieties from the one for fields via taking normalizations and saturations of relevant notions.

5.2.1.
Power Towers

Please, recall Definition
1.2
.

Convention 5.8

(All is
k
-linear here)
.

All our purely inseparable morphisms
X\to Y
between varieties will be twisted to be
k
-linear, i.e., morphisms of
k
-varieties, and to fit
X\to Y\to X^{(n)}
for some
n
. See Definition
2.19
for how to do these twists. Nothing changes, but this is always assumed, and we will not mention it again.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.

A
power tower
on the variety
X
is a sequence of purely inseparable morphisms
f_{i}:X\to Y_{i}
between normal varieties over
k
for
i\geq 0
, such that the sequence

(K(Y_{0}),K(Y_{1}),K(Y_{2}),\ldots)

is a power tower on
K(X)
; we denote it by
K(Y_{\bullet})
. And, we denote the power tower
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2},\ldots
by
f_{\bullet}
, or
Y_{\bullet}
.

Lemma 5.10

(“On
X
” equals “On
K(X)
”)
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.
Then, there is a bijection between power towers on
X
and power towers on
K(X)
.

Proof.

Every power tower on
X
induces a power tower on
K(X)
by Definition
5.9
. And, any power tower
W_{\bullet}
on
K(X)
can be “lifted” to a power tower on
X
. Indeed, by Proposition
2.21
, for every subfield
K(X)\supset W_{i}
there exists a unique purely inseparable morphism
f_{i}:X\to Y_{i}
between normal varieties such that
W_{i}=K(Y_{i})
. Collecting all these morphisms defines the power tower on
X
lifting
W_{\bullet}
.
∎

There are MANY arrows in a definition of a power tower. We label all of them below.

Convention 5.11

(Notation for Power Towers)
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.
Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
X
.

Then, every morphism
f_{i}:X\to Y_{i}
is of exponent at most
i
. (Its exponent is the exponent of
K(X)\supset K(Y_{i})
)
And, they factorize through each other. We denote the between morphisms by

f^{\prime}_{i}:Y_{i-1}\to Y_{i}.

Each of the morphisms
f^{\prime}_{i}
is of exponent at most
1
by Lemma
4.5
. We denote the second factor of the factorization of
F_{Y_{i-1}/k}
by

g_{i}:Y_{i}\to Y_{i-1}^{(1)}.

Consequently, all these morphisms fit into the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X=Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}=f^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{(1)}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime(1)}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime(1)}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\prime}}_{2}^{(2)}}

There are many of them, and then there are their
p^{n}
-power versions with the index
\bullet^{(n)}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, not only for
n\geq 0
. In many cases, we can go backwards, too, which is often a critical trick for some reasoning.

Convention 5.12
.

Generally, we will follow a convention that a power tower
Y_{\bullet}
on
X
is
P
, where
P
is a property of power towers on fields, if the corresponding to
Y_{\bullet}
power tower
K(Y_{\bullet})
on
K(X)
is
P
.

Here is an example for Convention
5.12
.

Definition 5.13
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.
Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
X
.

•

We say that the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
is of length at most
n
, where
n\geq 0
is an integer, if the morphisms
f^{\prime}_{m}
are identities for
m>n
. It is of length
n
if this
n
is minimal. And, it is of
finite length
, if an integer
n\geq 0
exists, such that
Y_{\bullet}
is of length at most
n
.

•

We say that the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
is a
n
-foliation on
X
, where
n\geq 0
is an integer, if it is of length
n
and the ranks of finite maps
f_{1},f^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{n}
are equal.

•

We say that the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
is a
\infty
-foliation on
X
if the rank of finite maps
f_{1},f^{\prime}_{2},f^{\prime}_{3},\ldots
are equal.

Again, we have the following result: Lemma
4.8
.

Lemma 5.14

(Purely Inseparable Morphisms inject into Power Towers)
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.
Then, there is a bijection between purely inseparable morphisms
f:X\to Y
between normal varieties and power towers on
X
of finite length.

Proof.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a purely inseparable morphism between normal varieties. It is equivalent to the field extension
K(X)\supset K(Y)
by Proposition
2.21
. This field extension is equivalent to a finite length power tower on
K(X)
by Lemma
4.8
. This power tower on
K(X)
is equivalent to a finite length power tower on
X
by Lemma
5.10
.
∎

5.2.2.
Jacobson Sequences

We define Jacobson sequences on varieties by saturating Jacobson sequences from generic points, Definition
4.12
. First, for convenience, we recall a definition of a
p
-Lie algebra on a variety.

Definition 5.15
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
.
A
p
-Lie algebra
on
X
is a quasicoherent subsheaf
\mathcal{F}
of the tangent sheaf
T_{X/k}\coloneqq\Omega_{X/k}^{*}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\Omega_{X/k},\mathcal{O}_{X})
such that

•

\mathcal{F}
is saturated, i.e., the quotient
T_{X/k}/\mathcal{F}
is torsion-free,

•

\mathcal{F}
is closed under Lie bracket, i.e., for every open subset
U\subset X
and sections
v,w\in\mathcal{F}(U)
we have
[v,w]\in\mathcal{F}(U)
,

•

\mathcal{F}
is closed under
p
-power, i.e., for every open subset
U\subset X
and a section
v\in\mathcal{F}(U)
we have
v^{\circ p}\in\mathcal{F}(U)
.

We recall that Ekedahl Correspondence
3.14
says that there is a bijection between purely inseparable morphisms of exponent at most
1
between normal varieties from
X
and
p
-Lie algebras on
X
. It extends the Jacobson correspondence
3.9
to varieties. In particular, if
X\to Y\to X^{(1)}
corresponds to a
p
-Lie algebra
\mathcal{F}
, then
Y=\operatorname{Spec}_{X^{(1)}}(\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}))
, where
\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F})
is the sheaf of functions that are killed by derivations in
\mathcal{F}
.

Definition 5.16
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.
A
Jacobson sequence
on
X
is a sequence of
p
-Lie algebras
\mathcal{F}_{i}
for
i\geq 1
such that:

•

\mathcal{F}_{1}
is a
p
-Lie algebra on
X
,

•

\mathcal{F}_{i}
, for
i>1
, is a
p
-Lie algebra on
Y_{i-1}\coloneqq\operatorname{Spec}_{Y_{i-2}^{(1)}}(\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{i-1}))
,

•

the sequence of vector spaces
\mathcal{F}_{i}\otimes K\left(Y_{i-1}\right)\subset T_{K\left(Y_{i-1}\right)/k}
is a Jacobson sequence on
K(X)
, i.e., the following intersection is trivial, for
i>1
:

\left(\mathcal{F}_{i}\otimes K\left(Y_{i-1}\right)\right)\cap T_{K\left(Y_{i-1}\right)/K\left(Y_{i-2}^{(1)}\right)}=0\subset T_{K\left(Y_{i-1}\right)/k}=T_{K\left(Y_{i-1}\right)/K\left(Y_{i-1}\right)^{p}}.

We denote the Jacobson sequence
\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2},\ldots
by
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
.

Moreover, we say that the Jacobson sequence
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
is a
saturation
of the Jacobson sequence
\mathcal{F}_{1}\otimes K\left(Y_{0}\right),\mathcal{F}_{2}\otimes K\left(Y_{1}\right),\ldots
on
K(X)
, which we denote by
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}\otimes K\left(Y_{\bullet-1}\right)
.

Remark 5.17
.

It is worth emphasizing that the last condition in Definition
5.16
is a generic point condition. It does not involve the variety. Because of this fact, measuring the quality of the saturation will bring us precious information about the variety, e.g., it will explain the behavior of canonical divisors for purely inseparable morphisms.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
. Then, there is an explicit bijection between Jacobson sequences on
X
and Jacobson sequences on
K(X)
.

Proof.

We recall that for a torsion-free coherent sheaf
M
on a variety
X
, there is a bijection between saturated quasicoherent subsheaves of
M
and vector subspaces of the restriction of
M
to the generic point that will be denoted here by
M\otimes K(X)
, Lemma
2.24
. This implies that there is a bijection between
p
-Lie algebras on
X
and
p
-Lie algebras on
K(X)
. This immediately implies that there is a bijection between Jacobson sequences, because all conditions for a sequence to be Jacobson are essentially identical for varieties and fields.
∎

5.2.3.
Saturated Subalgebras of Differential Operators

For a variety
X
over a field
k
, we can apply the definition of differential operators, Definition
2.31
, locally. (It is analogous to the definition of
T_{X/k}
and
\Omega_{X/k}
.) This leads to the formation of a sheaf of algebras on
X
determined by

\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)(\operatorname{Spec}(A))\coloneqq\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A),

where
\operatorname{Spec}(A)\subset X
is an open affine subset. The sheaf
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
is torsion-free, and it admits a natural decomposition

\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)=\mathcal{O}_{X/k}\oplus{\swabfamily E}(X/k),

where
{\swabfamily E}(X/k)
is the subsheaf of operators that are zero on one, i.e., the operators
D
such that
D(1)=0
.

Analogically, in characteristic
p>0
, one can define a sheaf of algebras

\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(r)}}(X)(\operatorname{Spec}(A))\coloneqq\operatorname{Diff}_{A^{p^{r}}}(A)

that is a sheaf of subalgebras of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
. (The quotient
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)/\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(r)}}(X)
is torsion-free.) And, the collection of all of them is a
p
-filtration of the sheaf of algebras
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
.

On fields, every subalgebra corresponds to a power tower; however, this is no longer true on varieties. We have to assume that our subalgebras are saturated.

Definition 5.19
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.
A
saturated subalgebra
of differential operators on
X/k
is a quasicoherent subsheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X/k}\subset\mathcal{D}\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
such that:

•

\mathcal{D}
is saturated, i.e., the quotient
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)/\mathcal{D}
is torsion-free,

•

\mathcal{D}
is closed under composition, i.e., it is a sheaf of subalgebras.

The
p
-filtration
of the (saturated) subalgebra
\mathcal{D}
is defined by, for
r\geq 0
:

\mathcal{D}_{r}\coloneqq\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(r)}}(X)\cap\mathcal{D}.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
. Then, there is a bijection between saturated subalgebras of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
and subalgebras of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K(X))
.

Proof.

It is a special case of Lemma
2.24
.
∎

We can sheafify the operation of computing constants of a subalgebra of differential operators from Theorem
3.2
to obtain a similar operation on varieties.

Definition 5.21
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.
Let
\mathcal{D}\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K(X))
be a saturated subalgebra on
X/k
. We define a subsheaf
\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{D})
of
\mathcal{O}_{X/k}
by the following values on affine open subsets
\operatorname{Spec}(A)\subset X
:

\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{D})(\operatorname{Spec}(A))\coloneqq\{f\in\mathcal{O}_{X/k}(\operatorname{Spec}(A)):\ \forall_{D\in\mathcal{D}(\operatorname{Spec}(A))}\ [D,f]=0\}.

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.
Let
\mathcal{D}\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K(X))
be a saturated subalgebra on
X/k
.
Then, the sheaf
\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{D}_{n})
is a quasicoherent sheaf of algebras on
X^{(n)}
.

Proof.

The sheaf
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is a subsheaf of
\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(r)}}(X)
, by its definition. Therefore, we have an inclusion of sheaves of algebras on
|X|
:

\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{D}_{n})\supset\mathcal{O}_{X^{(n)}/k}=\operatorname{const}(\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(r)}}(X)).

This proves the lemma.
∎

5.2.4.
Galois-type Correspondence for Power Towers on Varieties

The following is the analogous statement of Theorem
4.15
for varieties.

Theorem 5.23
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.
Then, there are natural bijections between the following data on
X
:

(1)

power towers on
X
,

(2)

Jacobson sequences on
X
,

(3)

saturated subalgebras of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
.

Explicitly,
some of these bijections are given by the following operations.

(1)

From power towers to Jacobson sequences.
An iterated tangent sheaf
:

Y_{\bullet}\mapsto(T_{Y_{0}/Y_{1}},T_{Y_{1}/Y_{2}},T_{Y_{2}/Y_{3}},\ldots),

(2)

From Jacobson sequences to power towers.
An iterated Spec of annihilators
:

F_{\bullet}\mapsto f_{i}:X\to\operatorname{Spec}_{Y_{i-2}^{(1)}}(\operatorname{Ann}(\mathcal{F}_{i-1}))\ \text{for $i>1$}

(3)

From power towers to saturated subalgebras.
A saturated algebra of differential operators relative to a power tower
:

Y_{\bullet}\mapsto\operatorname{Diff}_{Y_{\bullet}}(X)\coloneqq\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\operatorname{Diff}_{Y_{i}}(X),

(4)

From saturated subalgebras to power towers.
Fields of constants of the
p
-filtration
:

\mathcal{D}\mapsto f_{i}:X\to\operatorname{Spec}_{X^{(i)}}(\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{D}_{i}))\ \text{for $i\geq 1$}.

Proof.

We already know that power towers, Jacobson sequences, and subalgebras of differential operators on
K(X)
are naturally in bijection with each other by Theorem
4.15
.
Now, power towers on
X
and power towers on
K(X)
are naturally in bijection by Lemma
5.10
.
Jacobson sequences on
X
and Jacobson sequences on
K(X)
are naturally in bijection by Lemma
5.18
.
Finally, saturated subalgebras of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
and subalgebras of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(K(X))
are naturally in bijection by Lemma
5.20
. Moreover, all these bijections are given by the formulas enlisted in the theorem, because computing
\operatorname{const}
and
\operatorname{Ann}
produces sheaves of normal algebras. Hence, these operations are “saturations” of the ones from Theorem
4.15
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.24
.

In Theorem
5.23
, we provided explicit formulas for some of the bijections. However, the remaining ones are less obvious. In particular, the unpacking, Theorem
4.17
(Unpacking), is tricky, because it uses differentials. It is unclear to me that a differential
d(X/Y)
exists since I use Criterion
2.57
to know if the differential is admitted.
This criterion applied to
X\to Y
requires
Y
to be a regular variety. It looks like a quite artificial assumption.

But, if the power tower is regular, Definition
5.26
, then it admits all required differentials, and then the unpacking is true, Corollary
5.32
. However, in general, I do not know what happens when all kinds of singularities are present, and I believe it is likely not true then. This is just a belief, not even a strong one.

5.3.
Regular Power Towers

We develop a notion of regularity for power towers on varieties. We define the notion. We prove what it means in practice. We show that every purely inseparable morphism is regular away from a codimension at least
2
subset.

In short, regular power towers almost behave like power towers on fields. Whenever this fails, something interesting happens at the saturation level between the variety and its generic point. Something emergent. We will see that in the next sections.
Here we collect good behaviors.

5.3.1.
Motivation

In characteristic zero, a foliation of rank
1
on a smooth variety
X
generated locally by a local vector field
v
is said to be regular if every such
v
does not admit “singularities”, e.g., for a global vector field
v=f\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+g\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
on a plane
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
it means that the vanishing locus of both functions
f
and
g
is empty. This notion of “regularity” of a foliation extends to foliations of all ranks (it measures how much a subsheaf
\mathcal{F}\subset T_{X/k}
is a subbundle) and it is true that for every foliation there exists a subset of the variety, which is of codimension at least
2
, such that the foliation is regular away from it. One can read
[
Bru15
, Chapter 2]
for more details about this topic.

5.3.2.
Development

We will say that a power tower is regular if its Jacobson sequence is a sequence of subbundles. This can be formally stated using a singular locus of a subsheaf. We recall that the singular locus of a subsheaf is a singular locus of the quotient, and the singular locus of a coherent sheaf is defined in
[
Fri98
, Chapter 2, Definition 18]
. This locus is where this sheaf is
not
locally free.

Definition 5.25
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.
Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
X
, and let
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
be the Jacobson sequence corresponding to
Y_{\bullet}
.
We define the following
singular loci
as subsets of the topological space
|X|
:

\operatorname{Sing}_{n}(Y_{\bullet})\coloneqq\bigcup_{i<n+1}\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{F}_{i}\subset T_{Y_{i-1}/k})
for
n\geq 1
or
n=\infty
.

Definition 5.26
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
. Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
X
.

We say that the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
is
regular
if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

X/k
is a regular variety,
[
Sta18
, Definition 07R7]
,

•

\operatorname{Sing}_{\infty}(Y_{\bullet})=\emptyset
.

Remark 5.27
.

We recall that for a variety
X/k
, it is true that
T_{X/k}=T_{X/X^{(1)}}
, because we work with tangent spaces/sheaves, and not with tangent complexes. This is nothing but “derivations kill
p
-powers”.

We say it, because there could be a confusion about why
k
and not
X^{(1)}
, because
X^{(1)}
is used in the definition of a
p
-Lie algebra
3.7
, a Jacobson sequence
5.16
, or
4.12
.

We recall that an open subset is sometimes called big if its complement is of codimension at least
2
.

Proposition 5.28
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
. Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a
finite length
power tower on
X
, i.e., a purely inseparable morphism.

Then, there exists a big open subset
U\subset X
such that the restriction of
Y_{\bullet}
to
U
is a regular power tower on
U
.

Proof.

First, a big open subset
U\subset X
exists such that
U/k
is regular. Indeed, it follows from
[
GW10
, Exercise 6.21]
: a geometrically normal scheme of finite type over a field is smooth away from a closed subset of codimension
2
. We can apply this result to our
X/k
, because
X
is normal over a perfect field
k
, and thus geometrically normal by
[
GW10
, Exercise 6.19: (iii)]
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
be the Jacobson sequence corresponding to
Y_{\bullet}
.

Next, we show that there is a big open subset of
V\subset U
such that
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{F}_{1}\subset T_{X/k})\cap V=\emptyset
. (This
V
will be big in
X
too.) Indeed, by definition of a singular support of a coherent module, we have to show that the quotient
T_{X/k}/\mathcal{F}_{1}
is locally free away from a closed subset of codimension
\geq 2
. It is true precisely by
[
Fri98
, Proposition 22]
that says a singular support of a finite module over a regular local Noetherian ring is of codimension at least
2
. This proposition, combined with the fact that being locally free is an open condition, implies the existence of
V
.

Finally, we assume that the length of the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
is
n
. Then, the tower is a finite sequence:

X\to Y_{1}\to Y_{2}\to\ldots\to Y_{n}.

By the first paragraph, there are big open subsets

U_{0}\subset X
,
U_{1}\subset Y_{1}
,
\ldots
, and
U_{n-1}\subset Y_{n-1}

that are regular. By the second paragraph, for every
U_{i}
, there exists a big open subset
V_{i}\subset U_{i}
such that
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{F}_{i}\subset T_{Y_{i-1}/k})\cap V_{i}=\emptyset
. Let
|U|\subset|X|
be the intersection of these open sets
\bigcap_{i=0}^{n-1}|V_{i}|
(we use the fact that purely inseparable morphisms are homeomorphisms), and let
U\subset X
be the corresponding open subscheme. It is a big open subset of
X
because it is a finite intersection of big open subsets. Thus, the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
restricted to
U
is regular. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.29
.

For a power tower of infinite length, there could be no big open subset such that the tower restricted to it is regular. Indeed, we can sketch a construction of an
\infty
-foliation of rank
1
on a plane
X=\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
with a wild singular locus for
n=\infty
. We will do it below. Anyway, this means that whenever we have a power tower that is regular at a big open subset, then this power tower is somehow special.

A
\infty
-foliation on
X
is equivalent to a sequence of vector fields satisfying some conditions on the generic point, i.e., they are local generators for the Jacobson sequence.

A sketch of a construction. The main point is accumulating singular points from different
\mathcal{F}_{i}
. First, we take any vector field that defines a
1
-foliation
X\to Y_{1}
. We extend it to a
2
-foliation
X\to Y_{2}
. (We can do this by (future) Proposition
6.4
.) This gives us a new vector field on
Y_{1}
. It
should
be possible to choose one with a singularity. It
should
be possible to decide where it is: if this singularity is shared with singularities from
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, then we can translate this singularity by a vector to a new point in a way that the translation of this vector field from
\mathcal{F}_{2}
still defines a
2
-foliation, but maybe it is a new one. We can continue it for
\mathcal{F}_{3},\mathcal{F}_{4},\ldots
. At each step, we can choose a new point from the closed points of
X
to add to the singular locus. In particular, we could produce a
\infty
-foliation on
X
whose
\operatorname{Sing}_{\infty}
is an infinite set of closed points that is dense in
X
.

The sketch is solid up to the explicit claims about how to work with power towers on
\mathbb{A}^{2}
that we do not discuss here in this paper in that much detail. In particular, as I have not provided formal statements and proofs of those results, this sketch should be considered speculation rather than an announcement of results. As we always should do with sketches of arguments.

The following result explains what being regular means in practice.

Lemma 5.30
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
p
-Lie algebra. Let
f:X\to Y\to X^{(1)}
be corresponding to it
1
-foliation.

Then, the
1
-foliation
f
is regular if and only if
\mathcal{O}_{X/k}
locally admits a
p
-basis over
\mathcal{O}_{Y/k}
and
\mathcal{O}_{Y/k}
locally admits a
p
-basis over
\mathcal{O}_{X^{(1)}/k}
.

Proof.

We recall and adapt Definition
2.15
(
p
-basis) to rings:

A ring
A
admits a
p
-basis over a subring
B\supset A^{p}
if there are
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in A
such that the map

\bigoplus_{0\leq a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}<p}\left(B\prod_{i=1}^{n}{x_{i}^{a_{i}}}\right)\to A

is an isomorphism of
B
-modules, i.e.,
A
is free over
B
and the monomials
\prod_{i=1}^{n}{x_{i}^{a_{i}}}
for
0\leq a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}<p
are a basis.

We come back to the proof.

Of course, one way is easy. If we have the
p
-bases, then we can compute the rest, though we have to notice that in our case, smooth and regular are equivalent, because we are geometrically regular. (It is a special case of Kunz’s theorem
[
Kun69
, Theorem 2.1.]
to conclude that
A/k
is regular from
A/A^{p}
being flat, or having a
p
-basis.) In greater generality, we could not conclude regularity from
T_{K/k}
being free (e.g., look at
k[x]/(x^{2})
).

The other direction is hard. We work on local rings and then “spread out” the results to affine rings. We use Yuan’s theorem
[
Yua70
, 12. Theorem]
to get
p
-bases, because this is precisely what that theorem does.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.31

(Regular Power Tower in Practice)
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
. Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a regular power tower on
X
, let
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
be the Jacobson sequence corresponding to
Y_{\bullet}
, and let
\mathcal{D}
be the saturated subalgebra of differential operators corresponding to
Y_{\bullet}
.

Then, the following are true:

(1)

Every variety
X,Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots
is regular over
k
.

(2)

The sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}
is locally free over
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i+1}/k}
for
i\geq 0
, and it is locally free over
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i-1}^{(1)}/k}
for
i\geq 1
. Moreover, each of these inclusions admits a
p
-basis locally.

(3)

The sheaf
\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(n)}}(X)
is a vector bundle for
n\geq 0
, and
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
is a locally free quasicoherent sheaf.

(4)

The subsheaf
\mathcal{D}_{n}\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(n)}}(X)
is a subbundle for
n\geq 0
.

(5)

Let
i\geq 1
. The morphisms
f_{i}:X\to Y_{i}
and
g_{i}:Y_{i}\to Y_{i-1}^{(1)}
are flat, and every one of them admits a differential.

(6)

The sheaves
\Omega_{Y_{i}/k}
,
T_{Y_{i}/k}
,
\Omega_{Y_{i}/Y_{i+1}}
, and
T_{Y_{i}/Y_{i+1}}=\mathcal{F}_{i+1}
for
i\geq 0
are vector bundles.

(7)

The subsheaf
\mathcal{F}_{i+1}\subset T_{Y_{i}/k}
for
i\geq 0
is a subbundle, and the quotient is isomorphic to
{f^{\prime}_{i+1}}^{*}\left(T_{Y_{i+1}/Y_{i}^{(1)}}\right)
, i.e., we have a short exact sequence of vector bundles:

0\to\mathcal{F}_{i+1}\to T_{Y_{i}/k}\xrightarrow{d(Y_{i}/Y_{i+1})}{f^{\prime}_{i+1}}^{*}\left(T_{Y_{i+1}/Y_{i}^{(1)}}\right)\to 0.

Proof.

The lemma
5.30
does all the heavy lifting. It gives us plenty of
p
-bases that we can use to compute every item of this theorem directly. (It is good to mention that one can reverse combinations of these conditions to get “regular if and only if blah and blah”, but we do not need that.)

Purely inseparable morphisms are finite, and all conditions can be checked locally, so we can work with affine schemes only. Let
X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
and
Y_{i}=\operatorname{Spec}(B_{i})
for
i\geq 0
.

Indeed,
Y_{\bullet}
is a regular power tower, thus
X
is regular,
T_{X/k}
is a vector bundle, and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
is a subbundle.
From Lemma
5.30
, we conclude that locally there is a
p
-basis
t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}
for
A/B_{1}
and a
p
-basis
l_{1},l_{2},\ldots,l_{m}
for
B_{1}/A^{p}
. By restricting ourselves to open subsets, we can assume that these
p
-bases are exactly for these rings. Now, a trick:
l_{1},\ldots,l_{m},t_{1}^{p},\ldots,t_{r}^{p}
is a
p
-basis for
B_{1}/B_{1}^{p}
, so by Lemma
5.30
applied to
X=\operatorname{Spec}(B_{1}),Y=\operatorname{Spec}(B_{1}^{p})
, we get that
Y_{1}
is regular,
T_{Y_{1}/k}
is a vector bundle, and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
is a subbundle. We can continue this reasoning over and over. This proves (1), (2), (5) by Criterion
2.57
, (6).

We prove (3), (4). Recall that Proposition
2.72
(Diff=End). It says that the algebras
\mathcal{D}_{n},\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(n)}}(X)
are actually isomorphic to sheaf homs that are locally given by
\operatorname{Hom}_{B_{n}}(A,A)
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{p^{n}}}(A,A)
. Locally,
A
is free and finite over
B_{n}
and
B_{n}
is free and finite over
A^{p^{n}}
, this implies that these sheaves are vector bundles, and that the former is a subbundle of the latter. Finally,
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(n)}}(X)
proves the final claim that it is quasi-coherent and locally free. (This is due to the fact how this filtration works. It adds more free variables with each
n
. Recall that
\mathbb{Q}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!}\mathbb{Z}
is not free.)

The final statement (7) is a result for a regular
1
-foliation
\mathcal{F}_{i+1}\subset T_{Y_{i}/k}
, so we can assume that we work with
X
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
. It is an equally arbitrary choice. Let
B=B_{1}
.

We start with an exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{F}_{1}\to T_{X/k}\xrightarrow{d(X/Y_{1})}T_{Y_{1}/k}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X/k}.

We want to compute the image of the last map.

After applying Proposition
2.72
(Diff=End), the above sequence is equal to

0\to\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(A,A)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{p}}(A,A)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{B^{p}}(B,B)\otimes A.

Since
A
is flat over
B
, and
B
over
A^{p}
. We observe that
\operatorname{Hom}_{B^{p}}(B,A)=\operatorname{Hom}_{B^{p}}(B,B)\otimes A
and therefore the image is equal to
\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{p}}(B,A)
. It is the local chart of
{f^{\prime}_{1}}^{*}\left(T_{Y_{1}/X^{(1)}}\right)
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.32

(Unpacking for Regular Power Towers)
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
. Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a regular power tower on
X
, let
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
be the Jacobson sequence corresponding to
Y_{\bullet}
, and let
\mathcal{D}
be the saturated subalgebra of differential operators corresponding to
Y_{\bullet}
.

Then, the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\cap T_{X/k}

\displaystyle=T_{X/Y_{1}}=\mathcal{F}_{1},

\displaystyle d(X/Y_{1})(\mathcal{D})\cap T_{Y_{1}/k}

\displaystyle=T_{Y_{1}/Y_{2}}=\mathcal{F}_{2},

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle=\ldots,

\displaystyle d(X/Y_{n})(\mathcal{D})\cap T_{Y_{n}/k}

\displaystyle=T_{Y_{n}/Y_{n+1}}=\mathcal{F}_{n+1},

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle=\ldots,

We call the set of these equalities the unpacking of the saturated subalgebra
\mathcal{D}
into the Jacobson sequence
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
.

Proof.

First, the differentials for
f_{i}:X\to Y_{i}
are admitted by Proposition
5.31
(Item 5).

Second, it is enough to prove these equalities locally. Therefore, by Proposition
5.31
(Item 2), we can assume that all
Y_{i}
for
i\geq 0
are affine and free over each other. Let
X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
and
Y_{i}=\operatorname{Spec}(B_{i})
for
i\geq 0
.

Let
m\geq n+1
be a fixed integer. Then, we have, by Proposition
2.72
(Diff=End),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{m}=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{B_{m}}(A)=\operatorname{Diff}_{B_{m}}(A)

\displaystyle d(X/Y_{n})(\mathcal{D}_{m})=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{B_{m}}(B_{n},A)=\operatorname{Diff}_{B_{m}}(B_{n})\otimes A

\displaystyle d(X/Y_{n})(\mathcal{D}_{m})\cap T_{Y_{n}/k}=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{B_{m}}(B_{n},A)\cap\operatorname{Der}_{B_{n}^{p}}(B_{n})=\operatorname{Der}_{B_{n+1}}(B_{n})=\mathcal{F}_{n+1}

Finally, since
m
is arbitrary, we have

d(X/Y_{n})(\mathcal{D})\cap T_{Y_{n}/k}=\mathcal{F}_{n+1}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.33

(Saturated Well-Chosen Generators for Regular Power Towers)
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
. Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a regular power tower on
X
, and let
\mathcal{D}
be the saturated subalgebra of differential operators corresponding to
Y_{\bullet}
. Let
\operatorname{Spec}(A)
be an open subset of
X
such that
A/k
admits a set of coordinates. Let
\operatorname{Spec}(B_{i})=Y_{i}\restriction_{|\operatorname{Spec}(A)|}
.

Then there exists a set of well-chosen distinguished generators
G
, Definition
4.18
, for the subalgebra
\mathcal{D}\otimes K(X)
such that

•

G\subset\mathcal{D}(\operatorname{Spec}(A))
, i.e., these operators are operators
A\to A
,

•

The set
\left\{\left(\prod_{m=1}^{k}\prod^{r_{m}}_{i=1}\left(G^{m}_{i}\right)^{b^{m}_{i}}\right):k\geq 0,0\leq b^{m}_{i}\leq p-1\right\}
is a
A
-basis of
\mathcal{D}(\operatorname{Spec}(A))
.

•

the set
\{d(A/B_{m-1})(G^{m}_{1}),\ldots,d(A/B_{m-1})(G^{m}_{r_{m}})\}
is a
B_{m-1}
-basis of
\mathcal{F}_{m}(\operatorname{Spec}(B_{m-1}))
.

We call any such set
G
by the name a set of
saturated well-chosen generators for the regular power tower

Y_{\bullet}\restriction_{|\operatorname{Spec}(A)|}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.31
(The meaning of regularity in practice), we know that all the gadgets used in the proof of Theorem
4.23
(Existence of Distinguished Generators) and Proposition
4.24
are available for regular power towers. Therefore, we can just repeat these proofs in this local affine chart. First, we construct a set
G
in the same way, and for
this
set we use the unpacking to conclude it is a basis, not just a generic basis. So, instead of separating the construction from the proposition, we do them together, and therefore, we do not need the counting dimensions argument at the end. We could do that for fields, but it is tricky to separate them for rings.
∎

5.4.
Frobenius Theorem for Power Towers

5.4.1.
Context

Let
(X/\mathbb{C},\mathcal{F})
be a smooth complex variety of dimension
n
with a regular foliation
\mathcal{F}
on it. The Frobenius theorem for
(X/\mathbb{C},\mathcal{F})
says that locally in the analytic topology the foliation
\mathcal{F}
, of rank
n-m
, where
0\leq m\leq n
, is induced by the projection
\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
, i.e., for every closed point
x\in X
there is an analytic open subset
x\in U\subset X
such that
U
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and there is an analytic map
f:\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{|U}=T_{f}
. For a precise statement with a proof, one can consult
[
Voi02
, Theorem 2.20]
.

However, this version makes sense only over complex numbers. Nevertheless, it implies a formal statement that makes sense over any field of characteristic zero:
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integrable at the formal point
\widehat{x}
, i.e., there exist local coordinates
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}}
such that:

\mathbb{C}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}]]\to\mathbb{C}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]]\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}}

induces
\mathcal{F}_{|\overline{x}}
.
We will prove an analogous result for regular power towers.

5.4.2.
Formal Frobenius Theorem for Power Towers

Theorem 5.34

(Formal Frobenius Theorem for Regular Power Towers)
.

Let
X
be a variety over an
algebraically closed
field
k
of characteristic
p>0
such that
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
. Let
d
be the dimension of
X/k
.
Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a regular power tower on
X
.

Let
m\geq 0
be an integer.
Let
x
be a closed point on
X
.
Let
y_{m}=f_{m}(x)
be its image in
Y_{m}
.
Then, there exist coordinates
t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}\in\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
at
x
such that
f_{m}:X\to Y_{m}
at the formal point
\widehat{x}
is given by

\widehat{f_{m}}:\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{Y_{m},y_{m}}}\simeq k[[t_{1}^{p^{a_{1}}},t_{2}^{p^{a_{2}}},t_{3}^{p^{a_{3}}},\ldots,t_{d}^{p^{a_{d}}}]]\to k[[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},\ldots,t_{d}]]\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}},

where
0\leq a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq a_{3}\leq\ldots\leq a_{d}\leq m
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{d}a_{i}=N
, where we have
p^{N}=[K(X):K(Y_{m})]
.

Moreover, for
m=\infty
, there exist coordinates
t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}
such that the function
\widehat{f_{\infty}}\coloneqq\bigcap_{m\geq 0}\widehat{f_{m}}
is given by

\widehat{f_{\infty}}:\bigcap_{m\geq 0}\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{Y_{m},y_{m}}}\simeq k[[t_{1}^{p^{a_{1}}},t_{2}^{p^{a_{2}}},t_{3}^{p^{a_{3}}},\ldots,t_{d}^{p^{a_{d}}}]]\to k[[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},\ldots,t_{d}]]\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}},

where
0\leq a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq a_{3}\leq\ldots\leq a_{d}\leq\infty
. (Our convention is
t^{p^{\infty}}=0
.)

Proof.

The proof has a simple structure: we choose any formal coordinates
t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}\in\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
at
x
, and then we modify them to obtain coordinates satisfying the theorem using the sequence of natural maps

\frac{m_{x}}{m_{x}^{2}}\leftarrow\frac{m_{y_{1}}}{m_{y_{1}}^{2}}\leftarrow\frac{m_{y_{2}}}{m_{y_{2}}^{2}}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow\frac{m_{y_{m}}}{m_{y_{m}}^{2}}\leftarrow\ldots,

where
y_{i}=f_{i}(x)\in Y_{i}
. Nevertheless, it requires some preparations beforehand. (We use the “being regular” assumption to know the dimensions of the vector spaces below, see Proposition
5.31
.)

We introduce some notation. We put
y_{0}=x
, and, for
i=0,\ldots,m-1
, we denote the images and cokernels of the above maps (from cotangent complexes) by:

\frac{m_{y_{i+1}}}{m_{y_{i+1}}^{2}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{K}_{i+1}\hookrightarrow\frac{m_{y_{i}}}{m_{y_{i}}^{2}}\to\widetilde{\Omega_{Y_{i}/Y_{i+1},y_{i}}}\coloneqq\frac{m_{y_{i}}}{m_{y_{i}}^{2}+m_{y_{i+1}}}\to 0.

In particular, the short exact sequence

0\to\mathbb{K}_{i+1}\to\frac{m_{y_{i}}}{m_{y_{i}}^{2}}\to\frac{m_{y_{i}}}{m_{y_{i}}^{2}+m_{y_{i+1}}}\to 0

is a sequence of
k
-vector spaces.

The map
f^{\prime}_{i+1}:Y_{i}\to Y_{i+1}
is a
1
-foliation, see Notation
5.11
, so it factorizes
F_{Y_{i}/k}
. The map
g_{i}:Y_{i+1}\to Y^{(1)}_{i}
induces a map

\frac{m_{y_{i}^{(1)}}}{m_{{y_{i}}^{(1)}}^{2}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{L}_{i+1}\hookrightarrow\frac{m_{y_{i+1}}}{m_{y_{i+1}}^{2}},

where
y_{i}^{(1)}=F_{Y_{i}/k}(y_{i})
, and
\mathbb{L}_{i+1}
is the image. The kernel of the surjection is
{m_{{y_{i}}^{(1)}}^{2}+m_{y_{i+1}^{(1)}}}
.

We prove that the maps
\mathbb{K}_{i+1}\to\mathbb{K}_{i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,m-1
, are surjective. Indeed, it follows from the equality of sheaves
\Omega_{Y_{i-1}/Y_{i}}=\Omega_{Y_{i-1}/Y_{i+1}}
. We can conclude this equality from comparing the cotangent complexes for
Y_{i-1}/Y_{i}
and
Y_{i-1}/Y_{i+1}
, which define these sheaves. The image of
\mathbb{K}_{i+1}
is the kernel of
\frac{m_{y_{i-1}}}{m_{y_{i-1}}^{2}}\to\frac{m_{y_{i-1}}}{m_{y_{i-1}}^{2}+m_{y_{i}}}
, so it is equal
\mathbb{K}_{i}
. Derivations kill
p
-powers.

We define

\mathbb{K}_{i+1\to i}\coloneqq\operatorname{Ker}(\mathbb{K}_{i+1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{K}_{i}).

We observe that there is a natural function
\frac{m_{y_{i}}}{m_{y_{i}}^{2}}\to\frac{m_{y_{i}^{(1)}}}{m_{y_{i}^{(1)}}^{2}}
given by the formula

t\operatorname{mod}m_{y_{i}}^{2}\mapsto{\left(t\operatorname{mod}m_{y_{i}}^{2}\right)}^{p}=t^{p}+\operatorname{mod}m_{y_{i}^{(1)}}^{2}.

This is a bijection.
Moreover, we can combine it with the surjection
\frac{m_{y_{i}^{(1)}}}{m_{{y_{i}}^{(1)}}^{2}}\to\mathbb{L}_{i+1}
to have a surjective function

\frac{m_{y_{i}}}{m_{y_{i}}^{2}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{L}_{i+1}.

It is essential to point out that it is not
k
-linear. It is Frobenius twisted
k
-linear, so we can treat this function as a
k
-linear if necessary, since
k
is perfect but will not be linear on the nose.

We have just proved that for each
i\geq 1
we have the following diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{m_{y_{i+1}}}{m_{y_{i+1}}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{m_{y_{i}}}{m_{y_{i}}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Omega_{Y_{i}/Y_{i+1},y_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}_{i+1\to i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{m_{y_{i-1}^{(1)}}}{m_{y_{i-1}^{(1)}}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{m_{y_{i}}}{m_{y_{i}}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{m_{y_{i-1}}}{m_{y_{i-1}}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Omega_{Y_{i-1}/Y_{i},y_{i-1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\mapsto t^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq^{\prime}}

where the composition
\mathbb{K}_{i}\to\mathbb{L}_{i}
is zero, because if
t\in\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i+1}}
, then the class of
t^{p}
is zero in
\frac{m_{y_{i}}}{m_{y_{i}}^{2}}
. Consequently,
\mathbb{K}_{i+1\to i}
is inside
\mathbb{L}_{i}
, and there is a map
\widetilde{\Omega_{Y_{i-1}/Y_{i},y_{i-1}}}\to\mathbb{L}_{i}
that is a (twisted) isomorphism.

We can link the above diagrams for unspecified
i
into the following big diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{m_{y_{4}}}{m_{y_{4}}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{m_{y_{3}}}{m_{y_{3}}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Omega_{Y_{3}/Y_{4},y_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}_{4\to 3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{m_{y_{3}}}{m_{y_{3}}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{m_{y_{2}}}{m_{y_{2}}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Omega_{Y_{2}/Y_{3},y_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}_{3\to 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{m_{y_{2}}}{m_{y_{2}}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{m_{y_{1}}}{m_{y_{1}}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Omega_{Y_{1}/Y_{2},y_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}_{2\to 1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{m_{y_{i}}}{m_{y_{i}}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{m_{x}}{m_{x}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Omega_{X/Y_{1},x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}

where the compositions
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to\mathbb{L}_{i+1}
are surjective, because of the equality of sheaves
\Omega_{Y_{i+1}/X^{(i+1)}}=\Omega_{Y_{i+1}/Y_{i}^{(1)}}
that follows from the condition
K(X)^{p^{i+1}}\cdot K(Y_{i+1})^{p}=K(Y_{i})^{p}
from Definition
4.1
. Moreover, the kernel of
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to\mathbb{L}_{i+1}
is equal to
\mathbb{K}_{i+1\to i}
since
\mathbb{K}_{i}\to\mathbb{L}_{i}
is zero, and their dimensions agree (see below).

We can observe that the dimension of
\mathbb{K}_{i}
is equal
d-r_{i}
, where
r_{i}
is the dimension of
\mathbb{L}_{i}
that is also the dimension of
\widetilde{\Omega_{Y_{i-1}/Y_{i},y_{i-1}}}
. So, the dimension of
\mathbb{K}_{i+1\to i}
is
r_{i+1}-r_{i}
.

Finally, we can proceed to the proof.

Let
t_{1,1},\ldots,t_{1,d}\in m_{x}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
be elements whose classes in
m_{x}/m_{x}^{2}
form a basis of this vector space. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
t_{1,r_{1}+1},\ldots,t_{1,d}
is a basis of
\mathbb{K}_{1}
.
Therefore,
t_{1,1}^{p},\ldots,t_{1,r_{1}}^{p}
is a basis of
\mathbb{L}_{1}
.

Let
b_{1},\ldots,b_{r_{1}-r_{2}}\in m_{y_{2}}
be elements whose classes form a basis of
\mathbb{K}_{2\to 1}
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
t_{1,1}^{p},\ldots,t_{1,r_{2}}^{p},b_{1},\ldots,b_{r_{1}-r_{2}}
forms a basis of
\mathbb{L}_{1}
. The elements
b_{j}
are
p
-powers in
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
, so we can take their
p
-roots in this ring. We denote
t_{2,j}\coloneqq b_{j}^{1/p}
.

The elements
t_{1,1}^{p^{2}},\ldots,t_{1,r_{2}}^{p^{2}}
form a basis for
\mathbb{L}_{2}
.
Let
b_{1},\ldots,b_{r_{2}-r_{3}}\in m_{y_{3}}
be elements whose classes form a basis of
\mathbb{K}_{3\to 2}
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
t_{1,1}^{p^{2}},\ldots,t_{1,r_{3}}^{p^{2}},b_{1},\ldots,b_{r_{2}-r_{3}}
form a basis of
\mathbb{L}_{2}
.
The elements
b_{j}
are
p^{2}
-powers in
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
,
so we can take their
p^{2}
-roots in this ring. We denote
t_{3,j}\coloneqq b_{j}^{1/p^{2}}
.

And so on and so on:

The elements
t_{1,1}^{p^{i}},\ldots,t_{1,r_{i}}^{p^{i}}
form a basis for
\mathbb{L}_{i}
.
Let
b_{1},\ldots,b_{r_{i}-r_{i+1}}\in m_{y_{i+1}}
be elements whose classes form a basis of
\mathbb{K}_{i+1\to i}
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
t_{1,1}^{p^{i}},\ldots,t_{1,r_{i+1}}^{p^{i}},b_{1},\ldots,b_{r_{i}-r_{i+1}}
form a basis of
\mathbb{L}_{i}
.
The elements
b_{j}
are
p^{i}
-powers in
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
,
so we can take their
p^{i}
-roots in this ring. We denote
t_{i+1,j}\coloneqq b_{j}^{1/p^{i}}
.

If our power tower is of finite length
m
, as it is in the first part of the theorem, then we continue the above procedure till
i=m
, and the following coordinates, in the opposite order,

t_{m+1,1},\ldots,t_{m+1,r_{m}},t_{m,1},\ldots,t_{m+1,r_{m-1}-r_{m}},\ldots,t_{1,1},\ldots,t_{1,d-r_{1}}

satisfy the theorem.

If our power tower is of infinite length, then we do not stop the procedure. And, in this case, there is an integer
m\geq 1
such that
Y_{m},Y_{m+1},\ldots
is a
\infty
-foliation of rank
r>0
on
Y_{m}
, i.e.,
r=r_{m+1}=r_{m+2}=r_{m+3}=\ldots>0
, because the degrees are nonincreasing by Proposition
4.9
. By the finite length case for
m,m+1,m+2,\ldots
, we have that:

\displaystyle\widehat{f_{m}}:k[[t_{m+1,1}^{p^{m}},\ldots,t_{m+1,r}^{p^{m}},t_{m,1}^{p^{m-1}},\ldots,t_{1,d-r_{1}}]]
