\displaystyle\to k[[t_{m+1,1},\ldots,t_{1,d-r_{1}}]],

\displaystyle\widehat{f_{m+1}}:k[[t_{m+2,1}^{p^{m+1}},\ldots,t_{m+2,r}^{p^{m+1}},t_{m,1}^{p^{m-1}},\ldots,t_{1,d-r_{1}}]]

\displaystyle\to k[[t_{m+2,1},\ldots,t_{1,d-r_{1}}]],

\displaystyle\widehat{f_{m+2}}:k[[t_{m+3,1}^{p^{m+2}},\ldots,t_{m+3,r}^{p^{m+2}},t_{m,1}^{p^{m-1}},\ldots,t_{1,d-r_{1}}]]

\displaystyle\to k[[t_{m+3,1},\ldots,t_{1,d-r_{1}}]],

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle\to\ldots.

This implies that

\bigcap_{i\geq 0}k[[t_{m+i,1}^{p^{m+i-1}},\ldots,t_{m+i,r}^{p^{m+i-1}},t_{m,1}^{p^{m-1}},\ldots,t_{1,d-r_{1}}]]=k[[t_{m,1}^{p^{m-1}},\ldots,t_{1,d-r_{1}}]],

because only the first
r
elements are modified each step.

Consequently, we have

\widehat{f_{\infty}}:k[[t_{m+1,1}^{p^{\infty}},\ldots,t_{m+1,r}^{p^{\infty}},t_{m,1}^{p^{m-1}},\ldots,t_{1,d-r_{1}}]]\to k[[t_{m+1,1},\ldots,t_{1,d-r_{1}}]].

This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.35
.

In Theorem
5.34
, we assume that
k
is algebraically closed instead of just perfect to avoid technicalities around variations of residue fields. I did not follow all the details for fields
k
, which are only perfect. Is the result correct in the form that we replace
k
with its finite extension
k^{\prime}=k(x)
,
k^{\prime}
is the common residue field for all
y_{i}
, and the proof goes the same way from there? Maybe, I don’t know.

Remark 5.36
.

The function
\widehat{f_{\infty}}
in Theorem
5.34
is a function from formal first integrals to all formal functions at the point
x
, i.e., it is of the same nature as the inclusion
\mathbb{C}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}]]\to\mathbb{C}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]]\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}}
from the context paragraph. Indeed, let say that
d=3
and
a_{0}=1,a_{2}=44,a_{3}=\infty
, then we get that the power tower at
x
corresponds to

k[[t_{1}^{p^{0}},t_{2}^{p^{44}},t_{3}^{p^{\infty}}]]=k[[t_{1},t_{2}^{p^{44}}]]\to k[[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}]]\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}},

and the elements in
k[[t_{1},t_{2}^{p^{44}}]]
are precisely the elements from
k[[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}]]
that are constants for the subalgebra
\operatorname{Diff}_{Y_{\bullet}}(X)_{|\overline{x}}
(of
continuous/formal
differential operators).

Question 5.37
.

Is the above theorem
5.34
true for every subalgebra of “continuous/formal” differential operators? Or is there an extra condition that needs to be added?

I believe that all “formal power towers” are of the form from the above theorem. Is it actually true? If so, then, in the language of Sweedler’s Galois theory
[
Swe68
]
, we could say that every “formal power tower” is “modular”, which is nice, because not every power tower on a field or on a variety is “modular”. Some examples are not such.

5.5.
Stratifications Induced by Regular Power Towers

The formal Frobenius theorem for power towers, Theorem
5.34
, says that every regular power tower on a variety
X/k
, where
k=\overline{k}
, at every closed point
x\in X(k)
is locally in a formal neighborhood given by

k[[t_{1}^{p^{a_{1}}},t_{2}^{p^{a_{2}}},t_{3}^{p^{a_{3}}},\ldots,t_{d}^{p^{a_{d}}}]]\to k[[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},\ldots,t_{d}]]\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}}.

The tuple
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})
is determined by the point
x
; however, it may differ at other points of
X
. Nevertheless, the function
x\mapsto(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})
is constructible, meaning that the sets of points with the same tuple are locally closed. This induces a stratification of the variety. This stratification may be nontrivial; an example is given later, Example
5.53
.

Theorem 5.38
.

Let
X
be a variety over an
algebraically closed
field
k
of characteristic
p>0
such that
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
. Let
d
be the dimension of
X/k
.
Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a regular power tower on
X
.

Then, there exists a constructible function

\displaystyle G(Y_{\bullet}):X(k)

\displaystyle\to\{\infty,0,1,2\ldots\}^{d}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}),

where the
d
-tuple
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})
is the unique one satisfying:

\widehat{f_{\infty}}:k[[t_{1}^{p^{a_{1}}},t_{2}^{p^{a_{2}}},t_{3}^{p^{a_{3}}},\ldots,t_{d}^{p^{a_{d}}}]]\to k[[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},\ldots,t_{d}]]\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}}

with
0\leq a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq a_{3}\leq\ldots\leq a_{d}\leq\infty
, where the elements
t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}
are some coordinates at the point
x
according to Theorem
5.34
.

In particular, the sets
X(k)_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})}\coloneqq G(Y_{\bullet})^{-1}((a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}))
are locally closed in
X(k)
, only finitely many of them are not empty, and their union is the set of all the closed points, i.e.,
\bigcup_{a\in\{\infty,0,1,2\ldots,\}^{d}}X(k)_{a}=X(k)
.

Remark 5.39

(Avalanche)
.

Before the proof of
5.38
, we will explain the main idea behind the proof and why it is true. The actual proof executes this idea, but it does so with some machinery that might hide the idea.

Let’s say that we have a regular power tower of length
2
with
d=4
,
r_{1}=3
, and
r_{2}=2
on
k[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}]
. Then it admits a set of saturated, well-chosen distinguished generators
5.33
:

3
in degree
1
:
G_{1},G_{2},G_{3}
and
2
in degree
p
:
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
.

Let say that the leading form
[H_{1}]
admits an invertible function in its formula in the coordinates, e.g.,
H_{1}=1\cdot\frac{1}{p!}\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial x_{4}^{p}}
, and let say that
[H_{2}]
does not, e.g.,
H_{2}=x_{1}\cdot\frac{1}{p!}\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial x_{3}^{p}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}
.
5
5

5

I do not claim that a power tower with such
H_{1},H_{2}
exists! This is simply an explanation of what is important to focus on.

Then, at every closed point
x
outside
V(x_{1})
the reductions of
G_{1},G_{2},G_{3},H_{1},H_{2}
form a set of distinguished generators for
\mathcal{D}_{|x}
. So, the tuple is
(0,0,1,2,2)
at these points.

However, if the point
x
belongs to
V(x_{1})
, then the reductions of
G_{1},G_{2},G_{3},H_{1},H_{2}
are still generators of the algebra, but not necessarily distinguished generators. Indeed, the degree of the operator
H_{2}
drops after assuming
x_{1}=0
, then
\left(\mathcal{D}_{|V(x_{1})}\right)_{1}
has greater rank than
\left(\mathcal{D}_{1}\right)_{|V(x_{1})}
.
(In general, the
p
-filtration does not commute with a restriction to a subvariety!)
However,
H_{1}
stays in degree
p
. Finally, by the proof, we will know that
\mathcal{D}_{|V(x_{1})}
admits a set of distinguished generators, so for the rank reasons it must be:

4
in degree
1
:
G^{\prime}_{1},G^{\prime}_{2},G^{\prime}_{3},G^{\prime}_{4}
and
1
in degree
p
:
H^{\prime}_{1}
,

where most likely we can assume that
G^{\prime}_{1},G^{\prime}_{2},G^{\prime}_{3}
are reductions of
G_{1},G_{2},G_{3}
and
H^{\prime}_{1}
is the reduction of
H_{1}
, and then there is an extra
G^{\prime}_{4}
that emerges somehow from
H_{2}
modulo
x_{1}
interacting with operators of order
<p
taken modulo
x_{1}
too. Consequently, the tuple is
(0,1,1,1,2)
for all points
x\in V(x_{1})
.

In this explanation, a part of one of the twos fell into the lower order after
x_{1}=0
. Such cascades explain all changes in the stratification. And, if the power tower’s length is great, there could be many of them co-occurring, and we could call this phenomenon an avalanche. This is a picturesque explanation, I like!

In a less poetic language, there is an order on partitions, our tuples. We can only get a smaller or equal partition after restricting our power tower to a subvariety, and whether we got a smaller one depends solely on the leading forms of saturated distinguished generators. It is independent of the generators chosen.

Proof.

First, we prove that the function
G(Y_{\bullet})
is well defined. Indeed, let
x\in X(k)
be a closed point. Then, according to Theorem
5.34
, there exist some coordinates
t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}
at
x
such that we have

\widehat{f_{\infty}}:k[[t_{1}^{p^{a_{1}}},t_{2}^{p^{a_{2}}},t_{3}^{p^{a_{3}}},\ldots,t_{d}^{p^{a_{d}}}]]\to k[[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},\ldots,t_{d}]]\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}}.

Let
m\geq 0
be an integer. By observing that
\widehat{f_{m}}
is given by

\widehat{f_{m}}:\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{Y_{m},y_{m}}}\simeq k[[t_{1}^{p^{\operatorname{min}(m,a_{1})}},\ldots,t_{d}^{p^{\operatorname{min}(m,a_{d})}}]]\to k[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}]]\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}}

and
k[[t_{1}^{p^{\operatorname{min}(m,a_{1})}},\ldots,t_{d}^{p^{\operatorname{min}(m,a_{d})}}]]
is a “composite” of the complete rings
k[[t_{1}^{p^{a_{1}}},\ldots,t_{d}^{p^{a_{d}}}]]
and
k[[t_{1}^{p^{m}},\ldots,t_{d}^{p^{m}}]]
,
thus we conclude that the indices
a_{i}
are determined by the dimensions of
k[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}]]
over
k[[t_{1}^{p^{\operatorname{min}(m,a_{1})}},\ldots,t_{d}^{p^{\operatorname{min}(m,a_{d})}}]]
that are finite, and they do not depend on the coordinates. Therefore, the indices
a_{i}
do not depend on the coordinates. This proves that the function
G(Y_{\bullet})
is well defined.

Next, we prove that
\bigcup_{a\in\{\infty,0,1,2\ldots,\}^{d}}X(k)_{a}=X(k)
. This is a property of every function: the union of all inverse images is the whole domain.

Finally, we proceed to prove that the function
G
is constructible.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a saturated subalgebra of differential operators corresponding to
Y_{\bullet}
, Theorem
5.23
. And let
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
be its saturated Jacobson sequence. We know that
\mathcal{D}_{n}=\operatorname{Diff}_{Y_{n}}(X)
, and that Unpacking
5.32
is true for
\mathcal{D}
.

Let
Z\subset X
be a closed subscheme/subvariety.
We will prove that a version of Unpacking works for the pullback
\mathcal{D}_{|Z}
of
\mathcal{D}
to
Z
. This will be enough to prove that the graded (commutative) algebra (!)
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}_{|Z}
admits generators that look like distinguished ones. (
\mathcal{D}_{|Z}
is not an algebra, just a module.) This will be enough to conclude that generically
\mathcal{D}_{|Z}
allows only for one tuple, which means that
G
is constructible, because outside the locus of vanishing of any of (the leading forms of) these generators, all is the same at every point.

We can assume that
X,Y_{1},\ldots
are affine and given by rings
A,B_{1},B_{2},\ldots
. Let
Z
be a subvariety of
X
corresponding to a ring
C
. Let
N\geq 1
be an integer.

First, by the definition we have
\mathcal{D}_{N|Z}=\operatorname{Diff}_{B_{N}}(A,A)\otimes C
. And, by Proposition
2.79
, we have that
\operatorname{Diff}_{B_{N}}(A,A)\otimes C=\operatorname{Diff}_{B_{N}}(A,C)
,
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A,A)\otimes C=\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A,C)
, and the inclusion
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{B_{N}}(A,C)\subset\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A,C)
factorizes through
{S}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{*})^{*gr}
, where
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\mathcal{D}_{N}\cap\operatorname{Der}_{k}(A,C)=\operatorname{Der}_{B_{N}}(A,C)=\operatorname{Der}_{B_{1}}(A,C)
.
We define
\mathcal{F}_{i}\coloneqq\operatorname{Der}_{B_{i}}(B_{i-1},C)
, for
i\geq 1
.
By iterating Proposition
2.79
, we can obtain the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{B_{N}}(A,C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{*})^{*gr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A,C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{B_{N}}(A,C)/(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{*})\simeq\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{B_{N}}(B_{1},C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S}(\mathcal{F}_{2}^{*})^{*gr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{*})^{*gr}/(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{B_{N}}(B_{1},C)/((\mathcal{F}_{2})^{*})\simeq\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{B_{N}}(B_{2},C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S}(\mathcal{F}_{3}^{*})^{*gr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S}(\mathcal{F}_{2}^{*})^{*gr}/((\mathcal{F}_{2})^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}

By Proposition
2.72
, we can check the surjectivity of the vertical maps before taking
\operatorname{gr}
and by replacing
\operatorname{Diff}
with
\operatorname{End}
. The surjectivity is clear using
\operatorname{End}
.

The number
N
was just a scaffolding to use
\operatorname{End}
instead of
\operatorname{Diff}
. We can choose it in an arbitrary way. We will use it to get a basis for the whole
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}_{|Z}
.

Now, we can repeat the proof of Proposition
5.33
, or
4.23
: for each
m\geq 1
we take a basis of
G_{1}^{m},\ldots,G_{r_{m}}^{m}
of
\mathcal{F}_{m}
, and we take their preimages from
\gamma_{p^{m-1}}\mathcal{F}_{1}\subset\mathcal{D}_{N|Z}
for
N>m
. This is possible because of the basic arithmetic of divided power rings.

The set
G=\{G_{1}^{m},\ldots,G_{r_{m}}^{m}\}_{m\geq 1}
is like a set of leading forms of a set distinguished generators (we can call any its preimage
G^{\prime}
with respect to grading “
a set of distinguished generators
G^{\prime}
for
\mathcal{D}_{|Z}
”), and by an “Unpacking” along the vertical lines they determine the dimensions of free modules
\mathcal{F}_{i}
, so
if we take them modulo another ideal, corresponding to
Z_{1}\subset Z
. All of them stay nonzero, then they will keep this role for
\mathcal{D}_{|Z_{1}}
and its
\mathcal{F}_{i}
’s (in this proof,
\mathcal{F}_{i}
’s depended on
\mathcal{D}
and
Z
). Generators will stay generators. Bases will stay bases.

In particular, it is true for closed points, where we will get that
r_{i}=\operatorname{dim}_{k}(\mathbb{L}_{i})
, where
\mathbb{L}_{i}
follows the notation from the proof of
5.34
. Consequently, there is an open subset, namely,
U\coloneqq Z\setminus\bigcup_{i,m}V(G^{i}_{m})\subset Z
, where
V(G^{i}_{m})
is the vanishing locus of
G^{i}_{m}
, such that all points
x\in U(k)
have the same value
G(x)
that is determined by integers
r_{1},r_{2},\ldots
.
∎

Question 5.40
.

Can we use power towers to study partitions?

Or partitions to study power towers?

Proposition 5.41
.

In Theorem
5.38
, if the function
G(Y_{\bullet})
is constant, then the value depends only on the degrees
p^{r_{i+1}}=[K(Y_{i}):K(Y_{i+1})]
for
i\geq 0
.

In particular, the tuple is equal to
d-r_{1}
of zeros,
r_{1}-r_{2}
of ones,
r_{2}-r_{3}
of threes, and so on till we reach for the first time the moment
r_{m}=r_{m+1}=\ldots
. Then, we have
r_{m-1}-r_{m}
of
m-1
, and finally
r=r_{m}
of
\infty
.

Moreover, the same formula works for the generic value of the function
G
on a subvariety
Z\subset X
, where we take
r_{i}
to be the ranks of
\operatorname{Der}_{Y_{i}}(Y_{i-1},Z)
.

Proof.

If the function is constant, then any set of saturated distinguished generators remains a set of saturated distinguished generators at every closed point, since the number of generators from each degree
p^{m}
determines the tuple, thus the tuple is constant, and given by the values in the statement.

The “moreover” part is proved in the same way by using “a set of distinguished generators
G
for
\mathcal{D}_{|Z}
” from the proof of Theorem
5.38
instead of
G
for
\mathcal{D}
.
∎

Remark 5.42

(Visualization is… Natural?)
.

The proof of Theorem
5.38
implies that every reduction
\operatorname{D}_{|Z}
of a saturated subalgebra
\operatorname{D}
of the algebra of differential operators
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
to a subscheme
Z
of
X
, assuming that
\mathcal{D}
corresponds to a regular power tower, admits a set of distinguished generators. In other words, the visualization
1
of a subalgebra
\operatorname{Diff}_{W_{\bullet}}(K)
is true for all modules
\operatorname{Diff}_{Y_{\bullet}}(X,Z)
. This seems to be a deep fundamental property of “regular
\operatorname{Diff}
objects”. I do not know how far this observation goes, and I do not know how to formulate it better.

However, it is a promising observation because power towers are close to being infinitesimal groupoids, and this observation strengthens that. It shows that structures on
\operatorname{Diff}
pullback quite well. Likely, power towers are avatars of something more categorical.

Remark 5.43
.

We can combine Theorem
5.28
: every purely inseparable morphism is a regular power tower away from a codim
2
subset (that depends on the morphism), and Theorem
5.38
: regular power towers induce stratifications. The result is:

Every purely inseparable morphism induces a stratification of its source away from a codim
2
subset that depends on the morphism. Each piece is determined by the local structure of the purely inseparable morphism according to the formal Frobenius theorem for PTs
5.34

5.6.
Ekedahl Power Towers

In this section, we study saturated subalgebras of differential operators that remain saturated after taking the gradation with respect to orders. Not all subalgebras satisfy this; therefore, we introduce names for those that do. This leads to gr-saturated subalgebras, and their more loose version we call
Ekedahl power towers
. We provide criteria. We compute not-examples.

Example 5.44
.

We provide a simple example of how gradation can disturb being saturated.

Let
G=\mathbb{Z}e_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{2}
be an abelian group. It admits a filtration
F_{0}G\coloneqq 0
,
F_{1}G\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}e_{1}\oplus 0
,
F_{2}G\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}e_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{2}
.

A free subgroup
M=\mathbb{Z}(e_{1}+2e_{2})\subset G
is saturated in
G
, however its gradation with respect to the filtration
F
is equal
\operatorname{gr}M=2[e_{2}]
, which is not saturated inside
\operatorname{gr}G=\mathbb{Z}[e_{1}]\oplus\mathbb{Z}[e_{2}]
.

5.6.1.
Definition and Criteria

Definition 5.45
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
.
Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
X
, and let
\mathcal{D}
be the saturated subalgebra of differential operators that corresponds to it.

We say that the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
is
gr-saturated
if the sheaf
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}
is saturated in
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
, i.e., the quotient
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)/\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}
is torsion-free.

Example 5.46
.

Every
1
-foliation is gr-saturated.
If the length of a power tower is at least
2
, then it may not be gr-saturated.

Therefore, being gr-saturated is a meaningful notion only for exponents
>1
.

Definition 5.47
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
.
Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
X
.

We say that the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
is an
Ekedahl power tower
on
X
if there is a big open subset
U\subset X
, i.e.,
X\setminus U
is of codimension at least
2
, such that the power tower
Y_{\bullet}\cap U
on
U
is gr-saturated.

Remark 5.48
.

An Ekedahl
n
-foliation is an
n
-foliation that is an Ekedahl power tower.

Remark 5.49
.

We named power towers on a variety
X
that are gr-saturated away from a codimension
\geq 2
closed subset
“Ekedahl”
to honor Torsten Ekedahl’s contributions to the theory of
n
-foliations, mostly present in the paper
[
Eke87
]
.
In that paper, he defined “foliations of height
n
” on
X
, which we can reinterpret in our vocabulary in the following way:

Ekedahl’s “foliations of height
n
” on
X
= gr-saturated regular
n
-foliations on
X
,

and he defined “foliations of height
\infty
” to be gr-saturated regular
\infty
-foliations on
X
. Our Ekedahl power towers are a slight generalization of his notion that is compatible with how he uses it in his proofs. Indeed, in his paper, he primarily works with “singular foliations of height
n
” defined as purely inseparable morphisms that are ”foliations of height
n
” away from a codimension
\geq 2
closed subset. Therefore, we took his working definition and put it into a proper definition, in which we dropped the assumption of being an
n
-foliation or regular. The result is a notion that is much more versatile.

So, the adjectives
regular
and
gr-saturated
are separated, though they will often come together.

Lemma 5.50
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
.
Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a
n
-foliation on
X
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
be its Jacobson sequence.

Then
\frac{T_{Y_{i}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i+1}+\mathcal{G}_{i}}
for
i=1,2,\ldots,n-1
are torsion sheaves, where we put
\mathcal{G}_{i}\coloneqq T_{Y_{i}/Y_{i-1}^{(1)}}
.

Proof.

We prove that the sheaves
\frac{T_{Y_{i}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i+1}+\mathcal{G}_{i}}
for
i=1,2,\ldots,n-1
are always torsion for
n
-foliations. Indeed, we have

\left(\mathcal{F}_{i+1}\otimes K(Y_{i})\right)\oplus\left(\mathcal{G}_{i}\otimes K(Y_{i})\right)=T_{K(Y_{i})/k},

so
\frac{T_{Y_{i}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i+1}+\mathcal{G}_{i}}\otimes K(Y_{i})=0
, i.e., it is a torsion sheaf. The equality follows from two facts. First, on the generic point
K(Y_{i})
, the vector spaces
\mathcal{F}_{i+1}\otimes K(Y_{i})
and
\mathcal{G}_{i}\otimes K(Y_{i})
are disjoint by Definition
4.12
. Second, the ranks of the sheaves
\mathcal{F}_{i+1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{i}
are complementary, because
Y_{\bullet}
is a
n
-foliation, Definition
4.41
.
∎

Proposition 5.51

(Criteria for Being gr-Saturated/Ekedahl)
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
.
Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a regular power tower on
X
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
be the Jacobson sequence corresponding to
Y_{\bullet}
.
Let
\mathcal{D}
be the saturated subalgebra corresponding to
Y_{\bullet}
. Then:

(1)

The following are equivalent:

(a)

The power tower
Y_{\bullet}
is gr-saturated.

(b)

For any/every set
G
is a set of saturated well-chosen distinguished generators for
\mathcal{D}
(on an open subset around any point of
X
) the submodule spanned by
[G^{m}_{j}]
inside
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
is saturated, i.e., all the submodules, for
m\geq 1
,

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{m}\coloneqq\operatorname{Span}_{A}([G^{m}_{1}],\ldots,[G^{m}_{r_{m}}])\subset\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}.

are saturated. Equivalently, each
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{m}
is saturated in
\left(\gamma_{p^{m-1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})\otimes A\right)
.

(c)

The stratification from Theorem
5.38
induced by
Y_{\bullet}
on
X
is constant, i.e., the image of
G(Y_{\bullet})
is one point.

(d)

All the subsheaves

d(Y_{i}/X^{(i)})(\mathcal{F}_{i+1})\subset T_{X^{(i)}/k}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}

are saturated for
i\geq 1
.

(e)

All the subsheaves

d(Y_{i}/Y^{(1)}_{i-1})(\mathcal{F}_{i+1})\subset d(Y_{i}/Y^{(1)}_{i-1})(T_{Y_{i}/k})=\mathcal{F}_{i}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}

are saturated for
i\geq 1
.

(2)

Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a
n
-foliation,
n<\infty
. Let
\mathcal{G}_{i}\coloneqq T_{Y_{i}/Y_{i-1}^{(1)}}
for
i\geq 1
.

(a)

The
n
-foliation
Y_{\bullet}
is gr-saturated if and only if
the quotient sheaves
\frac{T_{Y_{i}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i+1}+\mathcal{G}_{i}}
for
1\leq i<n
are zero.

(b)

The
n
-foliation
Y_{\bullet}
is an Ekedahl power tower
if and only if
the quotient sheaves
\frac{T_{Y_{i}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i+1}+\mathcal{G}_{i}}
for
1\leq i<n
are torsion with support of codimension at least
2
.

Proof.

We prove (1). The main difficulty is getting that (a) is equivalent to (b). After that, the rest will just unpack itself.

All conditions are local - it is checking torsion, so we can assume that all
X,Y_{i}
are affine, given by rings
A,B_{i}
, and the local distinguished generators are given on these rings already, not only on their localizations. Let
G
be any set of saturated well-chosen distinguished generators for
\mathcal{D}
, Proposition
5.33
.

We prove that if (a) then (b). Indeed, if
\mathcal{D}
is gr-saturated, then it is true for submodules spanned by elements from
[G]
from different orders, because

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{m}\coloneqq\operatorname{Span}_{A}([G^{m}_{1}],\ldots,[G^{m}_{r_{m}}])=\left(\gamma_{p^{m-1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})\otimes A\right)\cap\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}.

which is an intersection of two saturated submodules of
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
.

We prove that if (b) then (a). We know that the modules
\operatorname{Span}_{A}([G^{m}_{1}],\ldots,[G^{m}_{r_{m}}])
are saturated in
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
, so they are saturated in
\left(\gamma_{p^{m-1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})\otimes A\right)
, because it is a saturated submodule containing the span. Thus, if we write each of
[G^{m}_{r_{j}}]
in terms of some coordinates
x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}
for
A/k
, then, maybe after a
A
-base change of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{m}
, we can assume that
[G^{m}_{r_{j}}]
has
\frac{1}{p^{m-1}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{m-1}}}{\partial x_{c(j)}^{p^{m-1}}}
in its expansion with a coefficient from
k
that is not zero, and other
[G^{m}_{r_{i}}]
,
i\neq j
, do not have that element in their formulas, so
j\mapsto c(j)
will be injective.

Consequently, we can use that to conclude that leading forms of all monomials in
[G^{i}_{j}]
with exponents from
0
up to
p-1
, that are a basis of
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}
by Proposition
5.33
, include products of
\frac{1}{p^{m-1}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{m-1}}}{\partial x_{c(j)}^{p^{m-1}}}
with exponents from
0
up to
p-1
up to invertible function in their explicit formulas in terms of the coordinates, Corollary
2.50
and Proposition
2.48
. Therefore, there cannot be any graded operator
D\in\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
, that after multiplying by non-invertible
a\in A
gets into
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}
without being there already, because that would mean that its coefficient in front of that product of
\frac{1}{p^{m-1}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{m-1}}}{\partial x_{c(j)}^{p^{m-1}}}
would be from
\frac{1}{a}A\setminus A
, which cannot be true.

We prove (b) iff (c). The changes of values of
G(Y_{\bullet})
happen, i.e., not-(c), if and only if a set
G
stops being a set of distinguished generators after reducing it to a subscheme. This “stop” happens if and only if the rank of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{i}
drops after restriction, which is precisely not-(b). This proves not-(c) iff not-(b).

We prove (b) iff (d) iff (e). All these conditions in terms of subsheaves are equivalent, because there are some explicit isomorphisms around.

First, we focus on (b) iff (d). Let
T^{\prime}_{X^{(m)}/k}=\left(\gamma_{p^{m}}(T_{X/k})\otimes A\right)
, i.e., these are
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{i}
for the power tower of
p
-powers, Example
4.4
. We have

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{m}\subset T^{\prime}_{X^{(m)}/k}

and
\left(\gamma_{p^{m}}(T_{X/k})\otimes A\right)
is saturated in
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
. So, we have (b) iff
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{m}
is saturated in
T^{\prime}_{X^{(m)}/k}
. Now, we have an isomorphism

d(X/X^{(m)}):\frac{T^{\prime}_{X^{(m)}/k}}{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{m+1}}\simeq\frac{T_{X^{(m)}/k}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X/k}}{d(Y_{m}/X^{(m)})(\mathcal{F}_{m+1})}

that is induced by Packing, see Remark
4.25
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{m+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime}_{X^{(m)}/k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{m+1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X/k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X^{(m)}/k}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X/k},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

so the left-hand side is torsion if and only if the right-hand side is torsion.

We prove (d) iff (e). We take

\mathcal{F}_{i+1}\subset T_{Y_{i}/k}
. We apply
d(Y_{i}/Y^{(1)}_{i-1})
to it. We get

d(Y_{i}/Y^{(1)}_{i-1})(\mathcal{F}_{i+1})\to d(Y_{i}/Y^{(1)}_{i-1})(T_{Y_{i}/k})\to T_{Y^{(1)}_{i-1}/k}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}.

By Proposition
5.31
, we have that
d(Y_{i}/Y^{(1)}_{i-1})(T_{Y_{i}/k})=T_{Y^{(1)}_{i-1}/Y_{i}^{(1)}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}=\mathcal{F}_{i}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}
. Since we know that
\mathcal{F}_{1}
is saturated in
T_{X=Y_{0}/k}
, thus by an induction we can proceed to conclude that
d(Y_{i}/Y^{(1)}_{i-1})(T_{Y_{i}/k})=\mathcal{F}_{i}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}\subset T_{Y^{(1)}_{i-1}/k}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}
is a saturated subsheaf. Finally,
d(Y^{(1)}_{i-1}/X^{(i)})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}
is injective on
\mathcal{F}_{i}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}
and the image is saturated in
T_{X^{(i)}/k}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}
, so
d(Y_{i}/Y^{(1)}_{i-1})(\mathcal{F}_{i+1})
is saturated in
\mathcal{F}_{i}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}
if and only if
d(Y_{i}/X^{(i)})(\mathcal{F}_{i+1})
is saturated in
T_{X^{(i)}/k}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}
.

We prove (2). The quotient sheaves are torsion by Lemma
5.50
.

(a) implies (b), because
Y_{\bullet}
is Ekedahl if and only if
\frac{T_{Y_{i}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i+1}+\mathcal{G}_{i}}
is zero away from codimension
2
subset. So, after leaving that subset, it is enough to prove that
Y_{\bullet}
is gr-saturated if and only if
\frac{T_{Y_{i}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i+1}+\mathcal{G}_{i}}
is zero.

We can use (1): (a) iff (e) that we already proved.

Indeed,
Y_{\bullet}
is gr-saturated if and only if

d(Y_{i}/Y^{(1)}_{i-1})(\mathcal{F}_{i+1})\subset\mathcal{F}_{i}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}
are saturated for
i=1,2,\ldots,n-1
. These sheaves are of the same rank. This means they must be equal. However, the quotient satisfies:

\frac{\mathcal{F}_{i}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i}/k}}{d(Y_{i}/Y^{(1)}_{i-1})(\mathcal{F}_{i+1})}=\frac{d(Y_{i}/Y^{(1)}_{i-1})(T_{Y_{i}/k})}{d(Y_{i}/Y^{(1)}_{i-1})(\mathcal{F}_{i+1})}\simeq\frac{T_{Y_{i}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i+1}+\operatorname{ker}d(Y_{i}/Y^{(1)}_{i-1})\cap T_{Y_{i}/k}}=\frac{T_{Y_{i}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i+1}+\mathcal{G}_{i}}.

So, the equality holds if and only if
\frac{T_{Y_{i}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i+1}+\mathcal{G}_{i}}
is zero. This finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.52

(Ekedahl’s “foliations of height
n
”)
.

Let
X/k
be a
regular
variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
. Let
Y_{\bullet}
be an
n
-foliation on
X
,
n=1,2,\ldots,\infty
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
be its Jacobson sequence and let
\mathcal{D}
be its saturated subalgebra. We assume that the
1
-foliation
\mathcal{F}_{1}
is regular.

Then, the
n
-foliation is gr-saturated and regular if and only if the equality

\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}={S}({\mathcal{F}_{1}}^{*})^{*gr}\cap\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(n)}}(X)

holds.

In particular, the
\infty
-foliation is gr-saturated and regular if and only if the equality

\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}={S}({\mathcal{F}_{1}}^{*})^{*gr}

holds.

Proof.

Let it be gr-saturated and regular. For
n
-foliations, we always have
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}\subset{S}({\mathcal{F}_{1}}^{*})^{*gr}\cap\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(n)}}(X)
, see Proposition
2.79
. And the equality is true on the generic point:
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}\otimes K(X)={S}(({\mathcal{F}_{1}}\otimes K(X))^{*})^{*gr}\cap\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(n)}}(X)\otimes K(X)
, Proposition
4.44
. So, if it is gr-saturated, then they are equal.

We prove that the equality implies being gr-saturated and regular.
We show it for the finite
n
- the case of
\infty
follows from it.

Being gr-saturated is easy, both
{S}({\mathcal{F}_{1}}^{*})^{*gr}
and
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(n)}}(X)
are saturated in
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
. The first is because it is a divided power polynomial subring, and the second is almost by definition. So, the harder part is to get regularity.

We can work affine locally.
We know
\mathcal{F}_{1}
is regular. Let assume that we enough local that we can use Lemma
5.30
directly: Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
, and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{d}
be
p
-bases fo
A
over
B
and
B
over
A^{p}
, where
X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
, and
Y_{1}=\operatorname{Spec}(B)
. In particular,
X
and
Y_{1}
are regular. Together,
x_{i},y_{j}
are coordinates for
A/k
.

Let
G=\{G^{m}_{1},\ldots,G^{m}_{r}\}_{m=1,\ldots,n}\subset\mathcal{D}
be a choice of operators such that
G^{1}_{i}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}
is a basis of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, and
[G^{m}_{i}]=\gamma_{p^{m-1}}[G^{1}_{i}]
.

By Proposition
5.31
, we know
d(X/Y_{1})
exists. We can unpack:
d(X/Y_{1})\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{Diff}_{Y_{n}}(Y_{1})\otimes A
. In particular,
d(X/Y_{1})(G^{2}_{i})\in\mathcal{F}_{2}=d(X/Y_{1})\operatorname{Span}_{A}(G^{2}_{1},\ldots,G^{2}_{r})\cap T_{Y_{1}/k}
. And,
\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}
restricts to be derivations on
Y_{1}
. They are a basis of
T_{Y_{1}/X^{(1)}}
. Consequently,
\mathcal{F}_{2}\oplus T_{Y_{1}/X^{(1)}}=T_{Y_{1}/k}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}_{2}
is a regular
1
-foliation. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}_{Y_{n}}(Y_{1})\otimes A=d(X/Y_{1})\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle=d(X/Y_{1})({S}({\mathcal{F}_{1}}^{*})^{*gr}\cap\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(n)}}(X))

\displaystyle={S}({\mathcal{F}_{2}}^{*})^{*gr}\otimes A\cap\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{Y_{1}^{(n-1)}}(Y_{1}))\otimes A

\displaystyle=({S}({\mathcal{F}_{2}}^{*})^{*gr}\cap\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{Y_{1}^{(n-1)}}(Y_{1})))\otimes A,

but
A
is faithfully flat over
B
, so
\operatorname{Diff}_{Y_{n}}(Y_{1})={S}({\mathcal{F}_{2}}^{*})^{*gr}\cap\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{Y_{1}^{(n-1)}}(Y_{1})
. Consequently, we can repeat the reasoning for
\mathcal{F}_{3}
, then for
\mathcal{F}_{4}
, and so on till
\mathcal{F}_{n}
. This proves that
Y_{\bullet}
is regular.
∎

5.6.2.
Not-Ekedahl Power Tower

In this section, we describe a
2
-foliation that is not Ekedahl. Moreover, we draw pictures to visualize this
2
-foliation.

Example 5.53

(A non-Ekedahl
2
-foliation on a variety.)
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
X=\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}=\operatorname{Spec}(k[x,y])
.

Let
Y_{1}=\operatorname{Spec}(k[x^{p},y])
,
Y_{2}=\operatorname{Spec}(k[x^{p^{2}},y^{p},y^{p+1}-x^{p}])
.

Then
X,Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{2},Y_{2},\ldots
is a
2
-foliation on
X
, and its Jacobson sequence is:

\mathcal{F}_{1}
is spanned by the vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
on
X
,

\mathcal{F}_{2}
by spanned by the vector field
y^{p}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
on
Y_{1}
,

and
T_{Y_{1}/k}
is spanned by
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
.

Also,
\mathcal{G}_{1}=T_{Y_{1}/X^{(1)}}
is spanned by the vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
on
Y_{1}
.

By the definition of a Jacobson sequence, we have that the vector fields
y^{p}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
on
Y_{1}
are perpendicular on the generic point. However, this is not true at every closed point of
Y_{1}
.
Indeed, on the vanishing locus
V(y)
, i.e., if
y=0
, then we have

y^{p}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial y}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
, so they are equal and therefore parallel on this locus. Now, we can observe that the locus where these two generators are parallel to each other is precisely
\frac{T_{Y_{1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{2}+\mathcal{G}_{1}}
, so in our case, its support is of codimension
1
. Therefore, by Proposition
5.51
, the
2
-foliation
f_{2}:X\to Y_{2}
is not Ekedahl.

The figures
3
and
4
contain pictures (These pictures were made with Wolfram Cloud
https://www.wolframcloud.com/
.) of the above vector fields with an extra image for the support of
\frac{T_{Y_{1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{2}+\mathcal{G}_{1}}
.

Furthermore, it is worth to notice that the sheaves
T_{Y_{1}/k},\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathcal{G}_{1}
, and
\frac{T_{Y_{1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{2}+\mathcal{G}_{1}}
fit the following diagram, where we put
R=k[x^{p},y]
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R/y^{p}R=\frac{T_{Y_{1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{2}+\mathcal{G}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R=\mathcal{G}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}}\oplus R\frac{\partial}{\partial y}=T_{Y_{1}/k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R=\mathcal{F}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\mapsto(0;1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\mapsto(y^{p};1)}

Finally, if
k
is algebraically closed, then the stratification from Theorem
5.38
for this power tower is given by

\displaystyle X(k)_{(0,2)}

\displaystyle=\{(x,y):\ y\neq 0\}\subset k\times k=X(k),

\displaystyle X(k)_{(1,1)}

\displaystyle=\{(x,y):\ y=0\}\subset k\times k=X(k).

In other words, the function
G(Y_{\bullet}):k^{2}\to\{\infty,0,1,2,\ldots\}^{2}
is given by

G(Y_{\bullet})(x,y)=\begin{cases}(0,2),\ \text{if $y\neq 0$}\\
(1,1),\ \text{if $y=0$}\end{cases}.

Consequently, it is not constant, which is another reason why it is not an Ekedahl power tower, Proposition
5.51
.

.

k[x^{p^{2}},y^{p},y^{p+1}-x^{p}]\to k[x,y]
.
