.
A visualization of being non-Ekedahl for
k[x^{p^{2}},y^{p},y^{p+1}-x^{p}]\to k[x,y]
.

Remark 5.54
.

In general, it is true, that working with power towers on surfaces is (essentially) equivalent to working with a series of vector fields (if we discard the first (boring?) part of
p
-powers,
p
-powers,… and we only focus on the tail that is a
n
-foliation, for
n\in\{0,1,2,\ldots,\infty\}
). (See Proposition
4.9
.)

Therefore, one can draw pictures, like the ones in
3
and
4
, of these vector fields and
see
the geometrical interactions between different
Y_{i}
.

5.7.
Morphisms and Power Towers

Subfields were a good source of power towers. In this section, we use morphisms to get power towers. We primarily focus on fibrations and smooth morphisms.

5.7.1.
Basic Definitions

Definition 5.55
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a dominant morphism, or a dominant rational map over
k
.
Then, the power tower on
X
that corresponds to the power tower of
K(Y)
on
K(X)
is called
the power tower of
f
on
X
. We denote this power tower by
\left(f_{1}:X\to Y_{1},f_{2}:X\to Y_{2},\ldots\right)
,
f_{\bullet}
, or
Y_{\bullet}
.

Definition 5.56
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
.
Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
X
.

We say that the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
is
algebraically integrable
if there is a dominant rational map
f:X\to Z
such that
Y_{\bullet}=Z_{\bullet}
.

Remark 5.57
.

It is generally hard to reconstruct a nice morphism from an algebraically integrable power tower. This is similar to the GIT theory’s study of quotients. The similarities for foliations are discussed in more detail in Bongiorno’s preprint
[
Bon21
]
. Power towers are analogous to foliations, so much of this discussion carries over to power towers of infinite length.

On the other hand, the generic point could be figured out completely, Theorem
4.36
. So, the problem is in “spreading out” what we know at the generic point to rings.

Definition 5.58
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
.
Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a power tower on
X
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be the saturated subalgebra of differential operators that corresponds to
Y_{\bullet}
.

Then, the
sheaf of first integral
of
Y_{\bullet}
is defined by

\mathcal{O}_{Y_{\infty}/k}(U)\coloneqq\{f\in\mathcal{O}_{X/k}(U):\ \forall_{D\in\mathcal{D}(U)}[D,f]=0\}=\operatorname{const}(\mathcal{D}(U)),

where
U\subset X
is an open subset.

5.7.2.
Fibrations Inject into Power Towers

In this section, we define fibrations and prove that a particular class of fibrations is determined by its rational equivalence class.

The following definition is a version of
[
Voi21
, Definition 0.1.]
, which is a definition of a fibration due to Claire Voisin from her survey paper. However, we dropped an assumption “
X
is a projective/compact complex variety” to allow affine fibrations.
I did this because I want the projections
\mathbb{A}^{n}\to\mathbb{A}^{m}
for
n\geq m
to be fibrations, as I consider these maps to be a basic example of what a fibration is, since in differential geometry every fibration is such locally.

Definition 5.59
.

Let
k
be a field.
A (rational)
fibration
on a variety

X/k
is a dominant (rational) map
X\to Y
with connected general fiber, where
Y/k
is a variety.

Definition 5.60
.

Let
X
be a variety over a field
k
. Let
f:X\to Y
be a fibration on
X
. Let
P
be a property of a morphism of varieties, e.g., surjective, flat, faithfully flat, smooth. We say that the fibration
f
is
P
if the morphism
f
is
P
.

Definition 5.61
.

Let
X
be a scheme over a field
k
. Let
f:X\to Y
be a dominant morphism over
k
.

•

We say that
f
is
separably closed
if
K(Y)
is separably closed in
K(X)
, i.e.,
K(Y)=K(Y)^{s}\subset K(X)
,

•

We say that
f
is
separable
if the field extension
K(X)/K(Y)
is separable, Definition
2.13
.

Remark 5.62
.

I am aware that the definition
5.61
is clumsy, and there are probably better words to be used, or these things already have names. If so, send it to me.

Definition 5.63
.

Let
g:X\to Y
and
g:X\to Y^{\prime}
be morphisms between varieties over a field
k
. We say that
f
and
g
are
rationally equivalent
if there exists a commutative diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}_{V}}

where the maps
j_{U},j_{V},j^{\prime}_{V}
are nonempty open immersions.

Proposition 5.64
.

Let
X,Y,Y^{\prime}
be varieties over a field
k
.
Let
f:X\to Y
and
g:X\to Y^{\prime}
be faithfully flat, separable, dominant morphisms of finite type.

If
f
and
g
are rationally equivalent, then there exists an isomorphism
\rho:Y\to Y^{\prime}
such that
\rho\circ f=g
.

Proof.

First, we prove that a closed subscheme
X\times_{Y}X\subset X\times X
is a subvariety. To do this, it is enough to prove that it is reduced.

We prove that
X\times_{Y}X
is reduced. Being reduced is a local property; therefore, we can check it on an open affine covering of
X\times_{Y}X
. We can produce a convenient covering by observing that
f
is flat and of finite type, therefore it is open. Let
U=\operatorname{Spec}(B)
be an open affine subset of
X
, then the restriction of
f
to this open subset defines a morphism

f:U\to f(U),

where
f(U)
is an open subscheme of
Y
. Now, since
Y
is separated, it means that this morphism is affine, see e.g.
[
hl
]
. Therefore, if
\operatorname{Spec}(A)\subset f(U)
is an open affine subset, then
f^{-1}(\operatorname{Spec}(A))
is an open affine subset of
U
. Consequently, we can conclude that there is an open affine covering
\operatorname{Spec}(A_{i})
of
Y
, for some indices
i
, such that the set
f^{-1}(\operatorname{Spec}(A_{i}))=\operatorname{Spec}(B_{i})
is an open affine covering of
X
.
Now, the set
\operatorname{Spec}(B_{i})\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(A_{i})}\operatorname{Spec}(B_{i})
is an open affine covering of
X\times_{Y}X
. Consequently, it is enough to prove that
X\times_{Y}X
is reduced, assuming that
X
and
Y
are affine.

Let
X,Y
be affine schemes corresponding to rings
B
and
A
respectively. Let
f^{\#}:A\to B
be the ring map corresponding to
f
.
Then, there exists a nonzero
c\in A
and algebraically independent over
A_{c}
elements
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in B_{c}
such that

A_{c}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\subset B_{c}

is a finite étale ring extension, i.e., it is a smooth ring map. Indeed, since
(A)\subset(B)
is separable, then there is an open subset
U
of
Y
such that
f:f^{-1}(U)\to U
is smooth by
[
MO
, Chapter V, Corollary 4.3.]
. So, we can take
U=Y-V(c)
, and then we have the above decomposition by
[
Sta18
, Lemma 00T7]
.

Now, we can conclude that we have an inclusion

B\otimes_{A}B\hookrightarrow\left(B\otimes_{A}B\right)_{c}\simeq B_{c}\otimes_{A_{c}}B_{c}.

Indeed, the inclusion follows from the element
c
not being a zero divisor on
B\otimes_{A}B
, so the localizing
c
is an inclusion. (This is an element of this ring as
c\coloneqq c\otimes 1=1\otimes c
.) The
c
is not a zero divisor, because we have that the short exact sequence

0\to B\xrightarrow{c}B\to B/cB\to 0

tensored with
B
over
A
is still exact, by
A\to B
being flat, and equal to the short exact sequence

0\to B\otimes_{A}B\xrightarrow{c}B\otimes_{A}B\to B/cB\otimes_{A}B\to 0.

The isomorphism follows from an elementary property of tensoring:

B_{c}\otimes_{A_{c}}B_{c}\simeq(B\otimes_{A}A_{c})\otimes_{A_{c}}(B\otimes_{A}A_{c})\simeq B\otimes_{A}B\otimes_{A}A_{c}\simeq\left(B\otimes_{A}B\right)_{c}.

From this inclusion, we can deduce that
B\otimes_{A}B
is reduced if
B_{c}\otimes_{A_{c}}B_{c}
is reduced. The last statement can be directly computed from the above form for
B_{c}/A_{c}
. This proves that
X\times_{Y}X
is reduced, and therefore a variety.

Next, let the following be a diagram given by
f
and
g
being rationally equivalent to each other.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}_{V}}

We have that
U\times_{V}U\subset X\times_{Y}X
is an open dense subset. Now, since
X\times_{Y}X
is a closed subvariety of
X\times X
, we get that the schematic closure
\overline{U\times_{V}U}
of
U\times_{V}U
in
X\times X
is equal
X\times_{Y}X
. This reasoning also applies to
X\times_{Y^{\prime}}X
. Consequently, we have the following equalities of groupoids on
X
:

X\times_{Y}X=\overline{U\times_{V}U}=X\times_{Y^{\prime}}X.

Finally, we know that
f,g
are fpqc coverings since they are faithfully flat morphisms between varieties, so these morphisms are effective epimorphisms
by
[
Sta18
, Lemma 023Q]
. This means precisely that
Y
is a categorical quotient of
X\times_{Y}X
and
Y^{\prime}
is a categorical quotient of
X\times_{Y^{\prime}}X
. Now, we know that these groupoids are equal. Therefore, from the universal property of a groupoid quotient, there is an isomorphism
\rho:Y\to Y^{\prime}
such that
\rho\circ f=g
. This finishes the proof.
∎

The above was a step towards the following.

Theorem 5.65

(Flat Separable Fibrations into
\infty
-Foliations)
.

Let
X
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
.

Then, there is an injection from a set of flat, separably closed, separable, surjective fibrations on
X
up to rational equivalence to
\infty
-foliations on
X
.

In particular, if
f:X\to Y
is a flat, separably closed, separable, surjective fibration on
X
, then the first integrals of the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
are equal to
f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y/k}
.

Proof.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a flat separably closed separable surjective fibration on
X
.
This morphism is faithfully flat, because it is flat and surjective. It is dominant because it is surjective. And,
X,Y
are varieties over
k
, so
f
is of finite type. Consequently, we can use Proposition
5.64
to conclude that the operation

f:X\to Y\mapsto k\subset K(Y)\subset K(X)

is injective precisely up to rational equivalence of flat separably closed separable surjective fibrations on
X
.

Next, by the assumptions on
f
, we have that
K(Y)
is separably closed in
K(X)
, i.e.,
K(Y)=K(Y)^{s}
, and the field extension
K(X)/K(Y)
is separable. Therefore, by Proposition
4.49
, the subfield
K(Y)\subset K(X)
corresponds to the power tower of
K(Y)
on
K(X)
that is a
\infty
-foliation on
K(X)
.

Finally, by Lemma
5.10
, power towers on
X
and on
K(X)
are equivalent, thus
f
injects into power towers on
X
and its image is a
\infty
-foliation on
X
. This proves the injection.

Now, we prove that the sheaf of first integrals equals
f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y/k}
. We can observe that the groupoid
X\times_{Y}X
admits a quotient and it is equal to
f
, by
[
Sta18
, Lemma 023Q]
. This means that
Y
represents the sheaf of invariant functions for this groupoid,
[
Sta18
, Section 02VE]
. By the definition, a local section
g\in\mathcal{O}_{X/k}(U)
is invariant with respect to
X\times_{Y}X
, i.e., it belongs to
f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y/k}(U)
, if and only if
g
commutes with
\operatorname{Diff}_{Y}{X}(U)
, i.e., it is a first integral of the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
, Definition
4.35
. This finishes the proof.
∎

5.7.3.
Smooth Morphisms Inject into Power Towers

Proposition 5.66

(Smooth Fibrations into gr-Saturated Regular
\infty
-Foliations)
.

Let
X
be a regular variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
.
Let
Y
be a regular variety over
k
.
Let
f:X\to Y
be a smooth surjective morphism such that
K(Y)=K(Y)^{s}\subset K(X)
.

Then, the power tower of
f
is a gr-saturated regular
\infty
-foliation.

Moreover, the sheaf of first integrals of the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
is equal to the pullback sheaf

f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y/k}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X/k}.

Therefore, there is an injection from separably closed smooth surjective morphisms
X\to Y
with
Y
regular into gr-saturated regular
\infty
-foliations on
X
.

Proof.

A separably closed smooth surjective morphism is a flat separably closed separable surjective fibration, so, by Theorem
5.65
, we conclude that the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
of
f:X\to Y
is a
\infty
-foliation, the sheaf of first integrals is equal
f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y/k}
, and we have an injection from separably closed smooth surjective morphisms into
\infty
-foliations.
Therefore, the only thing that remains to be proved is that the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
is gr-saturated and regular.

First, we prove that
Y_{\bullet}
is gr-saturated. This is a local question, so we can assume that
X
and
Y
are affine. The morphism
f:X\to Y
is smooth, so, by
[
Sta18
, Theorem 039Q.]
, we can assume that
X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
,
Y=\operatorname{Spec}(B)
, and that there exist
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in A
such that we have

B\subset B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\subset A,

where
B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
is isomorphic to a polynomial ring, and the inclusion of rings
B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\subset A
is étale. Moreover,
Y
is regular over a perfect field
k
, so it is smooth by
[
Sta18
, Lemma 038V (3), Lemma 038X]
. So, we can apply
[
Sta18
, Theorem 039Q.]
again, this time to
k\to B
, meaning that there exist
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\in B
such that

k\subset k[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}]\subset B

with
k[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}]\subset B
being étale.
Now, the set
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
is a set of coordinates for
A/k
. Therefore, by using explicit formulas for differential operators, we conclude that
\operatorname{Diff}_{B}(A)=\operatorname{Diff}_{Y_{\bullet}}(X)
is generated by
\frac{1}{n!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial x_{i}^{n}}
. This observation implies the power tower is gr-saturated as
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{B}(A)
is generated by all
\left[\frac{1}{n!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial x_{i}^{n}}\right]
and the whole graded algebra
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(A)
by all
\left[\frac{1}{n!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial x_{i}^{n}}\right]
and all
\left[\frac{1}{n!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial y_{j}^{n}}\right]
.

Now, we prove that the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
is regular. First,
X
is regular based on the assumption. So, we only have to prove that all subsheaves
\mathcal{F}_{i}\subset T_{Y_{i-1}/k}
are subbundles. We put
Y_{i}=\operatorname{Spec}(B_{i})
and use the previous paragraph’s assumptions. We have that
B_{i}
is equal to the integral closure of
B[x_{1}^{p^{i}},\ldots,x_{n}^{p^{i}}]
in
(B[x_{1}^{p^{i}},\ldots,x_{n}^{p^{i}}])^{s}\subset(A)=K(X)
by the Galois-type Correspondences
5.23
. In particular, the rings
B_{i}
are regular with coordinates
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m},x_{1}^{p^{i}},\ldots,x_{n}^{p^{i}}
, and therefore we have, for
i\geq 0
,

\mathcal{F}_{i+1}=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}^{p^{i}}},\ldots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}^{p^{i}}})\subset\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}^{p^{i}}},\ldots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}^{p^{i}}},\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{m}})=T_{Y_{i}/k}.

Consequently, all of them are subbundles, meaning that
\operatorname{Sing}(Y_{\bullet})=\emptyset
. This finishes the proof.
∎

6.
Foliations,
1
-Foliations, and
\infty
-Foliations

The main source of power towers so far has been subfields and morphisms. In particular, if we are given a dominant map
f:X\to Y
, then we can take its
power tower
X\to Y_{1}\to Y_{2}\to\ldots
, where
Y_{i}
is an approximation of
Y
up to
p^{i}
-powers. We showed that nice classes of morphisms and subfields give rise to nice power towers. However, we can reverse this procedure. This leads to a question:

How to grow power towers?

Indeed, we can start with a
p
-Lie algebra/
1
-foliation on
X
, that is equivalent to
X\to Y_{1}\to X^{(1)}
, and then… what do we do next? How to get more
Y_{2},Y_{3},\ldots
for this
Y_{1}
systematically? Is it even always possible to continue, or maybe there are power towers that cannot be prolonged? If so, what is the obstruction? In general, these questions lead to:

Question 6.1
.

What is the moduli of power towers?

The above question is not necessarily tricky, but it is definitely perplexing. Consequently, this chapter will focus on much more minor problems.

Problem 1:
Given a
n
-foliation on
X
, can we find
(n+1)
-foliation on
X
that extends it?

This question depends only on
K(X)
, which is about fields. The answer is: yes, it is always possible to take an
n
-foliation:
K=W_{0},W_{1},\ldots,W_{n}
, and to find
W_{n+1}
such that
K=W_{0},W_{1},\ldots,W_{n},W_{n+1}
is an
(n+1)
-foliation. This is proved in Theorem
6.4
.

However, if we want to make it about a problem about varieties, then we have to add a variety-only adjective about power towers to the problem.

Problem 2:
Given a
regular/gr-saturated/Ekedahl

n
-foliation on
X
, can we find
regular/gr-saturated/Ekedahl

(n+1)
-foliation on
X
that extends it?

Now, it turns out that the answer might be negative! It was already shown by Ekedahl: there exists a variety
X
with a
1
-foliation on
X
given by
f_{1}:X\to Y_{1}
such that there is no Ekedahl
2
-foliation on
X
given by
f_{1}:X\to Y_{1},f_{2}:X\to Y_{2}
.
Indeed, in the paper
[
Eke87
, pages 145-146]
, Ekedahl constructed a “
p
-cyclic cover of an abelian surface” and showed that this
1
-foliation cannot be extended to any Ekedahl
2
-foliation.

Curiously,
1
-foliations that are “
p
-cyclic covers of abelian surfaces” tend to be counterexamples to many statements that are inspired by characteristic zero. Indeed, the same non-extendable
1
-foliations from Ekedahl’s paper show up in Miyaoka’s paper
[
Miy87
]
, and they are counterexamples to a “bend and break for special
1
-foliations” there.
These
1
-foliations are also counterexamples to bend and break results from Langer’s paper
[
Lan21
, Section 5.1.]
.
And, another example is Bernasconi’s preprint
[
Ber24
]
in which he shows that the main ingredients of a minimal model program fail for
1
-foliations. His counterexamples are again “
p
-cyclic covers of abelian surfaces”, i.e.,
1
-foliations that do not extend to Ekedahl
2
-foliations.

Consequently, I believe that a problem of studying extensions of
1
-foliations to Ekedahl
n
-foliations and Ekedahl
\infty
-foliations is vital for positive characteristic algebraic geometry.

For example, Miyaoka already studied this problem in his paper
[
Miy87
]
. Namely, I interpret his paper that he (not explicitly) uses a fact that we prove in the below Theorem
6.9
, that a lifting of a
1
-foliation to a foliation induces a unique extension to an Ekedahl
\infty
-foliation. Indeed, my understanding is that he actually performed “deformations along
special
Ekedahl
\infty
-foliations”, but he did not have a theory of such objects, so he manipulated them using the liftings instead of interacting with them directly.

Therefore, Miyaoka’s paper
[
Miy87
]
is the inspiration for the main result of this chapter, Theorem
6.9
: liftings of
1
-foliations to foliations extend them to nice
\infty
-foliations. So, there is an interaction between moduli of foliations and moduli of power towers.

6.1.
Extensions of
n
-Foliations on Fields

We prove that every
1
-foliation extends to a
n
-foliation for
n=2,3,\ldots,\infty
.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be the perfection of
K
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension.

Let
(K,W_{1},W_{2},\ldots)
be a power tower of finite length (
\leq n
) on
K
, then we denote it by
W=W_{n}
, i.e., by the subfield it corresponds to. The index is at least the length, if we write the index, then we assume that the length is not greater then that.

We say that a power tower
W_{\bullet}
on
K

extends
a power tower of finite length
V_{n}
on
K
if
W_{n}=V_{n}
.

The following lemma reduces the problem of extending
1
-foliation to an
n
-foliation to a problem of extending
1
-foliation to a
2
-foliation.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be the perfection of
K
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension.

Let
(K,W_{1},W_{2},W_{2},\ldots)
be a
2
-foliation on
K
, and let
(W_{1},W_{2},W_{3},W_{3},\ldots)
be a
2
-foliation on
W_{1}
. Then the tower
(K,W_{1},W_{2},W_{3},W_{3},\ldots)
is a
3
-foliation on
K
.

Proof.

If the tower
(K,W_{1},W_{2},W_{3},W_{3},\ldots)
is a power tower on
K
, then it is a
3
-foliation, because

[K:W_{1}]=[W_{1}:W_{2}]
and
[W_{1}:W_{2}]=[W_{2}:W_{3}]
by the definition of a
2
-foliation, Definition
4.41
. Therefore, we only have to prove this tower is a power tower.

By Lemma
4.11
, it is enough to prove that
W_{3}\cdot K^{p^{2}}=W_{2}
. We prove this equality below.
We have that
W_{1}=W_{2}\cdot K^{p}
, so
W_{1}^{p}=W_{2}^{p}\cdot K^{p^{2}}
. Consequently, we have

W_{2}=W_{3}\cdot W_{1}^{p}=W_{3}\cdot(W_{2}^{p}\cdot K^{p^{2}})=(W_{3}\cdot W_{2}^{p})\cdot K^{p^{2}}=W_{3}\cdot K^{p^{2}},

because
W_{3}\supset W_{2}^{p}
by Lemma
4.5
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be the perfection of
K
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension.

Every
1
-foliation on
K
can be extended to a
2
-foliation on
K
.

Proof.

The proof is a construction of a specific
p
-basis, and then using it to define a
2
-foliation extending the given
1
-foliation.

Let
\operatorname{tr.deg}(K/k)=N
.

Let
W_{1}
be a
1
-foliation on
K
of rank
r
.

There exist elements
t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{r}\in K
such that
W_{1}(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{r})=K
, i.e., a minimal set of generators. These elements are
p
-independent, Definition
2.15
, in
K
, because monomials
\prod_{i=1}^{r}t_{i}^{a_{i}}
, where
0\leq a_{i}<p
, are
W_{1}
-linearly independent. Therefore, they are
K^{p}
-linearly independent, because
W_{1}\supset K^{p}
.
Moreover, we can prove that their
p
-powers
t_{i}^{p}\in W_{1}
for
1\leq i\leq r
are
p
-independent in
W_{1}
. Indeed, by taking
p
-roots, it follows from that the monomials
\prod_{i=1}^{r}t_{i}^{p\cdot a_{i}}
, where
0\leq a_{i}<p
, are
W_{1}^{p}
-linearly independent. This means that differentials
d(t_{i}^{p})
are
W_{1}
-linearly independent in
\Omega_{W_{1}/k}
.

Let
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{N}
be a
p
-basis for
W_{1}/k
, Definition
2.15
. This means that differentials
da_{1},da_{2},\ldots,da_{N}
are a
W_{1}
-linear basis of
\Omega_{W_{1}/k}
.
Consequently, the notion of a
p
-basis admits an exchange lemma, i.e., any
p
-independent set can be completed to a
p
-independent set of maximal size by adding some elements from another maximal size independent set. Therefore, there are
N-r
elements among
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{N}
, say
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{N-r}
, such that
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{N-r},t_{1}^{p},t_{2}^{p},\ldots,t_{r}^{p}
is a
p
-basis for
W_{1}/k
.

We can prove that
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{N-r},t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{r}
forms a
p
-basis for the extension
K/k
.
In this situation, being a
p
-basis is equivalent to being a separable transcendental basis, Theorem
2.17
, so we will prove that this set is such a basis.

First, these elements are algebraically independent, because if not, then there would be a relation
F\in k[x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}]
between them, but then
F^{p}
would give such a relation for
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{N-r},t_{1}^{p},t_{2}^{p},\ldots,t_{r}^{p}
over
k^{p}=k
. This is a contradiction with the fact that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{N-r},t_{1}^{p},\ldots,t_{r}^{p}
is a
p
-basis for
W_{1}/k
.

Next, we show that

k(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N-r},t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})^{s}=K.

Indeed, we have a diagram of finite field extensions
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K=W_{1}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N-r},t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})^{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N-r},t_{1}^{p},\ldots,t_{r}^{p})^{s},}

wherein the separable closures are taken in
K
.
Now, we see that the extensions

k(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N-r},t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})^{s}/k(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N-r},t_{1}^{p},\ldots,t_{r}^{p})^{s}
and
K/W_{1}
are of order
p^{r}
.

So, the extension
K/k(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N-r},t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})^{s}
must be of order
1
. This proves the equality.

Consequently, we conclude that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{N-r},t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}
form a separating transcendental basis for
K/k
, so it is a
p
-basis for
K/k
.

Finally, we can define a
2
-foliation extending
W_{1}
. Indeed, we consider the following diagram of fields (in which
(a_{\bullet},t_{\bullet})
stands for
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N-r},t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})
, etc):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K=k(a_{\bullet},t_{\bullet})^{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{1}=k(a_{\bullet},t^{p}_{\bullet})^{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{2}\coloneqq k(a_{\bullet},t^{p^{2}}_{\bullet})^{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(a_{\bullet},t_{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(a_{\bullet},t^{p}_{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(a_{\bullet},t^{p^{2}}_{\bullet})}

Now, we claim that

K\supset W_{1}\supset W_{2}\supset W_{2}\supset W_{2}\supset\ldots

is a
2
-foliation on
K
.
Indeed, we have that
W_{1}\supset K^{p}
,
W_{2}\supset K^{p^{2}}
, and
[K:W_{1}]=[W_{1}:W_{2}]=p^{r}
. We want to show it is a power tower, i.e., that the equality
W_{2}\cdot K^{p}=W_{1}
holds.
It follows from the following calculation:

W_{1}\supset W_{2}\cdot K^{p}\supset k(a_{\bullet},t^{p^{2}}_{\bullet})^{s}(a_{\bullet},t^{p}_{\bullet})=k(a_{\bullet},t^{p^{2}}_{\bullet})^{s}(t^{p}_{\bullet})=k(a_{\bullet}^{p},t^{p}_{\bullet})^{s}=W_{1}

where we are allowed to move the separable closure and generators around, because all subfields involved are of finite exponent, this implies that separable closures of these subfields are these subfields.
This finishes the construction of
W_{2}
extending
W_{1}
.
∎

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
k=K^{p^{\infty}}
be the perfection of
K
. Let
K/k
be a finitely generated field extension. Let
n>0
be an integer.

Every
n
-foliation on
K
can be extended to a
\infty
-foliation on
K
.

Proof.

Let
W_{n}
be a
n
-foliation on
K
.
Then
W_{n}
is a
1
-foliation on
W_{n-1}=W_{n}\cdot K^{p^{n-1}}
.
Let
W_{n+1}
be an extension of
W_{n}
to a
2
-foliation on
W_{n-1}
. It exists by Proposition
6.4
.

Then
W_{n+1}
is a
1
-foliation on
W_{n}
.
Let
W_{n+2}
be an extension of
W_{n+1}
to a
2
-foliation on
W_{n}
.

Then
W_{n+2}
is a
1
-foliation on
W_{n+1}
.
Let
W_{n+3}
be an extension of
W_{n+2}
to a
2
-foliation on
W_{n+1}
.

And so on and so on, we obtain a tower of subfields of
K
:
(K,W_{1},W_{2},\ldots)
. This tower is a
\infty
-foliation on
K
extending
W_{n}
by Lemma
6.3
.
∎

Question 6.6
.

Is there an abstract
p
-Lie algebra cohomology? A purely inseparable cohomology? Assuming yes, and that we could use that cohomology to have a tangent obstruction theory for the problem of extending power towers, then we just proved that some elements in it are zero.

An
abstract
p
-Lie algebra
can be defined, see e.g.
[
Hoc54
]
.

Let
W_{1}
be a subfield of
K
of exponent one; it corresponds to a
p
-Lie algebra
\mathcal{F}_{1}=T_{K/W_{1}}
. Then, we have a short exact sequence of abstract
p
-Lie algebras on
W_{1}
:

0\to T_{W_{1}/K^{p}}\to T_{W_{1}/W_{1}^{p}}\xrightarrow{d(W_{1}/K^{p})}T_{K^{P}/W_{1}^{p}}\otimes W_{1}\to 0.

This sequence admits a right splitting of abstract
p
-Lie algebras (there is an
\mathcal{F}_{2}
) if and only if
W_{1}
can be extended to a
2
-foliation on
K
.
We can conclude that some
Ext groups of
p
-Lie algebras
, whatever they are, probably will control the extension problem for
n
-foliations for fields, for varieties, for whatever. However, it could be weirder than that. I do not know.

6.2.
Extensions via Liftings

We prove that a lifting of a
1
-foliation to a foliation induces an extension to an Ekedahl
\infty
-foliation for this
1
-foliation, and that this
\infty
-foliation is regular on a big open subset.

We denote (
p
-typical) Witt vectors over
k
by
W(k)
. For the basic theory of these rings, one can consult the following paper
[
Haz09
]
. (If one does not know Witt vectors, one can think they are just
p
-adic integers.) And, a flat lifting of a variety
X
over
k
to
W(k)
is an integral saturated scheme
\overline{X}
flat over
W(k)
whose pullback
\overline{X}\otimes_{\operatorname{Spec}(W(K))}\operatorname{Spec}(k)
, i.e., its reduction modulo
p
, is equal to
X
.

Definition 6.7
.

Let
X/k
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
p
-Lie algebra corresponding to a
1
-foliation
f_{1}:X\to Y_{1}
on
X
.

Then, a
lifting of the
1
-foliation

f_{1}
is a pair
(\overline{X},\overline{\mathcal{F}})
such that

•

\overline{X}
is a flat lifting of
X
to
W(k)
,

•

\overline{\mathcal{F}}\subset T_{\overline{X}/W(k)}
is a Lie subsheaf, i.e., a foliation on
\overline{X}/W(k)
, whose reduction modulo
p
is equal
\mathcal{F}\subset T_{X/k}
.

Definition 6.8
.

Let
X/k
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
p
-Lie algebra corresponding to a
1
-foliation
f_{1}:X\to Y_{1}
on
X
. Let
(\overline{X},\overline{\mathcal{F}})
be a lifting of
f_{1}
.

Then, we define a
saturation of a subalgebra of differential operators on
\overline{X}/W(k)
generated by
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
to be the following sheaf:

\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>(U)\coloneqq\left(\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>\otimes K(\overline{X})\right)\cap\operatorname{Diff}_{W(k)}(\overline{X})(U),

where
U\subset\overline{X}
is an open subset,
\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{W(k)}(\overline{X})
is the smallest subalgebra containing
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
, and
K(\overline{X})
is the field of rational functions on
\overline{X}
(It includes
\frac{1}{p}
.).

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
X/k
be a normal variety over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
satisfying
K(X)^{p^{\infty}}=k
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
p
-Lie algebra corresponding to a
1
-foliation
f_{1}:X\to Y_{1}
on
X
. Let
(\overline{X},\overline{\mathcal{F}})
be a lifting of
f_{1}
.

Then, the lifting of the
1
-foliation induces a unique extension of this
1
-foliation to an Ekedahl
\infty
-foliation that is compatible with the lifting.

Explicitly, the extension is given by the following saturated subalgebra:

\mathcal{D}(\overline{X},\overline{\mathcal{F}})\coloneqq\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>\otimes k\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{W(k)}(\overline{X})\otimes k=\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X).

Moreover, this
\infty
-foliation is regular on a big open subset.

Proof.

The proof is an explicit construction of a set of liftings of saturated distinguished generators
G
for the extension, Proposition
5.33
, on a big open subset of
X
, on which the given
1
-foliation is regular, and then using these liftings to show that

\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>\cong{S}(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}.

From this equality, the whole theorem follows. Indeed, this equality modulo
p
, i.e., after tensoring with
k
, gives

\operatorname{gr}\left(\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>\otimes k\right)\cong{S}({\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}.

And, since a reduction modulo
p
of a subalgebra is a subalgebra, thus by Proposition
5.52
we get that the subalgebra
\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>\otimes k
is gr-saturated (in particular, it is saturated) and regular. Hence, we will be done.

The main trick is an induction. We take already constructed
G^{i}_{j}
, we take its lifting
\overline{G^{i}_{j}}
, a lifting
C
of
{G^{i}_{j}}^{\circ p}
, and we put
\overline{G^{i+1}_{j}}=\frac{\overline{G^{i}_{j}}^{\circ p}-C}{p!}
up to a scalar. The technical part is to get control over the leading forms of these liftings.

Here are the details:

Without loss of generality, we can assume that the
1
-foliation
f_{1}
is regular, Definition
5.26
, because there exists a big open subset
U\subset X
such that
f_{1}\cap U
is regular, Proposition
5.28
, and being Ekedahl means being gr-saturated on a big open subset, Definition
5.47
, so it is enough to prove that a lifting of a regular
1
-foliation induces a gr-saturated regular
\infty
-foliation, which is characterized by Proposition
5.52
.

Let
f_{1}
be a regular
1
-foliation. This implies that
X
is regular and
\mathcal{F}\subset T_{X/k}
is a subbundle, Proposition
5.31
. Moreover, by a simple deformation theory, it implies that
\overline{X}
is regular over
W(k)
and that
\overline{\mathcal{F}}\subset T_{\overline{X}/W(k)}
is a subbundle as well.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
X
,
Y_{1}
, and
\overline{X}
are affine, because being gr-saturated and regular are local properties. We put
X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
,
Y_{1}=\operatorname{Spec}(B_{1})
, and
\overline{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\overline{A})
. (Because a flat lifting of an affine scheme is affine.) Moreover, we can assume that both subbundles
\mathcal{F}\subset T_{X/k}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}}\subset T_{\overline{X}/W(k)}
are actually free submodules.

Let
D_{1},\ldots,D_{r}
be a basis of
\mathcal{F}
.

We construct the following data:

•

For
j\geq 1
, operators
\overline{D_{i,(j)}}\in\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>
, where
i=1,2,\ldots,r
, such that:

–

For
j\geq 1
and
i=1,2,\ldots,r
, we have
\left[\overline{D_{i,(j)}}\right]=\gamma_{p^{j-1}}(\overline{D_{i,(1)}})
.

–

For
i=1,2,\ldots,r
, we have
\overline{D_{i,(1)}}\in\overline{\mathcal{F}}
and
\left(\overline{D_{i,(1)}}\ \operatorname{mod}\ p\right)=D_{i}
.

We begin the construction.

Let
j=1
. We put
\overline{D_{i,(1)}}\coloneqq\overline{D_{i}}
, where
\overline{D_{i}}\in\overline{\mathcal{F}}
is a lifting of
D_{i}
, i.e., an operator that modulo
p
is equal
D_{i}
.

Let
j=2
. Let
\overline{D_{i}^{p}}\in\overline{\mathcal{F}}
be a lifting of the
p
-power
D_{i}^{p}
. We have that
\overline{D_{i}}^{\circ p}
mod
p
is equal
D_{i}^{p}
, however its order is
p
, not
1
. Consequently, the difference
\overline{D_{i}}^{\circ p}-\overline{D_{i}^{p}}
is divisible by
p
. Therefore, the following operator is well defined:

\overline{D_{i,(2)}}\coloneqq\frac{\overline{D_{i}}^{\circ p}-\overline{D_{i}^{p}}}{p!}\in\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>.

By explicit computation in any coordinates, we can justify the following equality for the leading form:

[\overline{D_{i,(2)}}]=\gamma_{p}(\overline{D_{i}}).

Let
n\geq 2
. Let us assume that the construction is already done for
j\leq n
. We construct the operators for
j=n+1
.

Let
\mathbb{M}_{n}\subset\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>
be the
\overline{A}
-submodule spanned by the operators

\prod_{i=1,\ldots,r;\ j=1,\ldots,n}\overline{D_{i,(j)}}^{\circ a_{i,j}},

where
0\leq a_{i,j}\leq p-1
. (The order of compositions may matter, and we fix one order for each choice of indices.) These operators are a
\overline{A}
-basis of this module, because their leading forms are independent. Moreover, this module is gr-saturated, i.e.,
\operatorname{gr}\mathbb{M}_{n}\subset\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{W(k)}(\overline{X})
is saturated, because the leading forms
[\overline{D_{i,(j)}}]
are not divisible by any non-invertible element of
\overline{A}
.

The reduction modulo
p
of the module
\mathbb{M}_{n}
is a saturated subalgebra on
X
. We denote it by
\mathcal{D}_{n}\coloneqq\mathbb{M}_{n}\otimes k
. Indeed, we have that

\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>\subset{S}(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}

by the universal property of
{S}(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}
as in Proposition
2.79
. So, we have that the gradation of the subalgebra generated by all of
\overline{D_{i,(j)}}
for
j\leq n
is inside
{S}(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}
too, i.e.,
\operatorname{gr}\left<\overline{D_{i,(j)}}\right>_{i=1,\ldots,r;1\leq j\leq n}\subset{S}(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}
, and it is saturated.
Consequently, the reduction modulo
p
of
\left<\overline{D_{i,(j)}}\right>_{i=1,\ldots,r;1\leq j\leq n}
is a gr-saturated subalgebra whose gradation is inside
{S}({\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}
. Actually, because the reduction of the operator
\overline{D_{i,(j)}}
is of order
p^{j-1}
, we get that

\operatorname{gr}\left(\left<\overline{D_{i,(j)}}\right>_{i=1,\ldots,r;1\leq j\leq n}\otimes k\right)={S}({\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}\cap\operatorname{Diff}_{X^{(n)}}(X).

Therefore, by Proposition
5.52
, we get that
\left<\overline{D_{i,(j)}}\right>_{i=1,\ldots,r;1\leq j\leq n}\otimes k
corresponds to a gr-saturated regular
n
-foliation. The first order is
\mathcal{F}
, so it extends
\mathcal{F}
. Moreover, the set of the reductions
\{\overline{D_{i,(j)}}\otimes k\}
is a set of saturated distinguished generators of this subalgebra, Proposition
5.33
. So, the monomials
\prod_{i=1,\ldots,r;\ j=1,\ldots,n}(\overline{D_{i,(j)}}\otimes k)^{\circ a_{i,j}}
, where
0\leq a_{i,j}\leq p-1
, are a basis of this algebra, Proposition
5.33
. Finally, this shows that this is an algebra.

Let
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
. Let
E_{i}
be
\overline{D_{i,(n)}}^{\circ p}
modulo
p
. It is equal
\left(\overline{D_{i,(n)}}\ \operatorname{mod}\ p\right)^{\circ p}
, so it belongs to
\mathcal{D}_{n}
since it is an algebra. Let
\overline{E_{i}}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
be a lifting of
E_{i}
. (It is important that the lifting is taken from this module! Otherwise, we could enter a computational nightmare.)
Consequently, the following operator is well defined:

\overline{D_{i,(n+1)}}^{\prime}\coloneqq a\cdot\frac{\overline{D_{i,(n)}}^{\circ p}-\overline{E_{i}}}{p!}\in\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>,

where
a=\frac{p!\left(p^{n}!\right)^{p}}{p^{n+1}!}
is an invertible modulo
p
integer.
We put this extra
a
into the formula to in a moment get
\gamma_{p^{n}}(D_{i})
instead of
\gamma_{p}(\gamma_{p^{n-1}}(D_{i}))
. These two values are the same up to multiplication by this integer. Still, we have to modify this operator a bit to achieve this leading form, because this operator may have too high order.

If the leading form of
\overline{D_{i,(n+1)}}^{\prime}
is of order
>p^{n}
, then it is contributed solely by
\frac{\overline{E_{i}}}{p!}
, i.e.,
[\overline{D_{i,(n+1)}}^{\prime}]=a[\frac{\overline{E_{i}}}{p!}]=\frac{1}{p!}[\overline{E_{i}}]
. However, since
\operatorname{gr}\mathbb{M}_{n}
is saturated, it means that the form
\frac{1}{p!}[\overline{E_{i}}]
is well defined over
W(k)
if and only if the form
[\overline{E_{i}}]
is divisible by
p
.
In our case, it is well defined, so there exists an operator
L_{i}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
such that
p![L_{i}]=a[\overline{E_{i}}]
. We make a correction:

\overline{D_{i,(n+1)}}^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq\overline{D_{i,(n+1)}}^{\prime}-L_{i}.

The new operator has a lower order. If it is still
>p^{n}
, then we repeat the correction. So, we can assume that the order of
\overline{D_{i,(n+1)}}^{\prime\prime}
is
\leq p^{n}
. Actually, in this situation, it is equal
p^{n}
, because by the construction it has a nonzero coefficient next to an operator
\frac{1}{p^{n}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{n}}}{\partial x_{i}^{p^{n}}}
, where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}
is any set of coordinates for
\overline{A}/W(k)
. It is contributed from
\frac{\overline{D_{i,(n)}}^{\circ p}}{p!}
. (An explicit computation.)

Next, we observe that there exists a canonical decomposition, Lemma
2.67
:

{S}(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}\cap\operatorname{gr}^{p^{n}}\operatorname{Diff}_{W(k)}(\overline{A})=\left(\gamma_{p^{n}}(\overline{\mathcal{F}})\otimes\overline{A}\right)\oplus\text{REST},

where the submodule
REST
is given by

\text{REST}\coloneqq{S}(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}\cap\operatorname{gr}\mathbb{M}_{n}\cap\operatorname{gr}^{p^{n}}\operatorname{Diff}_{W(k)}(\overline{A})\subset\operatorname{gr}\mathbb{M}_{n},

so this submodule depends only on
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
, because
\operatorname{gr}\mathbb{M}_{n}
is determined by the leading forms that are compositions of divided powers of elements from
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
. With respect to this decomposition, a direct computation reveals that

\left[\overline{D_{i,(n+1)}}^{\prime\prime}\right]=\left(\gamma_{p^{n}}(\overline{{D_{i}}}),[G_{i}]\right),

where
G_{i}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}
.
Now, we can make the last correction

\overline{D_{i,(n+1)}}\coloneqq\overline{D_{i,(n+1)}}^{\prime\prime}-{G_{i}}.

This finishes the construction of the liftings of saturated distinguished generators.

Finally, we can prove the theorem. Indeed, by the universal property of symmetric algebras, we have a natural factorization (compare it with Proposition
2.79
):

\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>\subset{S}(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}\subset\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Diff}_{W(k)}(\overline{X}).

Also, the leading forms of operators
\overline{D_{i,(j)}}
generate
{S}(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}
, therefore we have
\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>={S}(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\coloneqq\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>\otimes k
. It is a subalgebra of
\operatorname{Diff}_{k}(X)
, because it is a reduction modulo
p
of a subalgebra. Moreover, taking a gradation commutes with reduction modulo
p
, so
we have

\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>\otimes k={S}(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}\otimes k={S}({\mathcal{F}}^{*})^{*gr}.

Therefore, by Proposition
5.52
, we get that
\mathcal{D}
corresponds to a gr-saturated regular
\infty
-foliation.

This
\infty
-foliation extends
\mathcal{F}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.10
.

In Theorem
6.9
, we only used that the algebras are flat over
W(k)
, so we could conclude divisibility by
p
in some calculations.

Consequently, a lifting of a
1
-foliation to a foliation modulo
p^{2}
(i.e., a saturated subsheaf of
T_{\overline{X}/W_{2}(k)}
closed under Lie brackets), i.e., a lifting to Witt vectors of length
2
denoted by
W_{2}(k)
, is enough to give a
\infty
-foliation that extends it. (Where the saturation must be defined differently using a substitute for the field of fractions.) Nevertheless, psychologically, it was easier to prove it for
W(k)
.

This observation is analogous to the famous Deligne–Illusie theorem that a lifting modulo
p^{2}
of a variety induces a degeneration of an important spectral sequence on it, see their paper
[
DI87
]
.

Remark 6.11
.

In Theorem
6.9
, we have to take the subalgebra
\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>
, because
\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>
is too small. Indeed, we can observe the essential behavior behind this choice on the simplest example: the vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
on
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}=\operatorname{Spec}(k[x])
, which spans the
p
-Lie algebra
\mathcal{F}\coloneqq T_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}/k}\subset T_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}/k}
. This
p
-Lie algebra admits a lifting to
\overline{\mathcal{F}}\coloneqq T_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{W(k)}/{W(k)}}\subset T_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{W(k)}/{W(k)}}
that is also spanned by
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
.

Now, the above algebras for this example are given by:

\displaystyle\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}^{\circ n}W(k)[x],

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial x^{n}}W(k)[x].

The main, and essentially only, difference is that we have

\frac{1}{p^{n}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{n}}}{\partial x^{p^{n}}}\in\operatorname{Sat}\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>
,
but
\frac{1}{p^{n}!}\frac{\partial^{p^{n}}}{\partial x^{p^{n}}}\not\in\left<\overline{\mathcal{F}}\right>
, for
n\geq 1
.

Consequently, in order to get the right leading forms, we must saturate our subalgebras. However, the reductions modulo
p
of both of these algebras are saturated anyway.

7.
Pullbacks of Canonical Divisors

7.1.
Formula for Pullbacks of Canonical Divisors

Theorem 7.1

(Canonical Divisor Formula)
.

Let
f:X\to Y\to X^{(n)}
be a purely inseparable morphism of exponent
n
between normal varieties over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
such that
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
be the Jacobson sequence corresponding to the power tower of
f
on
X
. Let
K_{X},K_{Y}
be canonical divisors of
X,Y
respectively.

Then, there exist divisors
D_{2},D_{3},\ldots,D_{n}
on
X
such that

f^{*}K_{Y}-K_{X}=(p^{n}-1)(-c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1}))+\sum_{i=2}^{n}(p^{n+1-i}-1)D_{i},

where
c_{1}
is the first Chern class.
Explicitly, these divisors are given by, for
i\geq 2
,

D_{i}\coloneqq f_{i-1}^{*}c_{1}\left(\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}}\right),

where
\mathcal{G}_{i}\coloneqq T_{Y_{i}/Y_{i-1}^{(1)}}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
of
f
on
X
is regular. Indeed, by Proposition
5.28
, there exists a big open subset
U\subset X
such that the power tower
Y_{\bullet}\cap U
is regular. And, any identity between divisors on a variety is true if it is true on a big open subset by the excision property
[
Har77
, Chapter II, Proposition 6.5 (b)]
.

Let the power tower
Y_{\bullet}
be regular. We recall that we put
Y_{0}=X
.

We prove the formula in
four steps
.

Step 1
, we prove that the formula holds for
1
-foliations. Indeed, let
f:X\to Y
be a
1
-foliation.
From Proposition
5.31
, we conclude that we have the following short exact sequences of vector bundles on
X
:

\displaystyle 0\to\mathcal{F}_{1}\to T_{X/k}

\displaystyle\to f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}_{1}\to 0,

\displaystyle 0\to f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}_{1}\to f_{1}^{*}T_{Y_{1}/k}

\displaystyle\to f_{1}^{*}\left(\frac{T_{Y_{1}/k}}{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\right)\to 0,

where
\mathcal{G}_{1}\coloneqq T_{Y_{1}/Y_{0}^{(1)}}=T_{Y_{1}/X^{(1)}}
.
So, from the linearity of the first Chern class, we get

\displaystyle c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})+c_{1}(f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}_{1})

\displaystyle=-K_{X},

\displaystyle c_{1}(f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}_{1})+c_{1}(f_{1}^{*}\left(\frac{T_{Y_{1}/k}}{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\right))

\displaystyle=-f_{1}^{*}K_{Y_{1}}.

And, from this, we get

f_{1}^{*}K_{Y_{1}}-K_{X}=c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})-c_{1}(f_{1}^{*}\left(\frac{T_{Y_{1}/k}}{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\right)).

Next, we can observe that

f_{1}^{*}\left(\frac{T_{Y_{1}/k}}{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\right)=F_{X/k}^{*}\left(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{(1)}\right),

where
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}^{(1)}
is the Jacobson sequence corresponding to
f^{(1)}:X^{(1)}\to Y^{(1)}
.
Indeed, this follows from the isomorphism

\frac{T_{Y_{1}/k}}{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\xrightarrow{d(Y_{1}/X^{(1)})}\mathcal{F}_{1}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y_{1}/k}=g_{1}^{*}\left(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{(1)}\right).

Thus, we have

c_{1}(f_{1}^{*}\left(\frac{T_{Y_{1}/k}}{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\right))=c_{1}(F_{X/k}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{(1)}))=pc_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1}),

because the line bundle
c_{1}(F_{X/k}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{(1)}))
is defined locally by
p
-powers of local generators of
c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})
. Finally, we conclude

f_{1}^{*}K_{Y_{1}}-K_{X}=c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})-c_{1}(f_{1}^{*}\left(\frac{T_{Y_{1}/k}}{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\right))=c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})-pc_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})=(p-1)(-c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})).

This finishes the proof of the formula for a
1
-foliation.

Step 2
, we prove that, for a power tower
Y_{\bullet}
of length
n
, we have
6
6

6

Actually, this formula holds for any sequence of
1
-foliations.

f_{n}^{*}K_{Y_{n}}-K_{X}=(1-p)\left(c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})+f_{1}^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{2})+\ldots+f_{n-1}^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{n})\right).

Indeed, we already know the formula from the theorem for
1
-foliations, so we can apply it to the
1
-foliations
X\to Y_{1},Y_{1}\to Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{n-1}\to Y_{n}
, and then we can pull back all these formulas to
X
:

\displaystyle(1-p)c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})

\displaystyle=f_{1}^{*}K_{Y_{1}}-K_{X},

\displaystyle(1-p)f_{1}^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{2})

\displaystyle=f_{2}^{*}K_{Y_{2}}-f_{1}^{*}K_{Y_{1}},

\displaystyle\ldots,

\displaystyle(1-p)f_{m-1}^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{m})

\displaystyle=f_{m}^{*}K_{Y_{m}}-f_{m-1}^{*}K_{Y_{n-1}}.

The statement follows from adding them all up.

Step 3
, we prove a relation:

{f^{\prime}}_{i-2}^{*}\left(c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{i})\right)=pc_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{i-1})-{f^{\prime}}_{i-2}^{*}c_{1}\left(\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}}\right),

where we used the functions
f^{\prime}_{i}
from Notation
5.11
. We recall
\mathcal{G}_{i}\coloneqq T_{Y_{i}/Y_{i-1}^{(1)}}
.

Indeed, it follows from the following diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{i-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\prime}_{i}}^{*}\mathcal{G}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{i-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i-1}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{i-1}^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

From which, we deduce an equality on
Y_{i-1}
:

c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{i})+c_{1}\left(\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}}\right)=c_{1}(g_{i-1}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{i-1})=g_{i-1}^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{i-1}),

where we used
g_{i}
from Notation
5.11
.
If we apply
{f^{\prime}}_{i-2}^{*}
to this equality, then we are done.

Last Step 4
, we can combine the previous steps to finish the proof. We recall that
D_{i}\coloneqq f_{i-1}^{*}c_{1}\left(\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}}\right)
.

By iterating the relation from step 3, we obtain

f_{i-1}^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{i})=p^{i-1}c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})-p^{i-2}D_{2}-\ldots-p^{2}D_{i-2}-pD_{i-1}-D_{i}.

Finally, we can combine the developments from the second and third steps:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-p}(f_{m}^{*}K_{Y_{m}}-K_{X})=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i-1}^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(p^{i-1}c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})-\sum_{j=2}^{i}p^{i-j}D_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}p^{i-1}c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=2}^{i}p^{i-j}D_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1-p^{m}}{1-p}c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=2}^{i}p^{i-j}D_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1-p^{m}}{1-p}c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})-\sum_{j=2}^{m}\frac{1-p^{m-j+1}}{1-p}D_{j}.

The proof is finished by multiplying the above equation by
1-p
.
∎

Some control over the divisors for
n
-foliations.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a purely inseparable morphism from Theorem
7.1
.

If
f:X\to Y
is a
n
-foliation on
X
, then
D_{i}\geq 0
, for
i\geq 2
.

If
f:X\to Y
is an Ekedahl
n
-foliation on
X
, then
D_{i}=0
, for
i\geq 2
.

Proof.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
n
-foliation. Then, for
i=2,\ldots,n
, the sheaf
\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}}
is torsion, Lemma
5.50
. Therefore, from functorial properties of Chern classes of coherent sheaves: a definition by using resolutions by vector bundles,
[
Sta18
, Section 0ESY]
and a proper pushforward
[
Sta18
, Section 02R3]
applied to the closed immersion
\operatorname{Supp}(\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}})\to Y_{i-1}
and a gradation of
\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}}
that is coherent on
\operatorname{Supp}(\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}})
, we deduce that
c_{1}(\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}})
is a sum of divisors
\sum a_{i}E_{i}
, where
E_{1},\ldots,E_{k}
are irreducible components of the support of
\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}}
that are of codimension
1
in
X
, and
a_{i}\geq 0
are integers. Consequently, the pullback satisfies
f_{i-1}^{*}c_{1}(\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}})=\sum a_{i}f_{i-1}^{*}(E_{i})
, where
f_{i-1}^{*}(E_{i})
are effective divisors on
X
that satisfy
|f_{i-1}^{*}(E_{i})|=|E_{i}|
, because
f
is a finite morphism and a homeomorphism. This proves that

D_{i}\coloneqq f_{i-1}^{*}c_{1}(\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}})\geq 0.

Let
f:X\to Y
be an Ekedahl
n
-foliation. Then, for
i=2,\ldots,n
, the supports of the sheaves
\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}}
are of codimension at least
2
by Proposition
5.51
, therefore
c_{1}(\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}})=0
, and so
D_{i}=f_{i-1}^{*}c_{1}(\frac{T_{Y_{i-1}/k}}{\mathcal{F}_{i}+\mathcal{G}_{i-1}})=f_{i-1}^{*}0=0
.
∎

Question 7.3
.

I believe that if I have a fibration
f:X\to Y
that can be recovered from its power tower, then the formulas for
K_{X}-f_{i}^{*}K_{Y_{i}}
converge (possibly in the
p
-adic sense) to a formula for
K_{X}-f^{*}K_{Y}
. Is it true? Is it a special case of a more general fact that there exists a “canonical divisor of a power tower”
K_{Y_{\bullet}}
and this divisor satisfies that formula?

I do not have any proof for it; however, there is evidence for this claim in Marta Benozzo’s research. She studied formulas for
K_{X}-f^{*}K_{Y}
in her PhD thesis, which is partially covered in her preprints
[
Ben23
]
,
[
BBC24
]
, and her paper
[
Ben25
]
, and she communicated to me that it seems that supports of her “extra divisors” in those formulas coincide with the supports of extra divisors appearing in “the limit” of my formulas, at least in very well-behaved cases. More research is needed.

7.2.
Application: Not-Lüroth Theorems

Lüroth-type theorems say that if a variety
Y/k
of dimension
n
is dominated by
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k}
, then, under some extra conditions,
Y/k
is birational to
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k}
. One such condition that works is
n=1
and
Y
being smooth. This is
the
Lüroth theorem, see e.g.
[
Har77
, Chapter IV, Example 2.5.5.]
.

In this section, we prove that if a morphism
f:\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k}\to Y
is purely inseparable, then in most cases
Y
is
not
like
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k}
, and we can be more precise about what it means. This result is an application of the formula from Theorem
7.1
.

The following definition is
[
Laz04
, Definition 2.1.3.]
; it is applied to a variety over an arbitrary field, not necessarily
\mathbb{C}
.

Definition 7.4
.

Let
D
be a divisor on a normal variety
X/k
. The
Iitaka dimension
of
D
is defined to be an integer
d

such that there are positive real numbers
a,b>0
satisfying

am^{d}\leq h_{0}(X,mD)\leq bm^{d}

for
m>>0
. In other words,
h_{0}(X,mD)\sim m^{d}
. We denote it by
\kappa(X,D)=d
.

Example 7.5
.

Let
X/k=\mathbb{P}_{k}^{n}
. Let
D
be a divisor on
X
. Then there are only three options for
\kappa(X,D)
:

•

If
d<0
, then we have
h_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{2}_{k},md)=0
, for
m>0
. So,
\kappa(X,D)=-\infty
.

•

If
d=0
, then we have
h_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{2}_{k},md)=1
, for
m>0
. So,
\kappa(X,D)=0
.

•

If
d>0
, then we have
h_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{2}_{k},md)\sim m^{n}
, for
m>>0
. So,
\kappa(X,D)=n
.

The computation follows from
[
Har77
, Chapter III, Theorem 5.1]
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a purely inseparable morphism between normal varieties over a perfect field
k
such that
k=K(X)^{p^{\infty}}
. Let
D
be a divisor on
Y
. Then, we have

\kappa(Y,D)=\kappa(X,f^{*}D).

Proof.

Let
n
be the exponent of
f
. It is an integer, not
\infty
, by Lemma
2.10
. Therefore, there is a factorization of
F_{Y^{(-n)}/k}
:

Y^{(-n)}\to X\xrightarrow{f}Y.

(We can take the negative exponent
-n
in the following way: we take
K(Y)
, and we take the normalization of
\mathcal{O}_{X/k}
in
K(Y)^{p^{-n}}
.)
From this factorization, we obtain the following sequence.

H^{0}(Y,mD)\to H^{0}(X,mf^{*}D)\to H^{0}(Y^{(-n)},m\left(F_{Y^{(-n)/k}}^{n}\right)^{*}D)\simeq H^{0}(Y,mp^{n}D).

All the above arrows are injections, because the composition takes a section to its
p^{n}
-power, i.e.,
a\mapsto a^{p^{n}}
, and it is an injection. (For the second arrow, we apply the same argument for
X^{(-n)}\to Y^{(-n)}\to X
.)

Finally, if
\kappa(Y,D)=d
, then there are positive real numbers
a,b\geq 0
such that

am^{d}\leq h_{0}(Y,mD)\leq h_{0}(X,mf^{*}D)\leq h_{0}(Y,mp^{n}D)\leq(bp^{nd})m^{d},

where
m\geq 0
is an integer. Therefore, we have
\kappa(X,f^{*}D)=d
. (The opposite implication follows from applying the same argument to
Y^{(-n)}\to X
and
f^{*}D
on
X
.) This proves the lemma.
∎

Theorem 7.7

(A Not-Lüroth Theorem)
.

Let
n>1
be an integer.
Let
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k}
be the
n
-dimensional projective space over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
p-1
not divide
n+1
.

If
f:\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k}\to Y
is a purely inseparable morphism between normal varieties over
k
, then the Iitaka dimension of a canonical divisor of
Y
is equal
-\infty
or
n
, i.e., we have

\kappa(Y,K_{Y})=-\infty,\text{or }n.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.6
, we know that

\kappa(Y,K_{Y})=\kappa(\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k},f^{*}K_{Y}).

By Example
7.5
, we know that
\kappa(\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k},f^{*}K_{Y})=-\infty
, or
n
if and only if
f^{*}K_{Y}\neq 0
. So, we have to prove that
f^{*}K_{Y}\neq 0
. Let us assume that it is false, i.e.,
f^{*}K_{Y}=0
. Let
m
be the exponent of
f
. Then, the formula from Theorem
7.1
applied to
f
gives:

\displaystyle f^{*}K_{Y}-K_{\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k}}

\displaystyle=(1-p^{m})c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})-\sum_{j=2}^{m}(1-p^{m-j+1})D_{j},

\displaystyle n+1

\displaystyle=(1-p^{m})c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})-\sum_{j=2}^{m}(1-p^{m-j+1})D_{j},

\displaystyle n+1

\displaystyle=(1-p)\left(\frac{1-p^{m}}{1-p}c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})-\sum_{j=2}^{m}\frac{1-p^{m-j+1}}{1-p}D_{j}\right).

This is a contradiction, because
p-1
does not divide
n+1
. This proves the theorem.
∎

Question 7.8
.

The conclusion of Theorem
7.7
is most likely false if
p-1
divides
n+1
. Indeed, I believe that counterexamples exist. In particular, the “normalisations of K3-like coverings” from
[
FS20
, Section 4]
look like a promising direction for finding these counterexamples for
n=2
.

Are these morphisms counterexamples? Are all counterexamples like them?

Remark 7.9
.

The condition “
p-1
does not divide
n+1
” is true if
p>n+2
, so if the characteristic is great enough with respect to the dimension of the projective space, then Theorem
7.7
holds.

Moreover, for
n=2
, the assumption on
p
in Theorem
7.7
can be replaced with
p>2
, because a prime number satisfies
p>2
if and only if
p-1
does not divide
3
.
